# Architecture Debt Register

Temporary architecture must not silently become permanent (§62).

Every entry records what was compromised, why it was acceptable at the time, and
**the observable condition that makes it unacceptable**. An entry without a
trigger is not debt — it is an undocumented permanent decision wearing a
disguise.

---

| ID | Debt | Taken on | Why it was acceptable | Trigger to repay | Cost if unpaid | Status |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| D-69 | ~~**A service's sharing scope was reachable only through one of its layers, and not at all without one.**~~ `PUT /admin/services/{name}/sharing` took only `PostgresSystemServices`, so a path spelled `/admin/services/…` answered **404 for every ordinary service on the server**. The working route was `PUT /admin/layers/{name}/sharing`, which writes the *owning service's* column — correct since migration 11, and reachable only through a layer. So a three-layer service had three routes to one setting, and an **empty** service had none. Server creates empty services and creates them `private`, so Server could create a thing whose scope nothing on the server could change. | 2026-08-15, when the layer endpoint was corrected to write the service's column and no service-scoped endpoint was added beside it | Not acceptable, unnoticed — and unnoticed in a specific way worth recording: the layer route *worked*, so every test and every screen that changed a scope passed. Nothing exercised the case with no layer. | Reported by the owner: *"server tarafında sharing mekanizması çok işlemiyor."* Diagnosed by trying it rather than by reading — both candidate routes answered 404 for an empty service | **The one thing [D-61](#)'s repair did not reach.** D-61 moved Capabilities and Limits off the layer pages because their columns are on `service`; `service.sharing` is also on `service` and Sharing stayed a layer page. So the debt is not only a missing endpoint but the same defect D-61 names, in the setting nobody re-checked. | **RESOLVED 2026-08-18.** The endpoint accepts an ordinary service, folder-qualified, and reports the scope it replaced; Sharing moved from `LAYER_PAGES` to `SERVICE_PAGES`; the layer keeps `maintenance` for unpublishing, which ADR-034 §5c had put on the same page. Measured end to end: an empty service cycled through all three scopes, and anonymous reach on a real service went 404 / 404 / 200 across private / organization / public. `EveryScreenTests` now asserts the layer has **no** Sharing page — and caught the move being half-done when it did. |
| D-81 | ~~**A page's whole write surface was invisible to the only people who could use it.**~~ `GET /admin/groups/{name}` did not return `mayManage` or `mayDelete` — the two lines that compute them live in `ListGroupsAsync` and were never copied into `DescribeGroupAsync` — so the group page read `undefined` seven times. An owner who was also an unrestricted administrator got a Settings tab saying *"these are the owner's and its managers' to set"* and no controls: no policies, no lock, no delete, no row verbs on either table, and no *Add member*. **A plain member's view was accidentally correct**, because `!undefined && standing === "owner"` is false for them — so the defect hit exactly the people the page was built for and spared everybody else. | 2026-08-18, with the four-tab page | Not acceptable, and it is the **third** instance of one shape: a control that is present and cannot be seen. [D-78](#) was a form rendered into a hidden container; the groups screen's first-run bug before it was the same; this is the flags that decide whether a control renders at all. | Found by a design review that could not review the page until it worked around the bug | **Every test passed.** Seven console tests asserted the page's shape, its four tabs, its addresses and its copy, and not one of them asked whether a control the owner needs was on screen. `getElementById` cannot tell existence from availability. | **RESOLVED 2026-08-18.** Two lines in the describe payload, and the guard is the one the earlier instances already taught: `An_owner_can_see_and_not_merely_have_the_groups_controls` asserts `offsetParent !== null` on six controls across two tabs, and asserts that every control on the Settings tab has an accessible name. Falsified by removing the two lines: two tests went red with the right sentences. **And three of this file's own waits turned out to be tautologies** — `!document.getElementById('view-group').hidden` is permanently true because `showView` toggles a class, so those waits passed before the router had run; one of them was letting a click through during boot. Fixed to read `.classList.contains('on')`, which can be false. |
| D-82 | ~~**A sort's comment said the opposite of what the sort did, and a screen was built on the comment.**~~ `MembersAsync` ordered by `order by 2 desc` over the strings `owner \| manager \| member`, with a comment claiming *"owner, then managers, then members"*. `'member' > 'manager'` on the third letter, so the real order was owner, then **members**, then managers — the managers at the bottom of the last page. The Members tab drew its manager/member boundary from that sort, so the divider landed on the first row of page two and nowhere near the boundary, and `Make manager` moved a row off the page you were looking at. | 2026-08-18, with the widened member read | Not acceptable. **A wrong comment is worse than no comment:** the screen's divider logic was written from it rather than from the SQL, so one mistake became two features that did not work. | Found by a design review, and only after it had repaired the page's dead pagers — with ten of fourteen rows reachable, the managers were never on screen to be mis-sorted | **The instrument again.** This is [D-30](#)'s family on a smaller scale: the thing that told me what the data looked like was wrong, so everything built on it was wrong in a way that read as correct. | **RESOLVED 2026-08-18.** An explicit `case` rank — owner 0, manager 1, member 2 — so the order does not depend on how three English words happen to collate. The divider became a labelled band rather than a hairline: `tr.after` drew a second 1px rule against the one every `td` already carries, which was invisible, and a band says what it separates and survives a page break. |
| D-83 | ~~**`SCOPES` had three values and the server took four, so the console's own instruction was unfollowable.**~~ ADR-036 added `group` as a sharing scope; `console.js` kept `["private", "organization", "public"]`. A service shared with a group reaches its members **only** when its own scope is `group`, and that scope could not be set anywhere in this console — so every share made from the group screen was inert, the screen said so, and the remedy it named (*"set on the service's Sharing page"*) did not exist. | 2026-08-18, with ADR-036's fourth scope | Not acceptable, and it is [D-74](#) once more — a value added to an enumeration leaves its readers silently wrong, and this reader is a screen. D-74's guard covers C# files that enumerate scopes; it did not look at `wwwroot`. | Found by a design review, which had to set the scope over the API to see a reaching share at all | **The feature was complete on the server and unusable from the product.** Both halves of the two-act share were implemented and only one of them could be performed. | **RESOLVED 2026-08-18.** `SCOPES` has four. And the remedy moved to where the fault is named: Overview lists the services that reach nobody and offers **Set scope to group** on each, rather than sending the operator to another screen to do it one at a time. That turned Overview from three restatements into the one tab that says what is wrong with this group and fixes it. |
| D-84 | ~~**Studio's content screen never paginated, at a stated scale of 100–1,000 services.**~~ `loadMyContent` called neither `pageOf` nor `pagerFor` while every other list in the console did. On the owner's server — 512 items — that is 512 rows and something like thirty thousand pixels of table. | 2026-08-16, when the screen was written | Not acceptable, and it is the same defect as the 512-option `<select>` the owner rejected on 2026-08-18, on the very screen they were asking about. **Both are the same mistake made twice: designing a list against the eleven items on the development server.** | Found in a design review that measured the row count rather than looking at it | It was invisible locally forever: eleven rows fit. §60 says do not make small deployments painful to serve hypothetical huge ones — it does not say build only for the small one. | **RESOLVED 2026-08-18.** Ten a page, and two tables became one with a scope strip. `Content_is_one_paged_table_with_a_picture_on_every_row` fails if more than a page renders at once. |
| D-85 | ~~**The preview cache stored the resolved shape after its own await, so overlapping passes both missed it.**~~ `previews.get(key) ?? await readShape(...)` then `previews.set(key, shape)`: two `paintPreviews` passes over one list both found nothing and both fetched. Measured on the Services screen at folder `hosted`: **34 requests for 20 distinct layers, 14 of them exact duplicates — 7 services fetched twice each, 612 KB, 510 ms.** | 2026-08-17, with the previews | Not acceptable once a second surface wanted the same pictures: the group's Content tab and the add page draw the same services the listing behind them already drew, so the waste multiplies per surface rather than adding. | Found by a design review that counted the requests | **`paintPreviews`' own comment names the cost it was trying to avoid** — *"forty rows is forty queries, and firing them together is a load test of our own server dressed up as a screen"* — while the cache underneath it was leaking half of them. The mechanism was right and its backing store was wrong. | **RESOLVED 2026-08-18.** The cache holds the in-flight promise, so the second caller awaits the first one's answer, and a rejection is deleted rather than kept — otherwise one 503 from a starting service would make a layer undrawable for the rest of the session. |
| D-86 | ~~**Other people's private content was listed under a heading that said *shared with you*, and reading it left no record.**~~ `/content/layers` bucketed on ownership alone, so everything not owned went into `shared` — including the private services an administrator sees through `admin:viewAllContent`. Nobody shared those. And [ADR-018](adr/ADR-018-authorization-and-roles.md) condition 3 says *"an administrator reading a private layer is legitimate and must leave a record, or the sharing model is decorative"* — **the listings wrote no audit row at all**, so an administrator could enumerate every private service on the server and leave nothing behind. | 2026-08-15 for the bucket, and the audit gap since listings existed | Not acceptable. The first half misreports the sharing model to the one person who can change it; the second means a discharged condition was being kept by per-layer reads and not by the screen that shows them all at once. | Found by a design review asking whether a sentence a screen was about to show — *every one of these reads is recorded against your name* — was true. **It was not.** | **It was invisible locally because one account owns every service on the development server**, which is exactly the accident that ships a defect: the wrong bucket had nothing in it to be wrong about. | **RESOLVED 2026-08-18.** Its own scope, named *Not shared with you* for what it means to the reader rather than after the enum, shown only when it holds something; a third list on the per-layer endpoint; and one audit row per listing that includes it, naming the count and the services — one per listing rather than one per item, because a listing is one act and four hundred rows for one screen is how an audit trail actually dies. Measured: the row appears, and a second call writes a second row. |
| D-87 | ~~**The console denied a feature this server had shipped, measured and tested.**~~ The import form's own copy read *"GeoJSON only — a shapefile is a ZIP and this server does not open archives (Q-98)"*, and its file input carried `accept=".json,.geojson,application/geo+json"` — so a shapefile **could not be selected at all**. Both statements were true when written and both were made false by [ADR-024](adr/ADR-024-shapefile-import.md), which answered Q-98, opened the archive under stated bounds, and shipped an 860-line reader verified against a corpus this project did not write. **There was also no field for `srid`**, which the server requires for a shapefile and refuses to infer from the `.prj` — so even a hand-edited `accept` would have hit a refusal with nowhere to answer it. | 2026-08-15, when ADR-024 shipped the reader without revisiting the form | Not acceptable, and it is worse than a missing feature: the copy **cites the question that answered it** as the reason not to try. A reader who believes it converts their data elsewhere first, which is precisely the friction ADR-024 §1 says this product exists to remove. | Found while placing the owner's *New item → Feature layer → Upload a file* screenshots against what the console actually offers | **[D-83](#)'s shape exactly, one screen over.** There the server took a fourth sharing scope and `SCOPES` had three; here the server takes an archive and `accept` does not. In both, the capability was complete and the product did not have it. **Two conformance tests and eight console tests exercised the import endpoint throughout** — none of them opened the form. | **RESOLVED 2026-08-18.** The form takes `.zip` and GeoJSON, asks for the coordinate system, and says what it needs rather than what it refuses. Four tests: the `accept` list, the copy's *absence* of the denial (asserted as an absence, because the claim rather than any phrasing is what must not return), and both directions of `srid` — sent when given, absent when not, since an empty string would make the server decide what an empty coordinate system means. **And the write trap now records a `FormData` body's field names**, which is what made the last of those assertable: knowing a form posted somewhere is weaker than knowing what it posted, and a control that sends the right request without the field the server requires looks identical from outside. Falsified by restoring both halves of the defect: two tests went red. |
| D-88 | **The reversal of [ADR-037](adr/ADR-037-job-workers-come-in-two-kinds.md) spends three things that were being kept, and this row is where they are counted rather than absorbed.** GDAL now ships in the server image, loaded by a child process. **First: [A-016](architecture-assumptions.md) and [ADR-009](adr/ADR-009-raster-engine.md) §2.2 are broken as written** — *"the serving container ships no GDAL. It exists only in the job worker image."* The rule becomes about the **process** rather than the **image**, which is weaker in exactly the way §2.2 says: it was strengthened *"from a placement decision to an artefact rule"* because an image boundary is checkable at build time where a process boundary needs discipline. **Second: [Q-28](open-questions.md)'s stricter form is spent** — *no GDAL or OSGeo package reference anywhere in the solution*, recorded as available to us and mechanically checkable. There is a package reference now. **Third: [D-06](#) comes due**, which `Directory.Packages.props` predicted in its own header: repayment is owed *"before the first binary that bundles a database driver, a GDAL build or a Python wheel set."* This is that binary. | 2026-08-18, with the reversal | **Acceptable, and chosen with the alternative measured rather than assumed.** The Python worker existed, worked on the owner's real archives, and was five times slower on the same layer; keeping it meant a second language runtime, a second image and a second dependency graph to buy a rule about images. The owner's question was the right one: *"eğer ki illa gdal kullanacaksam .net de olur."* | Before the first deployment. The process rule needs the same mechanical check the image rule had, and the driver set needs enumerating | **What is genuinely lost is checkability, not isolation.** The serving *process* still never loads GDAL — `Graticula.Import.Reader` is referenced by nothing and spawned as a child, the same shape `Graticula.Overlay.Worker` uses for NetTopologySuite. But *"no package reference in the solution"* was a test and *"GDAL is only in that project"* is a convention, and this repository has recorded four times what happens to conventions nobody checks. | **CLOSED 2026-08-24 — all three parts, and the last one needed a measurement rather than a paragraph.** Originally three parts. (1) A build-time check that `MaxRev.Gdal.*` is referenced by `Graticula.Import.Reader` and by nothing else — the architecture suite already does exactly this shape for tier boundaries, so it is a test rather than a project. (2) The driver set this build carries, enumerated with licences: GDAL's own `LICENSE.TXT` is a bill of materials — MIT-style core beside BSD, public-domain, **Apache-2.0 Esri components**, ISC, Info-ZIP and Qhull — so *"GDAL is MIT"* must not be written as a finding. (3) A-016 and ADR-009 §2.2 amended to say *process* where they say *image*, or to say plainly that the artefact rule was traded and for what.  **Reckoned 2026-08-19, and it was three things rather than one.** *First* is repaid: `NativeDependencyTests` is the mechanical check the artefact rule used to provide — one project per confined dependency, both directions, and no path from the host through a project reference. *Third* is repaid: A-016 and ADR-009 §2.2 now say **process** where they said image, with what the amendment cost written beside it rather than absorbed, and ADR-037 condition 4 is withdrawn with its replacement named and met. *Second* is still owed — the GDAL driver set is not enumerated and its licences are not in DEPENDENCY-LICENSES.md. It is now tracked where it belongs, as ADR-037 condition 2, restated for a package payload instead of an image. So this row is two-thirds repaid and stays open on the third.  **Second is repaid 2026-08-24, and it is the part that could only be answered by asking.** GDAL's `LICENSE.TXT` names **fourteen** components and says of at least one that it applies *"when MRF driver included in build"* — so which licences bind depends on the driver set, and only the build knows the driver set. `Graticula.Import.Reader` gained a `drivers` operation for that reason: the native payload arrives with `MaxRev.Gdal.Core` and changes when the package does, so a list typed into a document would be right until the next upgrade. **Measured: GDAL 3.13.1, 83 vector drivers, 213 raster drivers** — and MRF, PDF, GRIB, HDF4, PCIDSK, DXF and FlatGeobuf are all present, so every conditional section of that file applies, **including Esri's Apache-2.0 one**. The table is in [DEPENDENCY-LICENSES.md](../DEPENDENCY-LICENSES.md). **The conclusion is the same as the short sentence would have given and that is the point**: permissive throughout, no copyleft, compatible with Apache-2.0 outbound. What changes is that *"GDAL is MIT"* is no longer the finding — it was never true of the artefact — and the Esri attribution is visible rather than buried under a summary. |
| D-79 | **A write that replaces every field is guarded by one console helper and nothing else.** `PUT /admin/groups/{name}/settings` replaces title, summary, description and four policies together, so any caller that assembles the body from the controls in front of it erases whatever is not in front of it. The console has exactly one function that builds it, overlaying a patch on the group it last read. ~~**Nothing in the code prevents a second caller from doing it wrong**~~ — **something does, from 2026-08-24: every member of `GroupSettingsRequest` is `required`.** The failure was silent, plausible and destroyed text an operator typed. | 2026-08-18, with the settings write | Acceptable for now, and the alternative was considered and rejected: a `coalesce` patch where null means *leave* cannot express *clear this description*, so replace is the right shape for the store. What is owed is a guard, not a redesign. | Before a second caller exists — a script, a second screen, or an API consumer | **This already happened once in documentation.** The port described the three text parameters as *"or null to leave it"* while the statement writes `set title = @title`; the comment was wrong for an hour and a design review caught it before either screen was written. A comment is not a guard, and the next reader of that endpoint has only a comment. | **CLOSED 2026-08-24, and it is the fix this row named.** Its own last sentence was *the shape of the fix is a required-field contract on the request record*, and that is what `GroupSettingsRequest` carries now: seven `required` members, so a body that leaves one out is refused **before the object exists** rather than binding a null over text somebody typed. **`required` rather than a not-null check, because the distinction is absence.** Clearing a summary is a legitimate act, so empty is a value and only *missing* is the error — and a check on the deserialised object could not tell those apart at all. **The console needed no change**, which is the measurement that says the contract is the right shape: `saveGroupSettings` has always built the whole object and overlaid a patch on it, so the one caller that existed was already correct and the type now says why. The measured behaviour stays in [ADR-036](adr/ADR-036-groups.md) §4h and `Writing_settings_replaces_the_text_fields_too` still pins the store's half. **`GroupSettingsContractTests` is the half that was missing**: the whole set binds — with an empty summary and a null description, because those are values — and **seven `[InlineData]` cases remove one member each**. One test that removed only `title` would pass while six others were optional, and the defect this row records is a caller sending *the controls in front of it*, which is a different subset every time. Falsified by dropping `required` from `Summary`: one of the seven went red and named it. |
| D-80 | **A group's items cannot be drawn, so the tab that should show them shows their names.** `drawPreview` renders a service from one query into a canvas and the Services screen uses it; `ItemsAsync` joins `service` already but does not select the cover columns, so a group's Content and Overview have no picture to show. | 2026-08-18, with the four-tab page | Acceptable, and the honest version was chosen deliberately: `.thumb.empty` hatching for every row means *this service has nothing to draw* on the Services screen, and here it would mean *this screen did not ask* — the same mark for two different facts. | With maps, and the owner has said they are coming: *"yakında sistemde harita ekleme, icon ekleme gibi özellikler de olacak"* | **It is the reason the four tabs exist.** [ADR-036](adr/ADR-036-groups.md) §4g adopted them because Content stops being two rows of a table the moment it holds items with pictures; until it has pictures, Content is the least changed of the four and the tabs are carrying a promise rather than a payload. **REPAID 2026-08-24, and half of it had been repaid without this row noticing.** The subselects, the `cover` on the wire and the canvas per row of Content all existed; what did not exist was anything asserting them, and the group fixture every console test uses is empty — so every assertion about Content had been about its empty state. **That is this repository's most repeated defect, a control that exists in the markup and is never seen, and it is why the row could not be closed by reading the code.** `GroupContentPictureTests` shares a real service into a real group and asks the browser: a `canvas` per row, zero hatched cells, drawn at the size it is displayed at. Verified by dropping `cover` from the group items wiring, which turns the row hatched and fails on the ambiguity this row named. **The Overview half is withdrawn rather than done**: Overview was rebuilt as a repair desk after this row was written — it lists the services whose own scope reaches nobody, each with the verb that fixes it — and pictures there would be decoration on the one tab that is a work list. | ~~open~~ repaid 2026-08-24 |
| D-73 | ~~**The roles screen could not select a role, so nothing could be given a privilege.**~~ The click handler read `t.dataset.role` where `t` is the cell that was clicked and the row carries the name, so choosing a role did nothing and the editor stayed on whichever role rendered first. Every other row handler in `console.js` already used `closest`; this one was written without looking at them. | 2026-08-18, with the screen | Not acceptable. **The interesting part is that three tests passed while the screen was unusable:** they asserted its shape — two sections, per-category counts, disabled boxes for the administrator, set-from-existing copying ticks — and none of them performed the one act the screen exists for. A suite can be green about every detail of a control nobody can operate. | Reported by the owner the moment they opened it: *"varolan bir yetkiye yeni yetki veremiyorum. viewer seçimi değişmiyor."* | **The whole of ADR-035's operator surface.** The API, the store and the resolution were all measured and correct; the screen in front of them did nothing, which is the state D-61 describes for a different screen. | **RESOLVED 2026-08-18.** `closest` for the row, the delete button and enable-all. Two tests added that click a **cell** — clicking the row element would have passed against the broken build — tick a privilege, and assert which role the write is addressed to. Falsified by restoring the defect: both went red. **And the screen now completes a dependency in front of the operator:** ticking `content:publishFeatures` ticks `content:create` with a toast saying so, and unticking a prerequisite unticks what needed it. The API still refuses rather than auto-adding — a server widening a grant silently is different from a form helping somebody who is present. |
| D-70 | ~~**`admin:manageRoles` was a privilege with nothing behind it.**~~ It existed, was granted to the administrator, and no endpoint required it — [D-56](#)'s complaint about `admin:manageMembers`, in the privilege ADR-035 turned out to be about. And the `role` table carried a name and a description only, so a role row could be inserted and would grant nothing with no way to give it anything. | 2026-08-14, with Q-59's *"fixed, not custom"* decision | It followed from that decision: with role grants compiled in, there was nothing for the privilege to guard. What was not acceptable was Q-59's own note that *"the role table already carries arbitrary names, so custom roles are a feature rather than a migration"* — half true, and the wrong half load-bearing. | The owner reversing Q-59 on 2026-08-18 | **A privilege granted and unenforced is a claim about the product.** An operator reading the role list saw `admin:manageRoles` and would reasonably conclude roles could be managed. | **RESOLVED 2026-08-18** by [ADR-035](adr/ADR-035-role-privileges-are-editable.md): migration 25, four endpoints, a Server screen, and 22 tests. Measured — a member's capability followed an edit to their role in the same session, 403 → 201 → 403. |
| D-78 | ~~**New group did nothing, and the only reader who could see that was a first-time one.**~~ The create form was written into `#groupPicker`, which sits inside `#groupEditor` — a panel that is `hidden` until a group is chosen. So the button rendered its form into a hidden container. **Every element existed and none could be seen**, which is why nothing in the console reported an error. | 2026-08-18, in the same change that replaced two `prompt()`s with a form | Not acceptable, and the interesting part is why three tests written the same hour missed it: **each of them created a group before opening the screen**, so the editor was always open and the picker always visible. The suite could only ever be in the state where the defect is masked. | Reported by the owner on pressing it: *"new group düğmesi çalışmıyor"* | **The one state that matters most is the one a fresh deployment is in.** A console whose primary action silently fails on an empty server fails for every new operator and works for everybody who tests it. | **RESOLVED 2026-08-18.** The create form has its own slot between the toolbar and the list — which is also where it belongs, since creating a group is not an operation *on* a group — and a re-render no longer closes it, because closing a form somebody is typing into is its own defect. The test **refuses to provision a group first**, asserts `offsetParent` rather than mere presence, and names the masked state in its failure message. Falsified by putting the form back inside the editor: it went red with the right sentence. |
| D-76 | ~~**The Groups screen had no stylesheet section, and used two classes that do not exist.**~~ Found by a design review on 2026-08-18. `loadGroups` and `loadRoles` both write `class="pick on"` onto the open row and **there was no `tr.pick.on` rule** — so clicking a row changed nothing visible, while the panel it opens sits near 780px and is below the fold on a 1366-wide window. The markup used `class="head"`, which does not exist either: the Groups title rendered at browser-default `h2` against every other page's 33px, its subtitle as body copy, and the editor's two button groups stacked on two left-aligned lines instead of a row with the exit pushed right. `#groupEditor` had no padding, so every section label sat sixteen pixels left of the column it headed. And the inline picker wore `.setting` — a 620px flex row built for *a number and a unit* — so a listbox of `folder/service` paths inherited a 116px right-aligned monospace box and the checkbox inherited the width too. | 2026-08-18, with the screen | Not acceptable. **All five are one fact: the screen was written and the stylesheet was not.** Roles, built hours earlier, got `.rolesection` / `.rolegroup` / `.roleprivilege`; Groups got nothing and borrowed. | A design review, which is the only thing that would have found it — every test passed | **The screen looked finished and two of its interactions were invisible.** A reader clicking a row saw no change at all. | **RESOLVED 2026-08-18.** Its own section: the selected row (which repairs Roles as well), `.editorhead`, the editor's padding, a paired grid for members-beside-services, a fact list instead of two paragraphs of grey hint, and `.picker`. `scrollIntoView` on both screens. |
| D-77 | ~~**A backtick inside an HTML comment inside a template literal stopped `console.js` parsing, and the symptom was a sign-in screen.**~~ Two explanatory comments were written as `<!-- … -->` *inside* the rendered template; one of them contained `` `pill()` ``, which closed the literal. The file then failed to parse, the boot script never ran, and the console showed its sign-in view — so every browser test failed on a selector rather than on the cause, and the page's own text said *Sign in* while the session was perfectly valid. | 2026-08-18 | Not acceptable, and it is the third quoting mistake of the day in a nested language — after a CSS attribute selector inside a C# string inside JavaScript, and a `\s` that became a real newline. | Immediately | **A static file's syntax error builds clean and ships.** The compiler never sees `console.js`; `dotnet build` copies it. So the whole class of defect presents as a runtime symptom with no diagnostic — here, as *not signed in*. | **RESOLVED 2026-08-18**, and the guard is cheaper than the diagnosis: the development script now runs `node --check` over every script in `wwwroot` **before** building and refuses to build if one fails. Two seconds against half an hour of looking in the wrong place. **What is owed is the same guard in CI**, where [D-63](#) means nothing runs. |
| D-75 | **The console suite produced one failure in a solution-wide run that four later runs did not reproduce, and the name was not captured.** **Recurred 2026-08-18 with the name captured**, which is the half the repair bought: `ListPagingTests.Filtering_returns_to_the_first_page` failed in a nine-suite run — *"filtering left the table empty while there were matches"* — then passed in isolation and passed in the next full run. So the suspicion is confirmed as contention rather than as a paging defect, and the second half of this debt is still the fix: `OneSuiteAtATime` serialises the database-backed suites and not the HTTP-backed ones, so the console and conformance suites provision against one server in whatever order they happen to run. Two isolated runs of the suite and a second solution-wide run were all green (32/32, 32/32, and nine of nine suites). The suspicion is contention: `ListPagingTests` and `GroupsPageTests` both provision and remove objects on the shared development server while the conformance suite reads it, and `OneSuiteAtATime` serialises the database-backed suites but not the HTTP-backed ones against each other. **That last sentence is false, and it is the whole of what was still owed here.** `ArcGisClient` and `ConsoleTest` both call `OneSuiteAtATime.Enter()`, and both have since the console suite was created on 2026-08-18 at 01:26 — so the console suite has never existed without the lock, and the recurrence recorded later that same day was already serialised. **Measured 2026-08-23** by launching both test hosts at once rather than by reading: the conformance suite, 66 seconds alone, reported 199 and finished at 201 while the console suite finished at 142, which is one waiting for the other and not two overlapping. Both green, 83 and 316. **And the failure this row names has a different cause, now found:** `Filtering_returns_to_the_first_page` failed because its own fixture published `Math.Max(0, 11 - existing)` probe services, which is zero whenever the folder already holds eleven — so the filter for this suite's own prefix matched nothing and the test reported an empty table *while there were matches*. There were none. That is [D-143](#), fixed 2026-08-23 with a floor of one. | 2026-08-18 | Not acceptable, and recorded rather than dismissed because [D-30](#) is a debt about instruments being wrong three times and [D-43](#) is one about tests that pass on a warm cache. **A suite that fails once in five runs is a suite whose green is worth less than it looks.** | Now, and the first step is mechanical | **The method failure is the larger half:** the run was grepped for its summary lines and the full output was discarded, so the failing test's name is gone. Every measurement in this repository is only as good as the log that survives it. | **CLOSED 2026-08-23, and the second half was never owed.** The first half is enforced rather than remembered: the script's `test` mode writes the whole run to `suite.log` and prints the per-suite lines from it, so a name can never be lost to a grep again. The second half asked for a lock that already existed and was already held by both HTTP-backed suites; the measurement is in the description. **What this row actually cost is a diagnosis nobody re-read.** It named a cause, the cause was plausible, and it stood for five days while the real defect sat in the fixture of the very test it named — which is [D-130](#)'s shape, a claim left standing in the document that restated it. The instruction it left behind was also wrong in a way that mattered: *re-run a console failure in isolation before believing it* is exactly what hid D-143, because in isolation the folder holds fewer services and the probe count comes out positive. **A test that passes alone and fails in company is not thereby the company's fault.** **Recurred 2026-08-20, in the conformance suite this time, and the diagnosis got further than before.** `ArcGisConsistencyTests` failed six of fourteen in a full run and passed all fourteen twice when run alone. Every failure was a 404 on `hosted/d53_import_539fd2` — the alphabetically first service, and the only private one — reached with a valid `root` token. **The login throttle was ruled out by measurement rather than by argument**: four failed sign-ins in the fifteen-minute window against a per-address limit of fifty, counted out of `login_attempt`. So a private service was momentarily invisible to an administrator while another suite was running, and which suite has not been found. The class this row names — *serialised against each other only by luck* — is unchanged; what is new is that it now reaches the conformance suite, where a red run is the thing that decides whether a protocol surface works. **The suites need a shared-server discipline rather than another repaired test.** **And 2026-08-20 it was this repository's own new test doing it, which is the half worth admitting.** `WfsConformanceTests` switches a service's feature face off and back on; it restored by writing nulls, which is not *unchanged* but *unconfigured*, and it wiped an explicit capability ceiling on the service that happens to sort first. A console test failed hours later with seven checkboxes that had turned themselves on. **Restoring a value you never read is not restoring**; it reads the document first now and echoes it back. The window between the two writes is still there, and that window is what this row is about. **Recurred again 2026-08-20, and this time with the name this row was opened for**: `ListPagingTests.Filtering_returns_to_the_first_page` failed in a ten-suite run and passed three of three when run alone, minutes later, against the same server and the same data. **The console was not touched in that change**, so this is the row's original symptom repeating with nothing new to blame. Three recurrences in one day — this one, `ArcGisConsistencyTests`, and this repository's own WFS test corrupting a service's capabilities — is the argument for a shared-server discipline rather than a fourth repaired test. **CAUSE FOUND 2026-08-20, and it accounts for all three.** The conformance classes written that day — WFS, WMS, MapServer, OGC Features, `GateFindingsTests` and `ServiceFolderConformanceTests` — were not in the `[Collection("catalogue walk")]` collection, and xUnit parallelises test *classes* by default. So `WfsConformanceTests`, which switches a live service's feature face off and on, ran concurrently with every class that reads the catalogue. **This row spent two days calling it *serialised only by luck*; the luck was an attribute nobody had added.** All six classes are in the collection now. That does not close the row — `OneSuiteAtATime` still does not serialise the HTTP-backed suites against each other across processes, so a solution-wide run can still put two of them on one server — but the within-process half, which is what produced every recurrence actually observed, is closed. **PARTLY RESOLVED, and the condition this row set for itself is met: two consecutive full runs green with no isolation re-runs** — 1,591 tests, 10 suites, twice on 2026-08-20. That closes the within-process half, which produced every recurrence actually observed. **What is still owed is the cross-process half**: `OneSuiteAtATime` serialises the database-backed suites and not the HTTP-backed ones, so a solution-wide run can still put two of them on one server. The row stays open for that and for nothing else. **Recurred 2026-08-21 and the condition is un-met again.** `ReachabilityTests.Every_service_reaches_its_own_limits_page_from_a_click` failed in an eleven-suite run — the page's visible text began *Sign in*, so the browser was anonymous — and passed three of three when run alone, then passed in the next full run. **Nothing in that change touched the console or authentication**: it added a raster reader, a colour rule and a new test project. What it did change is the shape of the run, because an eleventh suite alters when the HTTP-backed ones overlap. That is exactly the cross-process half this row now names, and it is the fifth recurrence. **The two-green-runs condition is reset**, and re-earning it by luck is what this row exists to stop counting as evidence. **Recurred through 2026-08-21, and the shape changed enough to be worth writing down.** Four solution-wide runs in one afternoon each ended with exactly one console failure, a different test each time — `EveryScreenTests.Every_server_screen_opens_without_the_page_failing`, `EveryScreenTests.Every_page_of_a_layers_editor_opens_on_both_surfaces`, `ReachabilityTests.Every_service_reaches_its_own_limits_page_from_a_click` twice, and `ListPagingTests.Filtering_returns_to_the_first_page`. **Every one of them failed inside the browser rather than inside an assertion**: a `TaskCanceledException` wrapping a socket abort at 32-36 seconds, or `getComputedStyle(document.getElementById('app'))` throwing because the console shell had not loaded. **And they pass on re-run**, though not always the first time — one of them failed three times in isolation and passed the fourth, which is why *passes alone* stopped being a usable test for whether a failure is real. That cost an hour of diagnosis on 2026-08-21 and the hour is the reason this paragraph exists. **What this adds to the row.** The 2026-08-20 diagnosis — the `[Collection("catalogue walk")]` attribute — was real and fixed the catalogue-corruption half; it did not touch this. This half is the browser harness under a solution-wide run, where a second suite is driving the same server, and it is `OneSuiteAtATime` not covering the HTTP-backed suites across processes, which this row has named as owed since 2026-08-18. **Until that is paid, a single console failure in a solution run is not evidence of anything**, and this repository has now spent time twice treating one as though it were. **AND THE 2026-08-21 DIAGNOSIS ABOVE IS WRONG. The four failures were the machine, not this row.** The paragraph before this one attributes them to `OneSuiteAtATime` not covering the HTTP-backed suites across processes, and says a single console failure in a solution run is not evidence of anything. The owner then said the machine felt starved, and measuring found Windows at **96% of its commit limit** — 2.9 GB free of 69.7 — while physical RAM had 22 GB free and the CPU was at 23%. `vmmemWSL` alone held **14 GB of commit** for a 6.6 GB working set. **Every one of the four failures was a browser-level fault under that pressure**: socket aborts at 32-36 seconds, `getComputedStyle` throwing because the console shell never loaded, and one outright `OutOfMemoryException` in the conformance suite. None was an assertion. **`wsl --shutdown` returned 13 GB**, and the full suite then passed on the first attempt — 11 suites, 1,702 tests, including every console test that had failed that afternoon. **What made this hard to see is worth keeping.** *Passes alone* stopped being a usable signal, and the reason was not a flaky harness: commit pressure is load-dependent, so one test in isolation fits where a solution-wide run does not. A test that fails in a full run and passes alone looks exactly like contention and was not. **And the fix was already written.** `.wslconfig` has capped the VM at 15 GB and asked for gradual memory reclaim since 2026-08-19; the file's own closing note says it takes effect only when the VM next starts and that this was not done then, because it stops every container. Two days later it still had not been. **A configuration that is correct and unapplied is a configuration that is not doing anything**, which is the same shape as this repository's own debts. **What survives of the 2026-08-21 paragraph** is the underlying gap it names — `OneSuiteAtATime` genuinely does not serialise the HTTP-backed suites across processes, and that is still owed since 2026-08-18. It just was not what failed that day. |
| D-74 | **Adding a value to an enumeration leaves every reader of the old set silently wrong, and this has now happened three times in one day.** `SharingScope.Group` (ADR-036) left **five** parsers behind — `AdminEndpoints.TryReadScope`, `HostedDataEndpoints`, and the readers in `PostgresAdminCatalog`, `PostgresLayerCatalog` and `PostgresSystemServices` — plus two writers and the `creator` user-type ceiling, which withheld all four new `groups:*` privileges from every member who is not unrestricted. | 2026-08-18 | Not acceptable; it is [D-71](#) generalised. D-71 was about readers of a *constant* that had become a lookup; this is readers of an *enumeration* that gained a member. The compiler finds neither, because the old code still compiles and still means something. | Immediately, and the shape of the fix is known | **A parser that does not know a value refuses it**, so the feature silently does nothing and every test written around it passes — the group read-path measurement showed 200 for all four callers because the service had never left `public`. That is worse than a crash. | **RESOLVED 2026-08-18, and it happened a fourth time before the guard was written** — both member handlers validated a role against `Roles.All`, so a deployment could define a role and assign it to nobody. `EnumeratedValuesAreCoveredTests` now carries four checks: every file that enumerates two sharing scopes must enumerate all of them, every privilege must be admitted by some user type, no ceiling may admit a privilege that does not exist, and nothing outside the seed may read the compiled grants. **A discard arm is not accepted as proof of exhaustiveness**, deliberately: four of the five parsers that missed `group` had one and read the fourth scope as `private`, serving a group-scoped service to nobody — silently, which is worse than a refusal. A file that means its default names the value in an `enum-default-is-deliberate:` comment, which is greppable and forces the author to say which value they chose. |
| D-71 | **Three places read the compiled role grants after the store became the answer, and a fourth kind of failure hid all of them for half an hour.** `WithheldByUserType` (the method whose whole purpose is to explain a refusal), `/admin/members`'s `grants` block and its `roles` list all answered from `Roles.PrivilegesOf` — correct until 2026-08-18 and wrong immediately after. Each agreed with the store until something edited a role. | 2026-08-18, in the same commit that made grants editable | Not acceptable, and the pattern is worth naming: **replacing a constant with a lookup leaves every other reader of the constant silently wrong.** A compiler cannot find them because the constant still exists and still compiles. | Found by measuring the feature rather than reviewing it | Each was a screen or a refusal telling an operator something the server no longer believed. | **RESOLVED 2026-08-18, and the class is closed too.** All three read the store, and `EnumeratedValuesAreCoveredTests` now fails the build if anything outside the migration seed, the definition and the seam reads `Roles.Grants` or `Roles.PrivilegesOf`. **It found four offenders on its first run and all four were doc comments** — including one explaining the reason not to read it — so the check strips comments before searching: a guard that fires on its own documentation teaches people to disable guards. |
| D-72 | **A build that fails to copy its output reports no compiler error, and a filtered log looks clean.** A running server holds its dependencies' DLLs; `dotnet build` then emits `MSB3021`/`MSB3027` and the usual `grep -E "CS[0-9]+"` finds nothing. The server ran a mixture of old and new assemblies while two end-to-end measurements contradicted each other — a grant that should have taken effect did not, then a revocation that should have taken effect did not, in opposite directions. | 2026-08-18 | Not a compromise, a method defect. It cost more than the three code defects it was hiding. | Immediately, and it is done for the development script | **Every measurement taken against a half-built server is worthless and does not say so.** This is the same family as [D-30](#)'s *"the harness has now been wrong three times"*: the instrument, not the subject. | **RESOLVED for local runs 2026-08-18, after it cost a second measurement.** The script's `build` stops the server first and refuses to start on any error. Then the same fault reappeared in a different shape: `dotnet test graticula.sln` builds before testing, the running server holds `Graticula.Host.exe`, that project's build fails — and **its 213-test suite simply does not run**, while the summary reports eight suites passing, which reads as success. So the script gained `test`: build with the server down, start it, run with `--no-build`, keep the whole log, and **fail if fewer than nine suites reported**. Earlier runs were checked and did include the Host suite, so no claim in this repository rested on the gap. **What is owed is the same guard in CI**, where [D-63](#) means nothing runs. |
| D-67 | ~~**A per-service statement timeout was stored, reported, and never applied.**~~ ADR-031 §2a listed it as configurable from the decision onward; the admin API accepted it, `PostgresAdminCatalog` wrote it, the `GET` said it back and the console drew a box for it — and **no query path read the value.** The only thing bounding a statement was ADR-007 §4.8's fixed 30 seconds on the pool, shared by every layer on a data source. | 2026-08-16, when ADR-031 shipped | It was not acceptable; it was unnoticed. The knob was written with the screen and the enforcement was left for the query path, and nothing compared the two. Found 2026-08-18 while wiring the request deadline beside it, because putting the two time bounds on one page made *which of these actually does anything* a question somebody would ask. | Reached by putting a second time bound next to it | **Reporting a limit that is not enforced is the fault ADR-031 §3a already had to correct once** — a service advertising a `maxRecordCount` it did not honour. An operator narrowing a statement timeout during an incident would have got no narrowing and no error. | **RESOLVED 2026-08-18.** Carried onto `PublishedLayer`, lowered in `LayerConnections.SourceFor`, applied at the one factory every command in `PostGisFeatureSource` goes through. Measured: with 1,000 ms set, 11 of 30 concurrent full-table queries were cut with 504; with nothing set, 30 of 30 served. |
| D-68 | ~~**Npgsql's command timeout costs a pooled connection every time it fires.** The per-service statement bound (D-67) is enforced as `NpgsqlCommand.CommandTimeout`, which gives up on the socket read and throws `NpgsqlException` wrapping `TimeoutException` — the connector is left in an unknown state, so Npgsql discards the physical connection rather than returning it.~~ **Measured 2026-08-24 and it does not.** A clean server-side `statement_timeout` raises `57014`, leaves the connection usable, and has millisecond resolution — and so, it turns out, does the client-side bound on the first two counts. | 2026-08-18, with D-67's fix | **Two better routes were measured and both cost more than they were worth today.** A pool keyed by (connection string, timeout) is server-side and free per query, but fifty services with fifty bounds on one database would hold fifty pools — §60 forbids making a large deployment pay for this. A `SET statement_timeout` on the borrowed connection was measured as safe (Npgsql discards connection state on return to the pool — checked against this database, `SET` to 137 ms, next borrow of the same PID saw `0`), but it must lead the command text, and a leading `SET` changes which result set the reader opens on — a trap in the one path that streams. So: the working enforcement now, the cheaper mechanism later. | **When a deployment configures statement timeouts on enough services for the connection churn to show**, or when the command factory is next touched — it is the single seam, so the change is local. Also whenever the whole-second limitation bites: `CommandTimeout` is integer seconds, which is why the admin API refuses a sub-second value rather than rounding it up and giving a service more time than it asked for. | ~~A firing timeout is already the unhappy path, and it now costs a TCP connection and a new backend on top. Under sustained load against a slow layer that is churn where there was none.~~ **Measured 2026-08-24 and there is no churn: the backend id is the same before and after.** What is left costs nothing at runtime — a bound can only be whole seconds, and the admin API already refuses a sub-second value rather than rounding it up and granting more time than was asked for. | **CLOSED 2026-08-24: the premise was measured and it was wrong, and what survives is not a compromise.** **The instrument needs no reading of Npgsql's internals**: `pg_backend_pid()` names the server process at the other end of this physical connection, and a pool of exactly one makes a changed id mean one thing. A `CommandTimeout` firing left the same backend answering — **10981 before, 10981 after** — so the connector goes back to the pool rather than being discarded; Npgsql sends a cancellation request. The server-side bound keeps its connection too, so **the comparison this row rests on has no difference in it**. **Why that closes it rather than narrowing it.** [CLAUDE.md §62](../CLAUDE.md) says debt is a compromise somebody accepted with a condition that makes it unacceptable. The compromise recorded here was *paying a connection per firing*, and nobody is paying it. What is left is that `CommandTimeout` takes whole seconds — a property of a library, not a choice this project made — and the admin API already handles it correctly by refusing a sub-second value rather than rounding it up and granting a service more time than it asked for. **A library limit that is already handled is not debt**; it is how the thing works. `CommandTimeoutCostTests.Neither_end_of_a_statement_bound_costs_the_physical_connection` holds both halves in one test, because two tests each measuring one end can both pass while the comparison between them is false — which is exactly how this row came to say what it said. It asserts the measured behaviour, so a future Npgsql that does discard the connector fails it and names the backend ids, and this row can be opened again on evidence. |
| D-06 | **Dependency licensing is deliberately unexamined.** ~~Every row in [DEPENDENCY-LICENSES.md](../DEPENDENCY-LICENSES.md) is `UNVERIFIED`~~ **Narrowed 2026-08-15 by the §66 licensing gate:** all six direct dependencies are now verified from their own nuspecs and the gate passes. What remains unexamined is **transitive** dependencies. | 2026-08-12 | Verifying licences by hand was deferred while the dependency set was still moving. It stopped moving: six packages, four of them test-only. | Before any distribution, and before adding a dependency with a copyleft parent | A transitive GPL component linked into a shipped assembly would make the Apache-2.0 outbound licence (Q-73) unlawful to grant. The direct set is clean and the graph beneath it has never been enumerated — `dotnet list package --include-transitive` is the missing step. | **RESOLVED 2026-08-24.** Direct: verified 2026-08-15, passing. **Transitive: enumerated, and the answer is the one the row was opened on — nothing in the graph is GPL, LGPL or AGPL.** `dotnet list package --include-transitive` over the whole solution gives **thirty-three packages**, each licence read from its own `.nuspec` in the local cache, which is the method the direct six were verified by: MIT for most of them, BSD-3-Clause for `NetTopologySuite`, the PostgreSQL licence for `Npgsql`, Apache-2.0 for the xunit family, and a BSD-style licence stated by URL for `BitMiracle.LibTiff.NET`. Half the list is test-only and is enumerated anyway. The table is in [DEPENDENCY-LICENSES.md](../DEPENDENCY-LICENSES.md), which is where it belongs. **And the first pass got it wrong in a way worth keeping**: it read each nuspec from the oldest cached version rather than the resolved one, and reported two `Microsoft.Extensions.*Abstractions` packages under the old .NET Library EULA from 1.1.0 folders nothing references. Resolved, they are 8.0.2 and MIT. Which artefact was measured is part of the measurement. |
| D-05 | ~~A-019 is validated only on PostGIS.~~ **Rewritten 2026-08-12 by Q-67 and now much smaller: no engine except PostGIS serves tiles, so the tile half of this debt is gone. Deferring is the honest option; the alternative was to leave a benchmark permanently listed as "next" and never run. The PostGIS result is still informative — it bounds our own CPU and allocation cost, which is engine-independent — but it says nothing about the two engines' WKB output, driver materialisation cost, or spatial index selectivity. | **Before any commitment is made that Oracle or SQL Server layers serve tiles at parity.** Concretely: before ADR-008 §4.8's tile path is called decided, or before the multi-database claim appears in user-facing material. **Repayment path found 2026-08-12**, and it stands on the library rather than on where we noticed it: `Testcontainers.MsSql` and `Testcontainers.Oracle` are public packages that start both engines as throwaway containers from the test run itself. **Confirmed 2026-08-16** that a peer of our scale runs exactly this — both packages at 4.11.0 in their dependency manifest, alongside MySQL, PostgreSQL and Redis ([reference-reading-log.md](research/reference-reading-log.md)) — which makes it a used route rather than a plausible one. Nothing is installed and nothing persists. That removes the stated obstacle — what remains is only machine headroom, and the Oracle image is the heavy one. | Reduced by Q-67 but not eliminated. Registered Oracle and SQL Server layers still serve features, still return WKB into an NTS graph, and A-037's allocation ceiling was measured on exactly that machinery. If the feature path has the same ceiling on those engines — and there is no reason yet to think it does not — then the multi-database promise has an unmeasured limit on the workload the product leads with. | **CLOSED 2026-08-24, and it had been closed by decision since 2026-08-14 — this row was the last place that did not know.** Not repaid: **dissolved**. [v1-scope](v1-scope.md) says it in as many words — *"D-05 closes. The debt was that the feature path is unmeasured on SQL Server and Oracle. There is no SQL Server or Oracle"* — and [CLAUDE.md §1](../CLAUDE.md) makes that document authoritative where any other disagrees. **So the row's own diagnosis was stale rather than wrong when written.** Its last sentence, *"Registered Oracle and SQL Server layers still serve features"*, is false: **measured 2026-08-24, `src/` holds exactly one provider, `Graticula.Providers.PostGis`**, and every remaining occurrence of the word *oracle* in the source is either an account-enumeration oracle or PostGIS being called the oracle a test is checked against. There is nothing left to measure the feature path on. **The trigger's second clause was checked rather than assumed**: no user-facing material makes the multi-database claim — `README.md` mentions neither engine — and [architecture-completeness.md](architecture-completeness.md) already records *v1 is PostGIS only (Q-88); six dialects deferred*. What still named D-05 as live were two rows of [architecture-assumptions.md](architecture-assumptions.md), now corrected. **This is [D-126](#) and [D-130](#) in the flesh** — a decision taken in one file and left standing in every document that restated it — and the Testcontainers repayment path this row spent two dated paragraphs finding was work for a scope that no longer exists. |
| D-01 | ~~RLS delegation and per-data-source pooling are incompatible.~~ | 2026-08-12 | Found by fresh-challenger review G5 | — | — | **RESOLVED same day** — [security.md](security.md) §2. Our authorization is the baseline and was always going to exist; RLS delegation becomes an opt-in provider capability using transaction-scoped identity switching, so pools do not fragment. |
| D-02 | ~~Tenant identity is not part of the cache key.~~ | 2026-08-12 | Found by G5 | — | — | **RESOLVED same day** — [security.md](security.md) §3. Authorization splits into pre-lookup (uniform) and in-key (varies), and the key carries a grant fingerprint rather than a principal, so sharing survives where it is safe. |
| D-03 | ~~**Capability reports and detailed refusals disclose provider type and internal topology to any client that can reach a layer.**~~ **CLOSED 2026-08-24, and the trigger had fired twelve days earlier.** | 2026-08-12 | Designed for usability without an authorization dimension. Found by G7. | Before the capability report ships — **which it had**: both the WFS and WMS capabilities documents were serving the provider's name to anonymous callers, and `ErrorResponse` had shipped with no authorization dimension at all | Reconnaissance surface; reveals an organisation's internal database topology | **CLOSED 2026-08-24, and the rule was already understood in exactly one place.** [security.md](security.md) §5 stated it on 2026-08-12 — *detail is authorization-scoped* — and nothing implemented it. `ErrorResponse.WriteAsync` took an `HttpContext` and never asked it who was calling, so every caller read the same sentence. **Measured before repairing, by driving all sixteen arms of the switch.** Five named PostGIS or the engine behind a layer, two echoed the store's own `MessageText` verbatim to whoever sent the request, four sent the reader to `/admin/health`, `/admin/datasources/{id}/capability` or `/healthz/ready`, and one named `search_path`. **And the sixteenth arm already said the rule out loud** — the fallback refuses to repeat its reason *"because this endpoint is reachable without authentication"* — which makes this [D-46](#) as well as D-03: one arm knowing the rule and fifteen not. **The repair is one switch carrying both sentences**, not a second switch to keep in step with the first. `Explain` returns a `Refusal(Status, Operator, Anonymous)` and `Classify(exception, detailed)` picks; `WriteAsync` reads `RequestPrincipal` and asks for `admin:manageServer` — **the privilege the detailed sentences already assume**, since they say to go and read `/admin/health`, and telling a caller to open a door that will refuse them is worse than saying nothing. **No principal is treated as anonymous**, because a failure early enough that authentication has not run is exactly when a default-open read would make this handler its own hole. **The other surface, because one place is never all of them.** WFS 2.0's service abstract read *Read-only WFS 2.0 over PostGIS* and WMS 1.3.0's service keywords ended with `PostGIS` — both anonymous on any public service. The abstract loses three words no client reads; the keyword list keeps its own stated test, *five things a client can do here*, by replacing the one entry that failed it with `GetLegendGraphic`, a request this server actually answers. **Three guards, and the first two are a `[Theory]` over every arm rather than over the ones somebody remembered.** `Every_refusal_an_anonymous_caller_can_reach_is_free_of_the_provider` reads what an unprivileged caller receives and refuses ten disclosures — the engine, the extension, `search_path`, `/admin`, `/healthz`, the two words that reveal there are two stores. It reads the **public form** rather than checking that one was written, so an arm added with no anonymous sentence fails on what its operator sentence says. `Every_public_refusal_still_says_what_to_do` is the other half: scoping detail is not a licence to answer *an error occurred*, which is what this file was written to replace. And `An_administrator_still_reads_the_provider_and_the_reason` keeps the diagnosis reaching somebody, because otherwise this is deleting the diagnosis and calling it security. **Falsified**: nulling one arm's public form failed with the arm's name, the word it leaked and the whole sentence. 44 tests in the class, 549 across the four suites that touch this code. |
| D-04 | **Multi-tenant resource isolation is not designed.** One tenant's expensive query degrades another's in a shared request worker; §49's limits are per service, not per tenant. | 2026-08-12 | Raised by G5, not addressed by the D-01/D-02 resolution | Before multi-tenant deployments are supported | A noisy tenant becomes an availability problem for everyone on the worker | OPEN |
| D-07 | **A streaming response that fails after its first byte cannot report the failure.** The query endpoint pulls the first row before writing anything, so every failure the error classifier knows about lands while the response is still empty. A failure *after* that — the database dying mid-result — leaves a truncated JSON document, and the server aborts the connection because there is nothing better available. | 2026-08-13 | Found by running the endpoint: a forced statement timeout produced HTTP 504 whose body was a partially-written response object with an error appended into the middle of it. `Response.Clear()` resets status and headers and does **not** discard bytes already handed to the `PipeWriter`. | Before a client is expected to distinguish a truncated page from a short one | A client reading a truncated result sees fewer features and no error. For a paging client that is silent data loss, which is the failure mode this project has ruled worst. | **OPEN and blocked, with the trigger confirmed fired 2026-08-24.** Correct fix is a protocol decision, not a code change — [Q-91](open-questions.md). **The trigger is no longer a future condition**: it reads *before a client is expected to distinguish a truncated page from a short one*, and that is now how every ArcGIS paging client terminates against this server — `FeatureServerQueryWriter` writes `exceededTransferLimit` and a short page means *stop*. A truncated page is a short page with no error, which is the silent data loss this row names. **One half is repaired and it is not the half a client can see.** `ResponseOutcome.Truncated` records the request as a 500 rather than the 200 its header already promised, so an operator counting failures sees it; nothing reaches the caller. **Blocked rather than deferred**, and on an input rather than an opinion: Q-91's answer is an observation of what a real ArcGIS client does with each of three shapes, and [tools/pro-probe.py](../tools/pro-probe.py) is now the harness for exactly that kind of observation — what is missing is a server that can be made to fail mid-stream and somebody's Pro to point at it. Picking a shape by argument would put a protocol in the product without the evidence the question was opened to get. |
| D-08 | ~~**The statement timeout is 30 seconds because a number was needed.**~~ **CLOSED 2026-08-24.** The number is unchanged and its basis is not: a distribution under it, a measured slope out to sixteen million rows, and the one case it exists for constructed. Applied server-side as `statement_timeout` per pool, and not overridden when an operator has set `Options` themselves. | 2026-08-13 | Q-04 wants a measured value and has none. The default was previously *no timeout at all*, which surfaced as an unexplained HTTP 500. | When Q-04 is measured | Too low truncates legitimate large extents; too high lets one query hold a pooled connection while every layer sharing that data source waits. | **CLOSED 2026-08-24. Measured 2026-08-23 and the measurement moved the row rather than closing it; measured again on the 24th and it closes.** [benchmarks/statement-timeout](../benchmarks/statement-timeout/RESULTS.md), against a purpose-built table of **1,000,000 points**, because nothing in the existing corpus makes a thirty-second ceiling falsifiable. **Nothing this server can be made to issue comes near it.** The most expensive statement reachable through any face, over a million rows, is a full-extent render read at **330 ms** — ninety times inside the ceiling. The largest page the code will allow, `FeatureQuery.MaximumLimit` at 50,000 features with geometry, is **26 ms**. The most expensive one a *caller* can steer is an unindexed sort at **221 ms**. **The two statements that do approach it cannot be written.** A geodesic buffer over every row takes 9.3 s and a spatial self-join 5.8 s, and the query model has no way to express either — checked against the running server, not read out of the grammar: `lower(il) = 'x'`, `il in (select …)` and `il = il` are each refused by name. `AttributePredicate`'s remark lists what is absent — *arithmetic, function calls, column-to-column comparison and subqueries* — **and that was written as a safety property; this is the first time it has been measured as a performance one.** The thing bounding a caller's statement cost is the grammar, not the timeout. **So this row's own sentence is wrong about what the number does.** It cannot truncate a legitimate large extent, because a legitimate large extent is 330 ms. What is left for it to bound is a pathological plan and a lock wait — a DBA's `ALTER TABLE`, a checkpoint, a table that has lost its statistics — and for those thirty seconds is a deliberate choice: long enough to survive a brief lock, short enough that a permit is not held for a minute. **The number is unchanged and its basis is not.** It was *a number was needed*; it is now a figure with a distribution under it and a stated range where the distribution says nothing. **Two things the measurement could not say, and they were why this stayed open:** a table a hundred times this one — the extrapolation reaches 30 s at roughly 90 million rows, from one point on one machine with points rather than polygons — and a lock wait, which is the case the number now exists for and the one nobody has constructed. **CLOSED 2026-08-24: both were measured** — [benchmarks/statement-timeout](../benchmarks/statement-timeout/RESULTS.md) round 2, store-side, because `statement_timeout` is a PostgreSQL setting and it is the store's clock that runs out. **The extrapolation is now a slope.** The same full-extent render read at three sizes: **250 ms at 1 M, 1,024 ms at 4 M, 3,832 ms at 16 M** — 4.10× and 3.74× for 4× the rows, so **linear across a sixteen-fold range and slightly sublinear at the top**. The ceiling arrives at **roughly 120 million rows** extrapolating from either end, and the two ends agree. One point could not tell a linear cost from a superlinear one, and the difference decided whether the ceiling was at ninety million rows or at nine; it is at neither extreme, and no layer this product is likely to hold reaches it by size alone. **The lock wait is constructed, and it says three things.** One: `statement_timeout` does fire on a statement blocked on a lock — not obvious, because a blocked statement is not running — and it fires at the budget, **30.30 s against a 30 s ceiling**, and **3.31 s against 3 s**. Two: **the cost is exactly this row's own sentence, now with a number under it** — a read that takes **296 ms** unblocked holds its pooled connection for **30.30 s**, a hundredfold, while every layer sharing that data source waits behind it. Three: **`lock_timeout` separates the two cases in 2.30 s with a different SQLSTATE** — `55P03` rather than `57014` — and nothing sets it, though `LayerConnections` preserves an operator's `Options` so a deployment could today. **It should not yet**, and that is [D-150](#): `55P03` reaches `ErrorResponse` with no arm of its own and is answered *a database this server depends on is unreachable*, which sends whoever is on call to the network while a DBA holds a lock. Found by taking this row's own advice and looking at what happened next. **What is not closed here and is not this row's**: ADR-007 §4.8's quiesce — *drain its connections, hold its requests, let the DBA work, resume* — is still absent, and is still what would make the behaviour under DDL a choice rather than an accident. It is tracked as an obligation in [architecture-completeness.md](architecture-completeness.md), where every review column is `—`. This row's question was what the number bounds; the answer is that it bounds a lock wait, for thirty seconds, at the price measured above. |
| D-09 | ~~**Publishing a layer means hand-written SQL.**~~ **RESOLVED 2026-08-14.** `POST /admin/datasources` seals the credential inside the server with the key it already holds, and `POST /admin/layers` publishes over it. The sealing step that produced a wrong port and an authentication failure days later no longer exists as a human task. Registration probes before it writes, so a source we cannot reach leaves no row behind. Original: **Publishing a layer means hand-written SQL.** *(2026-08-13: no longer blocked — ADR-018 gives ADR-017's endpoints permissions to require. The friction is unchanged; the reason for not fixing it has gone.)* There is no admin API, so registering a data source requires sealing a connection string with the server's AES key outside the server and inserting rows by hand. | 2026-08-13 | ADR-017 is designed and unimplemented. | Before anyone but us runs this | The sealing step is easy to get wrong in ways that surface far away — a wrong port produced `28P01` at query time, not at registration. There is also no reason for the key to be handled outside the process at all. | **CLOSED 2026-08-24, and the row had been contradicting itself for ten days.** Its own text said **RESOLVED 2026-08-14** while this cell said `OPEN`, so the register disagreed with the register inside one row — which is the reason to check a row against the code before believing either half of it. **All three of the resolution's claims were measured, not read.** `POST /admin/datasources` and `POST /admin/layers` are mapped in `AdminEndpoints`. The credential is sealed **inside the process** — `PostgresAdminCatalog.RegisterDataSourceAsync` writes `_secrets.Protect(connectionString)` under a comment saying *sealed here, inside the process, with the key the server already* holds — so the out-of-process AES step this row was opened on no longer exists as a human task. And registration **probes before it writes**: `probe.ProbeAsync` runs first and a `CannotConnect` outcome refuses 400 with the reason, so a source nobody can reach leaves no row behind. The surface has since grown past what closed it — `PUT` and `DELETE` on a data source, `POST /admin/datasources/test`, `GET /admin/datasources/{id}/capability`. **The `ADR-017 §3` this cell pointed at is not unfinished work on this row**, and what is genuinely missing there is now [D-151](#) rather than a dangling reference. |
| D-10 | **ADR-015 is implemented for local passwords only.** Sessions, the first-start bootstrap, Argon2id and both rate limits are real. **OIDC, SAML 2.0, SCIM 2.0, API keys and mTLS are not**, ~~and neither is the ArcGIS `/generateToken` surface~~ — **that half was repaid 2026-08-20 and this row did not notice.** | 2026-08-13 | Built the identity source that air-gapped sites need first (ADR-015 §5 calls local accounts first-class, not a fallback), and stopped there rather than half-building five more. | OIDC before the first deployment with an IdP; `/generateToken` before any unmodified ArcGIS client is pointed at us | ADR-015 §5 lists four of these as free where a competitor gates them at Pro or Enterprise, so the positioning claim in Q-49 is not yet true. ~~`/generateToken` missing means Q-17's *existing clients keep working* is currently false for any client that authenticates.~~ **No longer true, since 2026-08-20.** | **OPEN and narrowed 2026-08-24; the second trigger had fired and been satisfied without this row hearing about it.** The trigger read *`/generateToken` before any unmodified ArcGIS client is pointed at us*, and both halves happened: Pro was pointed at the server, could read public services and **could not sign in** — the owner's *"user pass kabul etmedi"* — because ArcGIS clients post a form with `username` at `/rest/generateToken` and this server offered JSON with `name` at `/rest/auth/login`. **Measured 2026-08-24:** `POST` and `GET /rest/generateToken` are mapped, so are `GET` and `POST /admin/generateToken` for the Server Admin vocabulary, all four issue through the same `LoginService` so the throttle, the audit record and the one-message rule are shared rather than reimplemented, and `ArcGisTokenTests` in the conformance suite exercises them. So Q-17's *existing clients keep working* is now true for a client that authenticates, which is what this row said it was not. **What is left is the other five, and their trigger has not fired.** OIDC, SAML 2.0, SCIM 2.0, API keys and mTLS are absent — measured, not assumed: the words appear nowhere in `src/` outside two comments — and the condition on them is *OIDC before the first deployment with an IdP*. There is no deployment at all; the Operations review gate is still `—` for the same reason, waiting on an installation somebody runs. The port shapes exist; each source is an implementation rather than a redesign. |
| D-11 | ~~**Authorization does not exist.**~~ **RESOLVED 2026-08-13, and the resolution was replaced on 2026-08-14** when the owner directed that we adopt ArcGIS Portal's matrix. Authorization now follows Portal: roles grant privileges — five of Portal's six, Facilitator omitted, which nobody said out loud until 2026-08-24 ([ADR-018](adr/ADR-018-authorization-and-roles.md) §5's fourth inference) — a user type caps them, and **reading is governed by each layer's sharing scope rather than by a privilege**. The closed default survives in a better form — a layer is private to its owner until shared — and the ownership axis, which this entry said was *not yet due*, turned out to be how reading works. Original: **Authorization does not exist.** Authentication resolves a principal; nothing consults it. Every published layer is readable by anonymous, and the `role` table ships empty. | 2026-08-13 | Q-59 has not decided what the roles are, and ADR-015 §7 and review O3 both warn against inventing them — a control resting on a term nobody has defined. | Q-59, which now blocks rather than merely being open | A server that authenticates and does not authorize invites the assumption that signing in restricts something.
