The startup warning says otherwise at every start. | **CLOSED 2026-08-24, and like [D-09](#) this cell had been arguing with its own row.** The row said **RESOLVED 2026-08-13** and this cell said `OPEN`; worse, its reason — *the admin API is not built yet* — was refuted by [D-09](#) an hour earlier in the same pass, which measured `POST /admin/datasources` and `POST /admin/layers` mapped, sealing in-process and a probe before the write. **Two rows, the same stale-status defect, found the same day; that is the pattern rather than the coincidence.** **Every claim in the original was measured 2026-08-24 and every one of them has reversed.** *Nothing consults the principal*: `Authorize.RequireAsync` is called **73 times across seven endpoint files**, and reading is refused separately by `Authorize.RefuseReadAsync`, which answers **404 rather than 403** so a named layer cannot be used as a directory of what is published. *The `role` table ships empty*: `PlatformMigrations` seeds `role` and `role_privilege` from `Roles.All` and `Roles.PrivilegesOf`, so the five Portal roles and their privileges are created by the migration that creates the table. *Every published layer is readable by anonymous*: reading is governed by the layer's sharing scope, and [D-69](#) measured all three states end to end — anonymous reach went 404 / 404 / 200 across private, organization and public. *The startup warning says otherwise at every start*: there is no such warning left in `src/`. **And the condition this row hung on is answered.** It said *Q-59, which now blocks rather than merely being open*; [Q-59](open-questions.md) was **re-answered 2026-08-14** by owner direction — adopt ArcGIS Portal's role and user-type matrix — which is the decision the body of this row already describes. The register had the answer in one file and the block in another for ten days. |
| D-12 | **The per-address rate limit uses the socket address, never `X-Forwarded-For`.** Behind a reverse proxy every request appears to come from the proxy, so the address limit is effectively one shared bucket. | 2026-08-13 | Trusting a forwarded header without a configured trusted-proxy list lets any caller choose their own rate-limit bucket, which makes the limit zero. Too coarse is recoverable; forgeable is not. | Before a documented deployment behind a proxy | With one bucket, 50 failures anywhere disable sign-in for everyone. The per-account limit is unaffected, and by design a correct password still works. **REPAID 2026-08-24, [ADR-014](adr/ADR-014-tls-and-certificates.md) §5a, and its condition 3 discharged with it.** `Graticula:TrustedProxies` takes addresses or CIDR ranges and is **empty by default**, so a deployment that does not set it behaves exactly as it did. **A caller who is not a listed proxy is themselves, whatever they wrote** — that is the clause that makes the header unforgeable, and it is why this row could not be repaid by reading the header. The chain is read **right to left**, stopping at the first address that is not a listed proxy: the header is appended to, so a client's invented entries sit behind the one the proxy wrote and are never reached. **A mistyped range refuses to start**, because ignoring it leaves the deployment in exactly the state this replaces with nothing to say so. The caller is resolved once, before authentication, and the rate limiter and every path that records a source address read the same answer — two notions of *the caller* is how a log and a limiter come to disagree. `CallerAddressTests`, twelve cases. | ~~open~~ repaid 2026-08-24 |
| D-13 | **The timing gap between a known and an unknown account name is narrowed, not closed.** An unknown name pays for a hash it does not need, but it is not the same work, so the distributions still differ under enough samples. | 2026-08-13 | Closing it properly means verifying against a fixed decoy hash with the same parameters as the real ones, which requires knowing those parameters before the account is found — and they are stored per credential. | If account enumeration is ever demonstrated against a deployment | Account enumeration is the step before credential stuffing. | **CLOSED 2026-08-23, and this row was wrong about its own shape in two ways.** **First, the gap was not narrow.** The row says an unknown name *pays for a hash it does not need*, as though that were an approximation of the real cost. It was not: the real path does one verification, and `SpendComparableTime` hashed the submitted password and then verified against what it had just made — one hash *and* one verification. Measured with the real hasher at 4 MiB and two iterations, sampled alternately over fifteen rounds: **20.0 ms against 10.2 ms.** An unknown name took about twice as long as a known one, which is an enumeration oracle pointing the opposite way from the one that method was written to prevent. **Second, the reason it could not be closed does not hold.** The row says a fixed decoy needs the same parameters as the real ones and those are stored per credential, unknown before the account is found. The premise is true and the conclusion does not follow: a credential is rehashed at the current cost on the way through every successful login — which is why the parameters are stored per credential in the first place — so *the current parameters* are what an account in use has, and a decoy built from the same hasher matches it. `LoginService` now hashes one random password in its constructor and verifies against that. **11.6 ms against 11.1 ms**, the same measurement. **Perfect equality was never available and is not claimed.** An account that has not logged in since the cost was last raised verifies at the old cost, so it differs from the decoy — and from every other real account. A difference that exists between two real accounts does not say which names exist. **A third leak was found beside it and is closed with it.** The check read `!IsDisabled && Verify(...)`, so a disabled account skipped verification entirely and answered in a fraction of the time an enabled one took — while `LoginFailure.InvalidCredentials` exists precisely so that *wrong name*, *wrong password* and *disabled* are one answer, and its own remark says distinguishing them tells an attacker which names exist. The enum said one thing and the clock said another. Verification now runs first and the disabled check reads its result. **Guarded by counting, not by timing.** `LoginServiceTests` asserts that an unknown name performs the same hash and verification counts as a known one, that a disabled account is verified like an enabled one, and that the decoy is hashed once when the service is built rather than once per attempt — a lazy decoy would put a lock on the unknown-name path, and lock contention is itself a timing signal. `DecoyVerificationCostTests` keeps the two measurements above as ratios, because the claim they support is about Argon2 rather than about our code. **Its first version measured the two halves in sequence and was wrong by a factor of two** — each hash allocates four mebibytes and the first batch paid for the heap growing — which is now a comment on the helper. |
| D-14 | **A server that loses its last platform administrator cannot be recovered in band.** The setup flow only runs when there are no users at all, so a store with accounts and no `platform-administrator` grant has nobody who can grant one. | 2026-08-13 | **Found by running the ADR-018 upgrade against a store set up before it.** Migration 3 seeds the roles and grants none, so the existing administrator came out of the upgrade unable to administer anything — ADR-018 §4's failure reached by a different road. | Before the first deployment that is not ours, or sooner if role revocation reaches the admin API | Reads keep working; nothing administrative does. The only fix is SQL against the platform store. | **OPEN and blocked, with the trigger confirmed fired 2026-08-24 and the diagnosis confirmed accurate.** Detected and diagnosed, not repaired. Startup logs the exact recovery statement — `Log.NoAdministrator`, **Critical**, carrying the `insert into principal_role` that fixes it — and `RequireSetup()` really does run only when `AnyUserExistsAsync` is false, so this row's premise is right rather than merely plausible. Repairing it automatically means issuing a credential to a log whenever the last administrator's grant disappears, and *the last administrator's grant disappeared* is a state an attacker would like to arrange — so the safe version of this needs design, not a quick fix. **The trigger's second clause fired**: it read *sooner if role revocation reaches the admin API*, and revocation has five routes there. **All five refuse, and that is the half of this row that got better without it noticing** — `PUT /admin/members/{name}/role`, `DELETE /admin/members/{name}` and `POST /admin/members/{name}/disable` each name the only administrator in the refusal, and `DELETE /admin/roles/{name}` and `PUT /admin/roles/{name}/privileges` return `RoleChange.Administrator` before touching the store. Tested by `The_administrator_role_refuses_every_write` and `LastAdministratorTests`. **So the way in is now a migration, a partial restore, or direct access to the platform store**, and the last of those already holds the recovery statement — which is the premise the *said, not repaired* argument was resting on, and it has moved. Whether that changes the answer is [Q-137](open-questions.md), which names three and recommends none, because it moves a security boundary and §2 says that is an ADR rather than a change made while passing through. |
| D-15 | **The user-type ceiling is enforced but unreachable from any endpoint.** `Authorization.Resolve` intersects the role grants with the type's ceiling, and nothing in the HTTP surface currently requires a privilege — reads go through sharing, and the admin API does not exist. | 2026-08-14 | ADR-018 §3a was adopted with the model; the endpoints that consume it are the next increment. | The first admin endpoint | The ceiling is covered by unit tests and has never run against a request. Its failure mode is *silent over-permission*, which tests can demonstrate and only a live path can disprove. | **CLOSED 2026-08-23 by the live walk this row was waiting for.** Its trigger — *the first admin endpoint* — passed some time ago, and the row was left saying *the admin API does not exist*. **`UserTypeCeilingTests` makes a member with the `publisher` role and the `viewer` user type**, signs in as them the way a new member does, and publishes. `publisher` carries `content:publishFeatures`; the `viewer` ceiling is empty. The server answers 403 and says which of the two refusals it is: *your role grants it, and your user type does not permit it — granting the role again will not help*. **That sentence is the assertion**, because *ask an administrator to grant a role* would be advice that cannot work when the role is already granted. **With a control, which is what turns it into evidence.** The same role under `creator` is not refused for the privilege: a server that refused every publish would pass the first assertion and mean nothing. The request still fails — its data source does not exist — and what is asserted is that it fails for that reason instead of for the ceiling. **The member and its password exist for the length of one test**, and the password is made per run rather than written down. |
| D-16 | **Portal's privilege identifiers and default-role assignments are structural, not verified line by line.** The shape — two axes, three privilege groups, five default roles — is right; the exact catalogue is ours reading like Portal's. | 2026-08-14 | ADR-018 condition 2. Adopting a documented model from memory is how a compatibility claim becomes subtly false. | Before any surface claims Portal compatibility | An administrator migrating from Portal finds privileges that do not correspond, and trusts the mapping anyway. | **CLOSED 2026-08-24, and the trigger had fired: a surface does claim Portal compatibility.** `/sharing/rest` — Esri's own Portal path — serves `generateToken`, `search`, `community/groups` and `portals/self`, and `portals/self` emits Portal privilege identifiers to whoever asks. That is the condition, met. **Checked against Esri's published documentation** — the privilege list and the member-roles page, cited rather than the reference checkout, which is [ADR-030](adr/ADR-030-reading-the-reference-implementation.md)'s third condition. **The three identifiers that actually go on the wire are verbatim right**: `portal:admin:viewItems`, `portal:user:viewOrgItems`, `portal:user:viewOrgUsers`, each spelled that way in Esri's list and in the category it belongs to. **The eighteen internal ones were never a Portal claim, and the check proves it from the other direction**: not one matches Esri's spelling. [ADR-018](adr/ADR-018-authorization-and-roles.md) §6 already said the enum is ours and that the wire mapping lives in the compatibility layer, and this is that decision holding rather than a discovery. **The two worth naming are the two that look identical** — ours `features:edit` and `features:fullEdit`, Esri's `features:user:edit` and `features:user:fullEdit`, one segment apart. A reader skimming would call them the same thing; a client sending ours to a Portal would be refused. That is exactly this row's *reads like Portal's*, caught. **One thing was wrong, and it is the roles rather than the privileges.** Esri's built-in organisation roles are **six** — Viewer, Data Editor, User, Publisher, **Facilitator**, Administrator — and `Roles.All` has five. **Facilitator appears nowhere in this repository**, and two registers had written *five Portal roles* as though five were Portal's number. §4's rule justifies dropping it and nobody applied that rule out loud, so it is recorded as ADR-018 §5's fourth `INFERRED` entry for confirmation rather than adopted while passing through — which is [CLAUDE.md §2](../CLAUDE.md)'s rule about inference, and this row is what that rule exists to catch. |
| D-17 | **The request path reads the catalogue on every query, and that half is now deliberate.** *(2026-08-14: the table-shape half is fixed; the catalogue half is kept on purpose, and D-17's stated remedy is rejected as written — see the resolution column.)* `PostgresLayerCatalog.FindAsync` runs per feature request, so serving depends on the platform store being up. **Narrowed 2026-08-15 by [ADR-026](adr/ADR-026-serving-through-a-platform-store-outage.md):** the read still happens on every request, so revocation stays instant — but a store outage no longer stops serving outright. Public services are answered from the last catalogue entry seen; everything else is refused. The single-point-of-failure half of this debt survives for private and organisation data, deliberately. | 2026-08-14 | Found while writing [ADR-019](adr/ADR-019-portal-server-split.md) §4, which claims a seam between the catalogue domain and the runtime domain. **The claim was written down as a principle before it was checked.** | Before any performance claim about serving, and before ADR-017 §6's degraded surface is called done (ADR-019 condition 2) | Fusing the tiers spent the isolation Esri gets for free. If the catalogue store is down, serving stops. | **CLOSED 2026-08-24 — and partly resolved before that, with the rest reclassified.** D-17 said the fix was *a resolved service context, cached and invalidated*, and that remedy is now **rejected as stated**: a `PublishedLayer` carries the sharing scope, the owner and the started/stopped status, so caching it caches the authorization decision — a layer made private stays readable, and a service stopped mid-incident keeps answering. Two servers over one store makes that unfixable locally, because the mutation happens in the other process. So `ServiceContexts` caches **only the table shape** (`information_schema` + `ST_EstimatedExtent`), which is where the cost actually was: measured 2026-08-14, **5.76 ms → 1.64 ms of fixed per-request overhead**, a 100-row query 41% faster and layer metadata 48% faster. The residual 1.64 ms is the catalogue read, kept on purpose. **The availability half is not fixed and is now [Q-95](open-questions.md)**, because it is a question about what should happen rather than a compromise anybody accepted. **CLOSED 2026-08-24: both halves have somewhere to be, and neither is here.** [Q-95](open-questions.md) was **answered 2026-08-15 by the owner** — *serve public-only while blind* — and built as [ADR-026](adr/ADR-026-serving-through-a-platform-store-outage.md); what remains of it is ADR-026's own conditions and [D-127](#)'s per-face coverage, both tracked where the work is. **And this row's trigger is discharged**: it reads *before any performance claim about serving, and before ADR-017 §6's degraded surface is called done (ADR-019 condition 2)*, and ADR-019 condition 2 is discharged today on the evidence it asked for — the feature-query benchmark reports the catalogue read as **1.8 ms flat, 70% of a one-feature request**, which makes it the headline rather than the omission. **The table-shape half was always the measurable one and it was measured**: 5.76 ms → 1.64 ms of fixed per-request overhead. What is left of D-17 is a sentence about a decision that has been taken, which is not debt. |
| D-18 | **`/admin/health` is anonymous and therefore redacted, which is the wrong trade and the only one available.** Sessions live in the platform store, so during the outage the endpoint exists for, nobody can authenticate — and an unauthenticated caller must not be told the store's host and port. | 2026-08-14 | Found while discharging ADR-017 condition 1: the endpoint was echoing `127.0.0.1:55432` to anyone who could reach the port. | When ADR-017 §6's break-glass path is designed (A-051) | The administrator who most needs the detail is exactly the one who cannot prove they are an administrator. The endpoint currently says *redacted* to the person it was written for. | OPEN. The break-glass path is the fix and it is the classic shape of an authentication bypass, so it needs design rather than a quick patch. |
| D-19 | **There is no published image and no release.** The compose file builds from source, so the quickstart needs the repository and a working Docker build rather than a `docker pull`. | 2026-08-14 | Packaging was built before there was anywhere to publish to; a registry, a tag scheme and a release process are Q-72's territory and Q-72 is unanswered. | The first release | The target user has a lapsed licence and a deadline. *Clone the repository and build two images* is a different proposition from *docker compose up*, and the difference is most of the adoption. It also blocks the other half of ADR-016 condition 1, which needs two published tags to test a stale image against a newer store. | OPEN. |
| D-20 | **`features:edit` is stricter here than in ArcGIS Portal, because editor tracking does not exist.** Portal's *edit* covers adding and changing *your own* features; *full edit* reaches everybody's. We map adds to `features:edit` and **all** updates and deletes to `features:fullEdit`. | 2026-08-14 | The distinction rests on knowing who created each row, which is editor tracking, deferred under Q-58. Treating an update as *probably yours* would be a guess made with somebody else's data. | When **Q-58c** lands — *domains, subtypes and editor tracking*. **Sharpened 2026-08-24 and the imprecision was a trap**: this said *Q-58*, and Q-58 was split when [ADR-013](adr/ADR-013-feature-service-data-model.md) answered the identity and relationship halves. **Q-58a and Q-58b are both answered**, so somebody checking *has Q-58 landed* would find two green answers and could close this row on them — while the third, which is the one this row actually needs, is open and unstarted. | A deployment that wanted contributors able to edit only their own work cannot express it: they get everything or nothing. Narrower than Portal, and narrow is the safe direction. | OPEN, and documented in the endpoint rather than left to be discovered. |
| D-21 | **Paging by `resultOffset` is not stable.** *(2026-08-14: improved, not closed. `orderByFields` is now supported, and a client that orders by a unique key — which is what the ArcGIS SDK does — gets stable pages. A client that pages without ordering still does not.)* ~~The query orders by identity when an offset is given, which stops a page repeating itself~~ — **and that sentence hid a defect for four days, fixed 2026-08-18.** *When an offset is given* was literally true and was the bug: `resultOffset=0` is not given, so the **first page came back in heap order while every later page came back in identity order**. The two do not line up, so a client walking pages skipped rows and repeated others — **deterministically, not as a race**. Measured: `hosted/tr_il` answered objectids `[1, 33]` for offset 0 and `[2, 3]` for offset 1, four times out of four; three of the owner's ten layers did it and the other seven were small enough that heap order happened to be identity order. **Now every limited result is ordered**, because a `limit` with no `order by` does not have a wrong order, it has none — and a server that returns an arbitrary subset and calls it a page has answered a different question. Re-measured after: all ten layers line up; a five-page walk of 200 over the 25,280-row layer returns 1,000 rows, 1,000 distinct, 0 repeated. **The cost is not measurable:** 2,000 rows from the two largest layers in 0.02 and 0.03 s, because the identity column is the primary key and the planner reads the index. **What remains open is the other half**, which is genuinely hard: a feature inserted or deleted between two requests still shifts every later page. | 2026-08-14 | Added because the ArcGIS Maps SDK pages by offset and refusing it refuses the client. Cursor paging needs a stable sort key exposed to the client, which is ADR-008 territory. | Before anyone syncs a large layer | A client walking a changing layer silently misses features. It sees no error; the pages simply do not cover the set. | **OPEN, and narrower — with the part that was a plain defect closed.** The first page is no longer ordered differently from the rest, which was a correctness bug rather than the inherent limitation this row is about, and it is held by `ArcGisConsistencyTests.Pages_do_not_overlap_or_skip` — **which was already in the suite and passing.** It picks the first service in the catalogue, and for four days that was a layer small enough for heap order to be identity order; it went red the first time an unrelated change moved which layer came first. A conformance test that only checks the fixture it happens to land on is a test whose coverage is a fact about the data. What is still open is offset paging over a changing layer, which needs a cursor and a stable sort key exposed to the client — ADR-008 territory, and documented on `FeatureQuery.Offset` rather than only here. |
| D-22 | ~~**`where` accepts only `1=1`, and that special case is load-bearing.** Every ArcGIS client sends it to mean *no filter*, so it had to be accepted; anything else is refused.~~ **STALE for some time, corrected 2026-08-18.** `WhereClause` parses a real subset of SQL-92 and has since before this row was last read; what was still true was one half of one idiom. | 2026-08-14; the grammar arrived afterwards and this row was never re-read | Parsing SQL fragments out of a request is how injection happens, and the real answer is ADR-008's query AST — which is still the answer, and is still not built ([D-40](#)). | ~~When the query AST exists~~ **Done for the idiom; the AST is still owed.** | The gap between what clients expect and what this supports is large and invisible until a user filters a layer and is refused. **The risk is that this special case grows** — one more literal, then a simple comparison — until a parser exists that nobody designed. **That risk is why the fix is a `[Theory]` of ten refusals rather than a widened parser.** | **CLOSED, and the row was describing a state the code had left.** Measured on the running server 2026-08-18 before touching anything: `objectid>5` returned 7 features of 12, `objectid > 5` and `OBJECTID>5` the same — so the clause is parsed, filters, and is case-insensitive on field names — while `name='x'` was refused with *'name' is not a field of this layer*, and `1=1 or 1=1`, `1=1--` and `1=1; drop table x` were all refused. The grammar arrived and this row was never re-read. **What was genuinely still broken was the other half of one idiom.** Only the literal `1=1` was recognised ahead of the grammar, so `1=0` — which is how a client asks for a layer's schema *without* its rows, and which Esri's own documentation uses — fell through and answered **400: '1' is not a field of this layer**. A true sentence about a request that was not asking for a field. `1=2` and `1=0&returnCountOnly=true` failed the same way. **Now any two integers compared with `=` are answered before the grammar sees them**, true or false: `1=1` and `0=0` mean no filter, `1=0`, `1 = 0`, `1=2` and `0=1` become a predicate no row satisfies — so the fields come back and the features do not, which is the point of the request. Measured after: `1=0` returns 0 features and 3 fields, `1=0&returnCountOnly=true` returns `count: 0`, and `1=1` still returns all 12. **Deliberately not general constant folding.** `1<2`, `1<>2`, `'a'='a'`, `1=1.0`, `1=1=1`, `=1` and `1=` all stay refused, and a `[Theory]` pins each one — because the reason the grammar has no rule for comparing two literals is that it is the shape every injection attempt starts with, and recognising a shape ahead of the grammar is exactly how a parser gets widened by accident. The warning stays written into the method. |
| D-23 | **Nothing checks a password against known-breached lists.** Length is the only rule ([ADR-015](adr/ADR-015-authentication.md) §6a), so `Passw0rd!` and a random nine characters are treated alike — and the first is in every wordlist. | 2026-08-14 | Recorded when the minimum was lowered from 12 to 8. A breach check is worth more than either number, and the honest reason it does not exist is that it needs a corpus (Pwned Passwords is ~10 GB, or a k-anonymity API call that leaks a hash prefix to a third party). Neither fits a product whose baseline is *one deployable against one PostgreSQL*, and neither had been thought about. | Before this is recommended for anything internet-facing | The realistic attack on a small deployment is credential stuffing, not offline cracking — and this is the defence aimed at credential stuffing. Argon2id and the rate limit slow guessing; they do nothing about a password that is already published. **PARTLY REPAID 2026-08-24 — [ADR-015](adr/ADR-015-authentication.md) §6a — and the part that is not repaid is the part this row said could not be.** A breach corpus is still 10 GB and the k-anonymity API still leaks a hash prefix to a third party, and neither fits one deployable against one PostgreSQL. What exists is a few hundred **bases** — what sits at the top of every published list — read twice before comparing, once literally and once with letter-for-digit substitutions undone, either reading matching with or without a trailing run of digits. `Passw0rd!`, `P@ssw0rd`, `password123` and `Sifre123` all reach `password` or `sifre`; `12345678` matches itself. **Two readings rather than one was the whole difficulty**: a single normalisation that undoes substitutions turns `12345678` into letters and the entry stops matching itself, which the first version did and the tests caught. **`Graticula:CommonPasswordFile` names a list of any size**, read at startup and added to the built-in one; a named file that is absent refuses to start, because a silent fallback would leave a deployment believing it had a defence it does not have. `CommonPasswordTests`, twenty-three cases, half of them passwords that must still be accepted. | **PARTLY REPAID 2026-08-24, and the unpaid half is unpayable as stated — checked 2026-08-24.** A common-password check exists; a breach corpus does not, and the row's own reasoning for why still holds: 10 GB does not fit one deployable against one PostgreSQL, and the k-anonymity API leaks a hash prefix to a third party. **The trigger has not fired either** — it reads *before this is recommended for anything internet-facing*, and there is no release, no published image and no deployment ([D-19](#), measured: zero git tags, zero GitHub releases). **What makes the residual bearable is that the mechanism is the durable part**: `Graticula:CommonPasswordFile` takes a list of any size, so a deployment that *does* keep a breach corpus points at it and gets exactly what this row wanted, without the product carrying 10 GB for everybody. |
| D-24 | **`layer.is_hosted` and `layer.sharing` are columns nobody reads, and writes still reach both.** Schema 7 derives *hosted* from `data_source.is_datastore` instead; the old column remains, written `false` by `PublishLayerAsync`, ignored by the catalogue. **2026-08-15: the second one was not harmless.** Since migration 11 the serving path reads sharing from the owning *service*, but `PUT /admin/layers/{name}/sharing` was still writing `layer.sharing` — so making a layer private returned 200, changed a column nothing reads, and left the layer readable by anybody. Fixed; found while testing [Q-95](open-questions.md). The lesson is the same one `is_hosted` taught and it cost more this time: **a dead column is a live hazard while a writer can still reach it**, so dropping them is the repayment and keeping them in step is not. **And the reading half is a check as of 2026-08-24** — `DeadColumnsStayDeadTests` fails the build on any read of `l.is_hosted`, `l.sharing`, `l.status` or `l.owner_principal_id` outside the migrations. It does not stop the writer, which is what dropping the columns is for; it stops the next reader, which is how both of this row's incidents actually did their damage. | 2026-08-14 | Dropping a column is a contract migration and moves `minimum_reader_version`, which would stop a version-6 node reading the store mid-upgrade. Expand now, contract later — that is [ADR-016](adr/ADR-016-packaging-deployment-upgrade.md)'s whole shape and skipping the wait is how a rolling upgrade breaks. | When no supported release reads schema 6 | **A column that lies is worse than a column that is missing.** The next person to need *is this hosted* will find `is_hosted`, use it, and get `false` for everything — which is exactly the bug schema 7 was written to fix, reintroduced by someone reading the obvious field. | **CLOSED 2026-08-24 for everything but the drop, which is [D-33](#): no writer reaches any of the three columns now, and the guard covers all of them.** The warning is still written into the schema-7 migration and the catalogue query rather than only here. **This row's own sentence is what was acted on** — *a dead column is a live hazard while a writer can still reach it* — and the reader check added on the 24th could not see a single writer, because a writer spells the column unqualified (`insert into layer (… sharing …)`, `update layer set owner_principal_id`) while the check looks for `l.sharing`. One guard on one half of a behaviour is [D-46](#), inside D-24's own repair. **Two writers stopped, and they were free to stop.** The publish path no longer mentions `owner_principal_id` or `sharing` in its layer insert — the first is nullable, the second defaults to `'private'` — and `TransferAsync` no longer carries the `l` CTE that updated `layer.owner_principal_id`, which was **keeping a dead column in step**, the thing this row says is not the repayment. That CTE's count was never in the transfer's returned total either, so nothing observable changed. **Measured: 351 Postgres integration tests against a live store, all passing** — publishing and transferring both still work without them. ~~**One writer remains and cannot stop yet**: `is_hosted` is `not null` with no default, so omitting it fails the insert.~~ **It stopped the same day.** Migration 34 gives `is_hosted` a default of `false`, the publish path no longer mentions it, and `DeadColumnsStayDeadTests` now names all three columns rather than the two that were free. **An expand, so `minimum_reader_version` does not move**: the column is still there and still holds `false`, and a version-33 reader is unaffected — which is the distinction this row and [D-33](#) both turn on. Nothing in the serving or publishing path writes any of the three now. **And the reasoning that said this needed the drop was wrong.** *The migration that would give it a default is the same one that should drop it* treated the two as one act; they are opposite halves of expand-and-contract, and the expand is available today while the contract waits for a release that does not exist. Measured: 356 Postgres integration tests against a live store, migrated to 34. **The new guard is `No_statement_outside_the_migrations_writes_a_layer_column_that_can_stop_being_written`**, scoped to statements that write the `layer` table because `service`, `folder`, `sharing_group` and `job` each own an `owner_principal_id` legitimately. Falsified by putting the transfer's CTE back: it named the file, the line and the statement. **And its first run failed on a doc comment** containing the words *update layer set sharing* — the sentence warning against the mistake — so comments are stripped first, which is the exemption the reader check next to it already argues for. |
| ~~D-25~~ | ~~**Tile cache TTL is one global number for every layer.**~~ **CLOSED 2026-08-15.** `layer.cache_seconds` (migration 13), settable at publish time and afterwards with `PUT /admin/layers/{name}/cache`. Null means *nobody has said* and takes the server default; **zero is a different answer** — never serve a cached tile — and both the cache and the `Cache-Control` header honour it. The same number governs every cache in the chain: our disk cache expires by it and `max-age` carries it to the browser and any CDN, which is the only way they can agree. For a multi-layer tile service the **shortest** of its layers wins, because one tile can only be as fresh as its most volatile part. No purge on change: freshness does not make a cached byte wrong, which is [ADR-010](adr/ADR-010-caching.md) §5.1's reasoning for sharing applied where it also holds. | 2026-08-15 | — | — | — | CLOSED. |
| D-26 | **A relationship's `composite` flag is stored, reported, and does nothing.** [ADR-013](adr/ADR-013-feature-service-data-model.md) §3 says a composite relationship cascades on delete — by the database where it already declares `ON DELETE CASCADE`, by us in the same transaction where it does not. Neither is implemented. | 2026-08-15 | Relationships shipped with declaration, validation and querying, which is the whole read path; the cascade is a write-path concern that touches `applyEdits` and needs the same-transaction guarantee ADR-013 §3 describes. Shipping the read half beat shipping neither. | Before anybody declares a composite relationship and deletes an origin feature | **A flag that is stored and reported but not honoured is worse than one that is absent.** An administrator sets it, sees it in the layer document, and concludes deleting a parcel removes its owners. It does not, and the orphans are silent — they accumulate, count against nothing, and surface as rows nobody can explain. The endpoint should refuse `composite: true` until it is honoured, and does not. | **CLOSED 2026-08-24, on this row's own stated remedy — narrowed 2026-08-23 when the flag stopped lying, and the cascade is still not built.** This row's own last sentence was already out of date when it was read: `RelationshipEndpoints.DeclareAsync` refuses `composite: true` with 501 and a comment citing this row, and has for some time. Nobody amended the row, which is [D-130](#)'s shape inside a single register and the second one found this way today. **What was actually missing is a test.** A refusal nothing exercises is one somebody removes while tidying, and the way that would be found is the way this row describes it: by orphans, months later. `CompositeRelationshipTests` asserts the 501, asserts the message names both what is missing and what to do instead — `ON DELETE CASCADE` in the database, where PostgreSQL enforces it — and carries a control declaring the same relationship without the flag, because a server that refused every declaration would pass the first assertion and mean nothing. **So the hazard this row is about is closed and the feature is not.** An administrator can no longer set a flag that is reported and not honoured. Declaring a composite relationship is still impossible, and that stays open with the trigger it always had. Named in ADR-013 §6a.  **Why this closes rather than waits for the cascade.** The cost cell is entirely about the flag — *a flag that is stored and reported but not honoured is worse than one that is absent* — and it ends by naming the repair: *the endpoint should refuse `composite: true` until it is honoured, and does not.* **It does.** What is left is an absent feature, and [CLAUDE.md §62](../CLAUDE.md) is explicit that debt is a compromise with a trigger; an unbuilt capability with nothing pretending otherwise is not one. It is recorded in [ADR-013](adr/ADR-013-feature-service-data-model.md) §6a, which is where absent features live, and that row now says so rather than saying the flag is ignored. |
| D-27 | **Twelve of twenty-two ADRs still describe a three-database product.** [v1-scope](v1-scope.md) §3a cut Oracle and SQL Server on 2026-08-13; the ADRs carry **70 mentions** of them and **two** mention the cut. ADR-002 is the worst: fourteen mentions, and an amendment still reading *"Oracle Spatial and SQL Server Spatial are first-class"*. | 2026-08-13 | The cut was taken as a scope decision under time pressure and the propagation was deferred — recorded then as review findings A2, A5, A6, P11 and P14, and counted for the first time by [contradiction sweep 2](reviews/contradiction-sweep-2.md). | Before anybody implements from an ADR rather than from the code | **Somebody building from these documents builds abstraction nobody needs** — ADR-008's per-dialect pushdown table, ADR-007's per-provider connection budget, ADR-010's per-engine change detection. The abstraction is exactly what the cut existed to remove, so the stale text does not merely mislead, it undoes the decision. And a reader who starts at ADR-002 is told the opposite of the scope. | **RESOLVED 2026-08-18, by the decision this row was waiting for.** The owner settled what the cut *means*: *"Şimdilik postgis ile gideceğiz. Sonra diğer db'ler eklenecek. V1'de sadece Postgis olarak kalabiliriz."* — a **deferral, not a removal** — which turns *"deciding which is which per paragraph"* from a judgement into a default: **deferred unless the paragraph claims to be current.** [v1-scope](v1-scope.md) §3a carries the amendment and is the only place that says what the deferral means; **all thirteen ADRs that reason about several engines now open with the same scope note pointing at it** (it was two of twenty-two that mentioned the cut at all). Six sentences that claimed the present tense are corrected individually, because a reader believes a specific sentence over a general disclaimer — the worst was ADR-002's *"Oracle Spatial and SQL Server Spatial **remain** first-class providers"*, on the first page a reader reaches. **And the deferral cost something this row had not counted:** v1-scope §3a argued ADR-008 condition 1a *dissolved* — the second-dialect compiler as a forcing function against PostGIS-shaped assumptions — on the grounds that with one dialect there is nothing to force. That argument required the cut to be permanent, so the forcing function was switched off while the drift it guards against continued. ADR-008 §4a-i is amended accordingly: `ParsedWhere` is the first instance, the architecture test that fails the build on a **second** SQL-shaped member is now the only thing watching, and **it was falsified rather than trusted** — a second `ParsedWhere` member added to `FeatureQuery` turned the build red naming it, and the member was removed. |
| D-39 | **`/admin/services/{name}` means two different things.** `…/sharing` addresses a *system* service (the geometry server); `…/groups` and `…/style` address a *published* one. A published service named `Geometry` would make a sharing change land on the system service instead. Latent today because no published service has that name. **REPAID 2026-08-24, and not by the rename.** The trigger cell called the fix a route rename; a rename moves eighteen console call sites and thirteen test ones to close a collision with a smaller cause — the lookup ignored a parameter it already had. Every one of these routes carries `?folder=`, because the published services they share the path with need it, and a system service lives in a folder: `Utilities/Geometry`. `SystemServiceAsync` compares them, so `hosted/Geometry` and a root-level `Geometry` resolve to the published service and `Utilities/Geometry` to the system one. **`start` and `stop` ask before they write**, because `SetStatusAsync` matches on the name alone and would otherwise have stopped the geometry server for a caller who meant something else, and told them it worked. The console names the folder on all four routes. `OneAddressOneServiceTests` holds four cases, the first of which is that the system service still answers on its own address — a refusal test that passes because nothing works asserts nothing. | 2026-08-24, found and repaid the same day | Not chosen. The two families grew from opposite ends — a system service is configured and a published one is created — and they met on one path prefix without either author seeing the other. Latent, because no published service has ever been named after a system one. | Before a published service can be named after a system one — or sooner, since the fix is a route rename and the cost only grows as clients bind to these paths | **A sharing change aimed at somebody's layer landing on the geometry server.** The damage is not that the request fails: it succeeds, on the wrong object, and the operator sees a 200. It also grows with time rather than staying still, because every client that binds to one of these paths makes the rename that would have fixed it more expensive. | ~~open~~ repaid 2026-08-24 |
| D-29 | **There is no CI, so nothing runs the checks a published promise depends on.** ~~**PAID 2026-08-15.**~~ **REOPENED 2026-08-18: the files exist and have never run.** Nine runs, every one a 0-second `startup_failure` — [D-63](#) has the measurement and the likely cause. This row was closed on the existence of two workflow files, which is the same mistake as reading behaviour out of code: what was verified was that the checks were *written*. [.github/workflows/ci.yml](../.github/workflows/ci.yml) now builds, audits dependencies, and runs **all eight suites** — the five that need nothing, plus the two that need PostGIS, against a `postgis/postgis:16-3.4` service container seeded by [tools/ci-corpus.sql](../tools/ci-corpus.sql). **The conformance suite runs too, against a server the workflow builds from nothing**: create the schema, migrate, start, take the single-use setup token out of the log, and seed with [tools/seed-conformance.py](../tools/seed-conformance.py), which publishes every fixture through the same HTTP API a client uses rather than writing to the database. **Proved locally against an empty database before it was written into the workflow**, which is how four of the findings below were found at all. **Six things the setup found, and none of them by review.** **(1)** A fresh install fails with a raw Postgres error — D-36. **(2)** `addFeatures` answers 200 with a per-feature results array, and a seed that trusted the status code inserted nothing while reporting success; eighty conformance tests then failed with things like “no tile with any content was found”. **(3)** Two multi-layer tests walked every entry in a service document and demanded a `geometryType` from each, which crashes on a group layer — asserted two tests below as correct behaviour. They passed only because the fixture they were written against had no groups. **(4)** Two discovery suites read `/rest/services` and took the first entry, so they failed on any server whose services all live in a folder — which is every server published through the hosting API, because hosted layers always land in `hosted`. They follow folders now, as a client does. **(5)** A layer's extent is cached with its description, so features that had just landed were invisible to the two edit tests that place a feature at the centre of it — they failed once and passed on the next run, which is the worst way for a race to present; the seed calls `POST /admin/layers/{name}/refresh` and waits for the extent to appear. **(6)** The corpus discovery took the alphabetically first geometry table, so adding the CI corpus to a developer database silently switched both oracle suites from real cadastral polygons to sixty generated ones — they now take the richest table by `reltuples`. And the obvious staleness check on the status page, a regenerate-and-diff, would have failed on every run because the page stamps its own generation time; [tools/registers-check.py](../tools/registers-check.py) compares the counts instead. Original text: No workflow of any kind: every test runs because somebody remembered. [ADR-025](adr/ADR-025-governance-and-maintenance.md) now commits in writing to fixing reported vulnerabilities in 90 days, and `dotnet list package --vulnerable` is clean today with nothing to report it going dirty. **The tension worth stating:** this project's suites *fail rather than skip* when their subject is absent, so CI cannot just run `dotnet test` — it needs a PostGIS service container and a running server, or it runs a subset and says so. | 2026-08-15, and reopened 2026-08-18 when the runs were read for the first time | Not chosen. The row was closed on the existence of two workflow files, which is the same mistake as reading behaviour out of code: what was verified is that the checks were *written*. Nothing in this repository read the Actions list, and a `startup_failure` shows there as a *completed* run with a red mark. | Before the repository is made public — the same trigger as ADR-025's conditions, because that is when the promise starts | **A promise nobody runs is worse than one nobody made.** The workflows build, audit dependencies and run eight suites, and they have never executed a step — so every claim resting on them is currently false, which [D-63](#) counts as four. The trigger is the moment the repository becomes public, because that is when the promise starts being made to somebody. | **REOPENED 2026-08-18.** Not by regression — nothing changed — but because it was never true. The workflows are the right workflows; nothing runs them. |
| ~~D-28~~ | ~~**Nothing structurally prevents the next service without a layer from shipping ungoverned.**~~ **CLOSED 2026-08-15.** Every route under `/rest/services` now carries a `SharingGoverned` marker naming what governs it, `GET /admin/routes` lists them with an `ungoverned` count, and a conformance test fails when that count is not zero. **Verified by breaking it**: an unmarked probe route was added, the test failed and named it, and the probe was removed. The marker enforces nothing by itself — enforcement is still `ServiceLookup` and the geometry group's filter — what it does is make the property enumerable, which is what the geometry hole needed and did not have. | 2026-08-15 | — | — | — | CLOSED. [ADR-018](adr/ADR-018-authorization-and-roles.md) condition 5 discharged. |
| D-33 | **`layer.sharing`, `layer.status` and `layer.owner_principal_id` still exist and mean nothing.** Migration 11 moved sharing, status and ownership to the `service` table and left the layer's columns in place, carrying whatever they held at the moment of the migration. | 2026-08-15 | Expand-and-contract ([ADR-016](adr/ADR-016-packaging-deployment-upgrade.md) §5): the expand half must not break a reader running the previous build, so the columns stay until a later release drops them. | The release after the one that ships migration 11 | **This file already contains the cautionary tale.** `is_hosted` stayed writable after its meaning moved, drifted to false on every insert, and silently disabled every vector tile service — nobody noticed because nothing errored. These three columns are the same shape of hazard aimed at the sharing model, which is a worse place for it. The defence today is that the catalogue's select list reads `s.sharing` and never `l.sharing`, so a stale value cannot be believed — a defence made of one SQL string and a comment. The contract migration is what makes it structural. **The comment is a check as of 2026-08-24.** `DeadColumnsStayDeadTests` walks every `.cs` file under `src`, skips the migrations — which are the history and have to name the columns they copied out of — skips comments, and fails on any read of `l.sharing`, `l.status`, `l.owner_principal_id` or `l.is_hosted`. Qualified with `l.`, because the service carries the same four names and `s.sharing` is the right answer. **Verified by adding `l.sharing` back to the catalogue's own select**: the test named the file, the line and the statement. A second test asserts it is reading the catalogue at all, because a search that finds nothing because it is searching nowhere passes silently. **This is not the contract migration and does not pretend to be** — the columns are still there, ~~and still written at publish, which the rollback window requires~~ **and as of 2026-08-24 none of the three is written at publish.** [D-24](#)'s round stopped the two that could stop for free: `layer.sharing` defaults to `'private'` and `layer.owner_principal_id` is nullable, so the publish insert no longer mentions either, and `TransferAsync` no longer keeps the owner column in step. `is_hosted` was `not null` with no default; **migration 34 gives it one**, so the publish path stopped mentioning it too. What is left of this row is the drop itself, and that is the contract half — it waits for the release after the one that ships migration 11, and there are no releases. **The rollback window does not require writing them** — it requires that they still *exist*, which they do; a previous build reading a stale value was the hazard, not a missing one. What changed is that the next reader fails the build instead of shipping. | OPEN, and named in [ADR-013](adr/ADR-013-feature-service-data-model.md) §4g. |
| D-30 | ~~**The feature query path has never been measured.**~~ **PARTLY PAID 2026-08-16 — [benchmarks/feature-query](../benchmarks/feature-query/RESULTS.md), run 5.** The path is instrumented in process and decomposes into lookup, prepare, driver, decode and serialise; nothing is timed unless the `query` logger is enabled for Debug, so it costs a level check in production. **Two findings.** The per-request catalogue read is **1.8 ms flat on every query** — 70% of a one-feature request, and 2.6× the cost of the data query it precedes. That read is deliberate (D-17: it carries the sharing scope and the started/stopped status) and what was missing was its price, which is now a number rather than a feeling. At a thousand features **serialisation is 43% and the driver is 16%** — so the cost is in this repository's own code and not in the database, which is the question F1 could not answer from outside. Decode was smaller than A-037 predicted for this shape of data: 9%. **Concurrency answered 2026-08-16 (§3b).** A C# generator replaced the Python one, speaks TLS, and reads the server's own GC and CPU counters from `/admin/health`. The feature query path scales **4.75× from concurrency 1 to 8** (198 → 942 req/s) and is **not what stops scaling after that** — the do-nothing control saturates in the same place. **It is not allocation-bound:** GC pause is 0.2% at concurrency 1 and never exceeds 2.5%, against the 80.9% a tile round measured, and allocation is flat at 142–164 kB per request across all levels. **A-037 does not transfer to this path**, and that transfer was the assumption D-30 was opened on. Not CPU-bound either: 3.6 of 16 cores at the peak. **What remains unseparated** is which of three shared costs is the ceiling — TLS, the per-request authentication, or the generator — which needs a second machine or a server with authentication off. **And the fifth wrong harness:** the server appeared to saturate at 130 req/s until that turned out to be .NET's console logger blocking the request thread on a full queue, because stdout had been redirected to a file to read the phase lines back. Without redirection the same path holds 1.15 ms flat. One command from being written up as a server defect. Original text: **The feature query path has never been measured.** Four benchmark rounds covered tiles and overlay; the word *query* appears in neither results document. It runs the same WKB-into-an-object-graph mechanism those rounds found to be allocation-bound at 18% CPU, and it is the most-used surface in the product. A black-box probe ([performance-gate-1.md](reviews/performance-gate-1.md) F1) shows throughput plateauing at 5–7× for 24× concurrency and cannot say why — from outside, an allocation ceiling, a pool limit and a contended host look identical. Needs the in-process counters the tile path already had. | 2026-08-13, and partly paid 2026-08-16 | Not chosen. Four benchmark rounds covered tiles and overlay; the word *query* appeared in neither results document, and the path was assumed to behave like the tile path because it runs the same WKB machinery. That assumption is A-037 and the measurement retired it. | The concurrency half is still what ADR-007 condition 3's connection budget needs, and they still need the same harness | **A performance claim about the product's most-used surface, made from a measurement of a different surface.** The phase breakdown answered that and found A-037 does not transfer — the query path is not allocation-bound. What the remaining half costs is narrower and still real: [ADR-007](adr/ADR-007-service-runtime.md) condition 3's connection budget is a number chosen against a curve nobody has measured past the point where the client became the bottleneck, so the budget rests on a ceiling that may be the test harness rather than the server. | **PARTLY PAID, and the residual is blocked rather than unstarted — 2026-08-24.** The phase breakdown exists; ~~the load generator does not~~ **the load generator exists and is on the wrong machine**, which is a different problem and is [D-145](#). The row's own body already records what it bought — 4.75x from concurrency 1 to 8, GC pause never above 2.5%, allocation flat, 3.6 of 16 cores at the peak, and A-037 shown not to transfer to this path — and what it could not separate: which of TLS, the per-request authentication or the generator is the ceiling. **All three are costs of running the client beside the server**, so the missing instrument is a second machine, which is now written down as [Q-140](open-questions.md). **Neither this row nor D-145 said they were blocked on the same input**, and that is the thing this pass added rather than a measurement. |
| D-34 | **A layer or group cannot be moved, re-parented, renamed or removed without unpublishing it.** Publishing puts a layer in a service and under a group; nothing takes it out again, moves it to another group, or reorders it. ~~Groups cannot be deleted at all.~~ **Measured 2026-08-24 and that half is wrong now**: `POST /admin/services/{name}/groups` creates a group layer and `DELETE /admin/services/{name}/groups/{index}` removes one, refusing while it still holds children — held by `PostgresAdminCatalogTests.A_group_is_refused_while_it_has_children_and_removed_once_it_has_none`. | 2026-08-15 | The service and group model shipped with the read path and the create path, which is what makes multi-layer services usable at all. Rearranging one has its own correctness questions — indices are referenced by saved web maps and must never be reused, and re-parenting is the one operation that could introduce a cycle, which is currently impossible by construction ([ADR-013](adr/ADR-013-feature-service-data-model.md) §4g-i) — and shipping the half that works beat shipping neither. | The first time somebody puts a layer in the wrong service or the wrong group | ~~**The workaround is destructive and will not look it.** Unpublishing a hosted layer may drop its table; an administrator correcting a mistake in the service or group name would reasonably expect to be moving a pointer. ~~Until there is a move operation, the admin API should say that unpublishing is the only route and what it costs, and it does not.~~ **It does, from 2026-08-24**: the unpublish response carries `moving`, which says there is no move, rename or reorder, that correcting a placement means publishing again, and that the layer will be given **the next free index rather than this one** — because an index is never reused (ADR-013 §4), so a client holding the old URL will not follow it. Said at the moment an operator arrives there, which is the last moment anything can say it.~~ **Measured 2026-08-24 and both halves are wrong now.** `UnpublishLayerAsync` is `delete from layer where id = @id` and touches no table; dropping one needs `DELETE /admin/services/{name}?drop=true`, which is explicit, opt-in, and refuses without the flag in a sentence naming what the flag would do. And the unpublish response already says what it costs — *"The registration was removed. The table in the data source was not touched"* — under a comment saying it is said out loud because *delete* on somebody else's database could reasonably be feared to mean more. **So the workaround is safe and looks it.** What is left of this row is the plain absence, and it is smaller than it was: there is no move, no rename and no reorder — **group deletion exists** — so correcting a placement still means unpublish and republish, which is cheap, manual, and changes the layer's index. **The index is the part that is not merely inconvenient**: [ADR-013](adr/ADR-013-feature-service-data-model.md) §4 says an index is referenced by saved web maps and must never be reused, so republishing to fix a placement moves the address a client already stored. **Re-parenting in particular needs a cycle guard before it exists** — today a cycle cannot be written because a parent must pre-exist and nothing moves, and adding the operation removes that argument without replacing it. | **CLOSED 2026-08-24: both halves of this row's own remedy are done, and what is left is an absent feature rather than a compromise.** The cost cell claimed a destructive workaround that does not exist — `UnpublishLayerAsync` touches no table, and dropping one needs an explicit `drop=true` on the service — and the response has always said so. **The other half was real and is now paid**: the response carries `moving`, naming the absence and its price, **the price being the index**. ADR-013 §4 says an index is never reused, so republishing does not restore the address a client stored; that is the part an operator could not have guessed and it is said before the republish rather than after. **Why this closes.** [CLAUDE.md §62](../CLAUDE.md): debt is a compromise with a trigger. The compromise here was *the API does not say* — that is discharged. Move, rename and reorder are capabilities nobody has built, with nothing pretending they exist, which is the same reading that closed [D-26](#) the same day. Group deletion, which this row said was impossible, already exists and is tested. |
| D-31 | **Overlay under concurrency is still unmeasured.** Every figure in [benchmarks/geometry-overlay](../benchmarks/geometry-overlay/RESULTS.md) is a single request on an idle machine, and the pool that now bounds overlay has never been measured with both its workers busy. | 2026-08-15 | Q-97's answer bounds the worst case by construction — two workers, 1 GB and ten seconds each — which makes the unmeasured case much smaller than it was. Smaller is not measured. | Before the overlay worker count is raised above two, or before anyone claims a throughput figure for overlay | **The bound is arithmetic, and arithmetic about allocation has been wrong here before.** A-037 measured an 80.9% GC pause at 18% CPU, and A-042 was an argument about asymptotics that measurement overturned. "Two workers at 1 GB is 2 GB" assumes the ceiling is respected under real concurrent load, that a killed worker's memory is returned promptly, and that process launches under contention stay inside the start-up allowance. Each is likely and none is measured. | **CLOSED 2026-08-24. Measured with the pool busy, and the arithmetic held.** [benchmarks/geometry-overlay/CONCURRENCY.md](../benchmarks/geometry-overlay/CONCURRENCY.md). **The load:** `intersect` of two 300,000-vertex rings, 2.1 s alone, at 1, 2, 4, 8, 12 and 16 concurrent. Medians 2.1, 2.7, 4.4, 6.5, 9.7 and 13.0 s; worst 17.0 s at sixteen. Every one answered 200, and the server's own request log agrees with the client to within 14 ms, so the queueing is inside the server rather than on the wire. Two workers running in parallel and a queue doing nothing clever: sixteen requests of 2.1 s through two workers is 16.8 s of work and the last answered at 17.0. **The ceiling, which is the doubt the row leads with:** exactly two worker processes at every concurrency, peaking at **754 MB and 618 MB** with eight 300,000-vertex overlays in flight. Neither reached the 1 GB it is allowed. **A killed worker:** one holding 644 MB was killed outright. Gone within a second. **Replaced lazily rather than eagerly** — the pool ran on one until concurrency needed a second, which the row did not predict and is the better behaviour. The next request cost 2,361 ms against a warm 2,118, so **a launch under contention is about 240 ms**, and the burst after the kill was not slower than the burst before it. **And the queue wait needed a different shape of work to reach, which is a finding in itself.** The intersect load never hit the ten seconds, because **the wait bounds queueing for a worker and not the request**: a 300,000-vertex operand is a 25 MB body, so sixteen of them arrive staggered and a client can watch 17 seconds pass without ever waiting ten for a worker. Measured instead with `union` of 4,000 overlapping rings — 5.4 s of work in a 7.5 MB body — four are all served and **six produce two 503s at 10.8 s**, carrying the pool's own sentence. The bound is enforced and it says what it is. **One thing turned up that this row did not ask about**, and it is [D-148](#): a request body over Kestrel's 30 MB default is closed rather than refused. Named in [ADR-022](adr/ADR-022-geometry-server.md) §9, which can now cite a measurement instead of an intention. |
| D-35 | **Planar area was computed by a shoelace sum with no local origin, and it shipped that way for a day.** In EPSG:3857 the coordinates are around 3.2 by 5.0 million, so each product in the sum is about 1.6×10¹³ and its rounding error near 0.003 — while the answer for a parcel is a few hundred. **Fixed 2026-08-15** by subtracting the first vertex, which is what PostGIS's `ptarray_signed_area` does and why `ST_Area` agreed with itself to eleven digits where we disagreed at five. **Measured before and after:** worst relative disagreement across forty real polygons was 1.58×10⁻⁵ and is now under 10⁻⁸. **The absolute error did not depend on the feature's size**, so a 2 m² utility box was out by about 0.1 per cent while a hectare was out by nothing visible. **How it was found is the part worth keeping:** not by review and not by a test of `areasAndLengths`, which had none — a cut's pieces failed to add up to their target, and the pieces were innocent, because both sides of that sum were this function. `Area_matches_PostGIS_on_real_polygons` now covers it and fails by three orders of magnitude when the fix is reverted. | 2026-08-15 | — | — | **The rest of the tree was swept the same day and there were two more.** `ShapefileReader.SignedArea` had the identical plain shoelace, used to rank rings by size when deciding which contains which — fixed the same way; the consequence there was small, because one part in a hundred thousand does not reorder rings that differ by orders of magnitude, and it is corrected because the correction is free and because leaving one known-wrong shoelace in the tree invites the next one. `LinearRing.SignedArea2` uses the trapezoid form, which subtracts the large x values before multiplying and is therefore accurate to about 10⁻¹¹ — far more than the sign it exists for needs, and less than an area deserves; it is now documented as an orientation test that must not be used to measure. `Length` subtracts before multiplying and has no equivalent cancellation. | CLOSED, but **`areasAndLengths` shipped 2026-08-14 with no oracle test at all** — that is the gap that let this live, and it is worth remembering that three of the four first operations still have none. |
| D-36 | ~~**Nothing in the repository says how to install against an empty database, and the first command fails with a raw Postgres error.**~~ **CLOSED 2026-08-15, partly.** Pointed at a brand-new store, `migrate --apply` printed the whole plan and then threw `Npgsql.PostgresException 3F000: no schema has been selected to create in` — Postgres saying that `SearchPath` names a schema that does not exist, which on a new database is the normal state rather than a fault. **The migrator still does not create it, and that is deliberate:** creating a schema is a privileged act, and doing it silently would turn a typo in a connection string into a second, empty installation instead of an error. What changed is that it now says `CREATE SCHEMA gisserver;` and exits 1. **Found by making CI build a server from nothing, which was the first time anything in this project had installed the product against an empty database** — every run before that inherited a schema somebody had made by hand a week earlier. | 2026-08-15 | — | — | *(The documentation half was closed the same day: [deployment.md](deployment.md) §0 now carries the whole sequence — database, schema, the two required settings, the explicit migration, and the one-time setup token — and every step of it is exercised by the conformance job on every push, so drift turns that job red rather than turning this page quietly wrong.)* | CLOSED. |
| D-37 | ~~**A test that had been written off as flaky twice was not measuring its own claim.**~~ **CLOSED 2026-08-16.** `A_worker_is_reused_rather_than_launched_per_request` counted processes named `Graticula.Overlay.Worker` **across the whole machine** before and after five overlays. That also counts the pools other test classes are running in parallel and any server the developer happens to have up, so it failed intermittently and was dismissed as flaky twice in this project's history without anybody reading it. It now asserts the pool's own launch counter, which is what the sentence in its name actually claims. **Worth keeping as a pattern rather than as an incident:** an intermittent failure is a defect in something, and 'flaky' is a description rather than a diagnosis — this one had been true for as long as the test had existed. | 2026-08-16 | — | — | It surfaced the day after CI was built, which is the point: a flaky test in CI is a test that teaches people to ignore CI. | CLOSED. |
| D-38 | ~~**The service lookup's unique index disagreed with the query that needed it, in meaning as well as in form.**~~ **CLOSED 2026-08-16, migration 15.** Migration 11 created `unique (coalesce(folder, ''), lower(name))` — case-insensitive on the name and case-*sensitive* on the folder — while every read asks `coalesce(lower(folder), '') = coalesce(lower(@folder), '')`. Two consequences. **The correctness one:** `Hosted/parcels` and `hosted/parcels` could both exist and the lookup matched both, returning whichever row came first — and since the two can carry different sharing scopes, the same URL could resolve to the public service or the private one depending on row order. Folders are taken from the administrator's request rather than generated, so the pair needed no trick; **reproduced against a real Postgres before the fix was written.** **The performance one:** an expression index is only an index for the expression it holds, so the predicate could not use it and the lookup sequentially scanned `service` on the most-used path in the product — 85 µs at 33 services, linear at the stated target of 1,000. **How it was found:** not by review. The D-30 instrumentation put the per-request catalogue read at 1.8 ms, and reading the query to find out why turned up the mismatch. | 2026-08-16 | — | — | The migration refuses rather than half-applies: it names any colliding pair and stops, because two services that were legal yesterday and ambiguous today need an administrator to say which survives. | CLOSED. Four tests, two of which fail when the migration is removed from the registry — the other two assert behaviour migration 11 already had, and correctly stay green. |
| D-32 | ~~**A transformation PROJ could not do accurately is reported as though it were accurate.**~~ **PARTLY PAID 2026-08-16 on the GeometryServer surface.** The accuracy is still unreachable — `ST_Transform` does not name its pipeline and PROJ's operation database is not queryable from SQL (Q-100) — but **whether a datum was crossed is readable from the two references' own WKT**, and that is the line between a transformation that is exact by construction and one that can be metres out. `project` now answers with `datumShift` and, when it is true, a caution naming both datums and saying in plain words that PROJ falls back to a ballpark without failing. 4326 to 3857 is not flagged, which is what keeps the flag worth reading. An unreadable reference is reported as unknown rather than as safe. **Still open on two surfaces:** FeatureServer's `outSR` transforms in SQL inside `PostGisFeatureSource` and never touches the projector, and the tile path does the same in `PostGisTileSource` — a protobuf tile also has nowhere to carry a caution, so for tiles it has to go on the service document instead, which is a different change. Original text: **A transformation PROJ could not do accurately is reported as though it were accurate.** Layers now keep their own reference and the tile path transforms per request (Q-96). PROJ picks the pipeline; when the shift grids for the accurate path are absent it falls back to a ballpark transformation — **without failing** — and the tile is drawn metres from where the data is. | 2026-08-15 | Q-96's third option was *transform anything and report the accuracy PROJ actually used*. The transform shipped; the reporting did not, because `ST_Transform` does not say which pipeline it chose and getting that out means `ST_TransformPipeline` or a PROJ query per layer, which is a design rather than a line. | Before anybody publishes cadastral or survey data in a national grid and reads a tile as authoritative | **Metres, silently, on exactly the data where metres are legally significant.** 4326 to 3857 is a closed formula and safe; EPSG:5254 or a British/Dutch national grid to WGS 84 is a datum change, and the grids are a separate download PROJ does not ship. The failure has no error, no log line and no visual signature — the map looks right and is in the wrong place. [geometry-crs-policy](geometry-crs-policy.md) §3 already asks for a pinned pipeline and this is the same gap on a second surface. | **PARTLY PAID, and the residual is blocked on a decision rather than on work — 2026-08-24.** The FeatureServer's `outSR` and the tile path both still have the identical exposure and the same absence of reporting. **The mechanism is not what is missing**: `PostGisProjector` already reads both references' WKT and decides whether a datum is crossed, and `project` answers with it. What is missing is somewhere to put it — a FeatureServer `query` response has no field for a caution and adding one is a non-standard member on the surface whose whole purpose is that an unmodified ArcGIS client keeps working, and a protobuf tile has nowhere at all. That is [Q-141](open-questions.md), which names three shapes and takes none. **The trigger has not fired either** — nobody has published cadastral or survey data in a national grid here — so the row is neither overdue nor idle: it is waiting on a compatibility decision about the product's leading surface. |
| D-54 | ~~**Unpublishing the last layer still leaves its service behind.**~~ **CLOSED 2026-08-18 by the second of the two routes this row named.** D-48 asked for two things and this is the one deliberately not done: `DELETE /admin/layers/{name}` removes the layer and leaves the service that publishing created implicitly, so an estate still accumulates empty services — they are now visible and removable, but in a second act. | 2026-08-17, when D-48's other half closed | **Chosen, and the argument is that the alternative guesses.** A service created implicitly by a publish and a service created deliberately by `POST /admin/featureservices` are the same row: nothing records which. So auto-removal would either delete a container somebody made on purpose, or need a new column to tell them apart — and a column that exists to support a convenience is how a schema grows facts nobody reads (D-24 is the last one of those). | **When the catalogue can say who created a service.** Either a flag written at publish time, which is the honest version of the convenience, or an operator-facing sweep — *remove the 5 empty services* — which needs no new fact and no guess. The second is cheaper and is probably right. | An operator who publishes and unpublishes while learning the product leaves a trail of empty services, and each one is advertised in the services directory as a FeatureServer with no layers. It is now a tidy-up rather than a mystery, which is the difference D-48's closure made. | **CLOSED by the operator-facing sweep**, which is the route this row called cheaper and probably right — and it is, because it needs no new fact and guesses about nothing. `GET /admin/featureservices/empty` names them; `POST /admin/featureservices/sweep` removes them in one `delete … returning`, so a layer published during a gap between deciding and acting cannot be caught by a sweep that had already made up its mind. **A service holding a group layer is not empty**, which is the case a layer count gets wrong: a group is a container the operator made, in a different table. **And the geometry service cannot be swept for a structural reason rather than a remembered one** — it lives in `system_service`, and this deletes from `service`. **The names come back, not a count**, because the whole risk of this button is being able to answer *which* afterwards; the audit record and the toast both carry them. **In the console it is on Operations, and Remove is disabled until Look has run** — a person cannot sweep an estate they have not looked at, and a destructive verb that is pressable and does nothing is how people learn a button is harmless. Measured on the running server: two containers created on purpose, both listed, both removed, twelve layers and the geometry service untouched, and a second sweep finding nothing. Held by a database test for the keeping half and a browser test for the gate, the latter falsified by removing the `disabled` attribute. |
| D-55 | **The data-source probe read PostGIS's `geometry_columns`, which any constraint in the database can break — and one did.** Measured 2026-08-17: registering a data source and testing a connection both answered `503` against a database where nothing of ours was wrong. `geometry_columns` is a compatibility view whose definition scans **every row of `pg_constraint` in the database**, looking for pre-2.0 declarations like `CHECK (srid(geom) = 4326)` and parsing the number out with `split_part(consrc, ' = ', 2)::integer`. A third-party schema in the same database carried `CHECK ((st_srid(geometry) = srid))` — comparing against a *column* named `srid` — so the parse produced the string `srid`, and **every read of the view in that database failed** with `22P02: invalid input syntax for type integer: "srid"`. | Pre-existing; the probe has read that view since it was written | Reasonable-looking: `geometry_columns` is the documented way to enumerate geometry columns, it is privilege-filtered, and it is what every PostGIS tutorial uses. What was not considered is that it is *derived from other people's constraints*. | **Repaid 2026-08-17**, because the trigger had already fired. The probe now reads `pg_attribute` and the geometry typmod directly — the same facts the view derives, minus the constraint-parsing joins — with the same `has_table_privilege` filter, so nothing is disclosed that was not before. Measured: `503` became `outcome: Usable, tables: 75, canPublish: True` against the same database. | An operator cannot register their own database and is told the *server's* store is unreachable, which sends them to check the wrong thing. **And the failure is more likely the more real the deployment is:** a registered source is somebody else's database, with their constraints and their other products in it. | **CLOSED, and the lesson is wider than the fix.** What remains recorded rather than repaired: `PostGisFeatureSource` and the tile path read table shapes from `information_schema`, which is a view over the catalogue with no third-party input, so the same class does not apply there — checked rather than assumed. |
| D-56 | ~~**A deployment has exactly one user, for ever, and that makes the second surface unusable and untestable.**~~ **CLOSED 2026-08-17.** The first-run setup creates the administrator; after that there is **no endpoint that creates a member, assigns a role, or issues a credential** — `/rest/auth` has login, logout and password, and nothing else. `admin:manageMembers` and `admin:manageRoles` exist as privileges with nothing behind them. Found 2026-08-17 while trying to discharge [ADR-034](adr/ADR-034-server-and-studio.md) condition 1, which asks for a test that signs in *without* `admin:manageServer` and finds no Server surface: there is no way to create such a reader. | Pre-existing; visible since ADR-015 shipped local accounts, and named here because ADR-034 turned it from an absence into a blocker | Reasonable while the only user was the person running the server — D-10 records the deliberate order (local accounts first, federation later), and one administrator is enough to administer. It stops being reasonable the moment a surface exists **for somebody else**. | **Now**, and the trigger has already fired twice over: ADR-034 built Studio for a publisher who cannot be created, and its condition 1 cannot be discharged without one. Concretely: `POST /admin/members` with a role, and a way to set that member's first password. Nothing about federation is needed for it — this is the local identity store ADR-015 §5 already calls first-class. | **Studio is a surface with no possible occupant.** Every claim about what a publisher sees is therefore an argument rather than a measurement, including the one this session made about a console that refuses four times — reasoned from the code, not observed.
