And the privilege gate, which is the whole of ADR-034 §5b, is unexercised. | **CLOSED, and the first thing the second account did was falsify a claim I had made about the gate.** `IMemberDirectory` with `POST /admin/members` (name, role, user type, first password, in one transaction), role and password changes, and disable/enable; a Members screen in Server; seven tests. **Refusals, because they are where this is unrecoverable:** a taken name is 409 and leaves nothing half-made, an unknown role or type is 400 naming the set, and the **last administrator can be neither demoted nor disabled** — that one has no way back except SQL against the platform store. **There is no delete**, and the listing's `ownsServices` column is the reason on the row: a member owns content, and removing the row would orphan every service naming them while the sharing evaluator still reads that column. **The owner corrected the password half the same day, and the correction is now [ADR-015 §6b](adr/ADR-015-authentication.md):** the first version had an administrator type the first password and admitted in its own note that *"this one is known to whoever typed it here"* — a hazard described rather than removed. Now the **server** issues it, returns it once, and marks it dirty; a middleware refuses everything except the replacement until its owner sets their own. **And the measurement:** a `publisher`/`creator` account signs in, holds seven privileges and neither `admin:manageServer` nor `content:registerDataStore`, is refused by every `/admin` route it touched (403 × 8, with `/admin/health` 200 because it is deliberately anonymous and redacted — D-18), reads `/content/layers` (0 owned, 12 shared), and asking for `/server/` in a browser lands in Studio. **What that browser check found immediately is the value of the whole exercise** — the Server/Studio switch was *visible* to them, because `[hidden]` loses to `#surfaces { display: flex }`. That is [D-46](#) instance 9, and the claim it falsified was one I had written the same day from reading the code. |
| D-57 | ~~**Stopping a service did nothing, and said it had.**~~ **CLOSED 2026-08-17, the day it was found.** Migration 11 moved status and sharing onto the service; the read paths moved with it and the *status setter did not*. So `POST /admin/layers/{name}/stop` answered `200` with *"Requests for this service now answer 503"*, wrote `layer.status` — a column nothing reads — and the layer kept serving. **Measured before the fix:** after stopping `tr_kara`, the layer document answered `200`, a count query returned 2 rows, `/admin/layers` said *stopped* and `/admin/featureservices` said *started* about the same service. | Since migration 11, 2026-08-15 | Nothing. It is the `l.sharing` defect a second time — that one was found and repaired on 2026-08-15 and **its record is the paragraph directly above the status setter in the same file**. Two facts moved in one migration, one setter was fixed, and the fix's own comment did not prompt anybody to look at its neighbour. | — closed | — closed | **CLOSED.** The setter writes `service.status` addressed by a layer name, the same shape as sharing; `/admin/layers` reads `s.sharing` and `s.status` so the two listings cannot disagree. Verified end to end: stop → the layer document answers **503** with the operator-facing sentence, start → **200** again. **Two tests, and the shape of them is the lesson:** one asserts through the *serving catalogue* rather than the setter's own answer — the setter reported the transition correctly throughout, so any test of its return value would have passed — and the other asserts the two listings agree, since disagreement is what made it visible. What remains: `layer.status` and `layer.sharing` are still there and still read by nothing, which is [D-24](#)'s standing complaint about `is_hosted`. Three dead columns now, one of which cost this. |
| D-58 | **The service list's preview is a sample the browser draws, not a picture of the layer, and a dense layer therefore looks sparse.** The owner asked for the thumbnails their reference has — *"bir de thumbnailler var. girmeden görebiliyoruz."* — and theirs are rendered map images. This server has no renderer: [ADR-004](adr/ADR-004-rendering-engine.md) is `DEFERRED` and [v1-scope](v1-scope.md) puts every map-image protocol out of scope. So the console queries up to **800 features** of the service's first layer at a `maxAllowableOffset` matched to the box and paints them onto a canvas. For `tr_kara` (2 features) and `tr_yer` (1,421 points) that is the layer. For `tr_yol` it is **800 of 46,041 — 1.7%** — and the picture is a faint dust of the road network rather than the road network. | 2026-08-17, with the row work in [ADR-034](adr/ADR-034-server-and-studio.md) §5i | **Chosen, and the two alternatives were worse.** A stock glyph per geometry type says only what `geometryType` already says, which §6 rules out as decoration carrying no signal; un-deferring ADR-004 for a hundred-pixel box is the shape §82 exists to refuse. **And the cheap version was measured rather than assumed:** 200 features spanned 75% of `tr_il`'s extent and still read as *empty*, so the fault was density, not sampling; 800 spans 96% at 115 KB and 34 ms, and 2,000 costs 290 KB and 114 ms for no better picture. | **When there is a renderer, or a cheaper source of one honest picture.** Two routes, and the second is nearer: (1) ADR-004 lands and a service gets a real thumbnail, cached, drawn once rather than per viewer; (2) a **low-zoom vector tile** already holds every feature of a hosted layer simplified for exactly this — one tile instead of a sample — but tiles are hosted-only (Q-67), so a registered layer would still need the query path, and decoding MVT in the browser is code we do not have. | **A picture that under-reports is a specific kind of wrong: it is read as a fact about the data.** An operator glancing at the list sees `tr_yol` nearly blank beside `tr_kara` solid and concludes the road layer is thin, when it holds twenty thousand times more geometry. The hover text says *800 of more of this layer's features* and the caption under the table says it samples — which is the mitigation, and hover text is a weak place to carry a correction. | **OPEN, and the trigger fired without this row noticing — 2026-08-24.** The honesty is still in the label rather than the picture, and what is not compromised is unchanged: the preview is drawn from the layer's own data at every step, never from a guess, and it works for registered tables as well as hosted ones. **But *when there is a renderer* has happened.** `Graticula.Render.Skia` is a project, [ADR-041](adr/ADR-041-the-map-renderer.md) is the map renderer, `GET /rest/services/{name}/MapServer/export` turns a service into an image and WMS `GetMap` does it through the other face — so **route (1) of this row's own trigger is available**: a real thumbnail, cached, drawn once rather than per viewer. **And `preview.js` was still telling the next reader the opposite** — *ADR-004 is `DEFERRED` … so this server has no path that turns geometry into pixels* — which is the sentence that would stop somebody from doing the work. Corrected, because a false premise in the file somebody would edit is worse than the debt it describes. ~~**Not swapped, and the reason is this file's own standard.** … a render per row against a list of forty has no number at all~~ — **it has one now. Measured 2026-08-24, and it is not close.** A layer of 5,000 polygons published against a scratch store, both paths against the same server, median of five: **the preview costs 17–23 ms and 139.5 kB; the rendered thumbnail costs 70–76 ms and 1.8 kB.** The render is three to four times slower per request and **seventy-seven times smaller on the wire**. **On a list of forty that is 5.6 MB against 72 kB** — and the asymmetry is worse than those numbers, because this row's own trigger says the thumbnail is *cached, drawn once rather than per viewer*: the 70 ms is paid once per layer and the 139 kB is paid by every viewer, every time. It also renders a **registered** layer, which was the open question about whether the export path covers what the preview covers; the measured layer was registered rather than hosted. **So the numbers favour the swap and the swap is still not done, for a reason that is not the numbers.** The preview is a `<canvas data-preview>` emitted from five places in `console.js`, and the `cover` object it reads has a different shape at three of them — so the change is five markup sites, a fallback for a service with no renderable face, and a look at the result on a screen the owner reviews. **That is a UI change with an owner in it, not a debt payment**, and doing it blind would be the mistake [D-46](#) instance 8 records. The debt is now *measured and favourable*, which is what was missing. The run is [benchmarks/thumbnail](../benchmarks/thumbnail/RESULTS.md). |
| D-59 | ~~**Nothing tests the console, and both of the day's defects were found by the owner pressing a button.**~~ **CLOSED 2026-08-18.** `tests/` has no browser and no JavaScript harness: the only assertion about these pages is `SecurityHeaderConformanceTests`, which checks that every file the HTML asks for is served and permitted by the policy. What it cannot see is behaviour. On 2026-08-17, within an hour of each other: **Stop on a service row opened the service page instead of stopping it** — the row's click handler carried an exception list (*unless it was Delete, unless it was the sharing select*) that a third control was not added to — and **a browser holding only the session cookie painted the whole console as a signed-in administrator while every write answered 401** *"you are not signed in"*. Both are one click deep. | Pre-existing; named 2026-08-17 when it cost twice in one session | **Reasonable while the console was a probe** — it began as a page to look at the admin API with, and a test harness for a page that may be thrown away is the wrong spend. It stopped being reasonable when [ADR-034](adr/ADR-034-server-and-studio.md) made it two surfaces with a privilege gate between them: the gate is UI behaviour, and there is nothing that can assert it. | **With ADR-034 condition 1**, which asks for a test that signs in without `admin:manageServer` and finds no Server surface — that is a browser test whichever way it is written, so the harness and the condition arrive together. Blocked behind [D-56](#) for the second user. **The cheap first step needs no browser:** the click dispatcher is a pure function of *what was clicked*, and could be exercised against a small DOM. **And a browser step turned out to be cheaper than this row assumed** — which is what made closing it a day's work rather than a project. Verifying the dialog above needed a click, and headless Chrome delivered one in about twenty lines: a same-origin page plants the token, a second script stubs `confirm` and polls for the button, and the screenshot is the assertion. That is not a test suite — nothing fails a build — but it is evidence that the harness this row asks for is a small piece of work rather than a project, and every console defect this month would have been caught by it. | **The owner is the test suite for the console, and they should not be.** Each of these cost a round trip — a screenshot, a report, a diagnosis — for a fault a harness would have caught before the page was served. And the *class* recurs: a click dispatcher with an exception list per row grows one control at a time, and an authentication check that asks the wrong question looks correct in every review. | **CLOSED by `tests/Graticula.Console.Tests`: seven tests in a real browser, run in CI, and each one falsified before it was believed.** **Four console defects, four tests, and only two of the four are named in this row** — the other two arrived after it was written: the Server/Studio switch painted for a publisher ([ADR-034](adr/ADR-034-server-and-studio.md) condition 1) and eight of nine services with no reachable Limits ([D-61](#)). The remaining three tests are their pairs, for the reason below. **No automation package.** Chrome speaks the DevTools protocol over a WebSocket and `ClientWebSocket` is in the framework, so `DevTools.cs` is three hundred lines of launch, navigate and evaluate — most of it launching a browser and finding its debugging port. Counted rather than estimated: the project file's own comment said *ninety* until the lines were counted, which is the same habit `tools/conditions.py` exists to break. Playwright would have added a browser download to a workflow whose runner already carries Chrome, for auto-waiting and a selector engine this suite does not use; the trade is written in the project file so that whoever needs those knows when it stopped being the right call. **Reads reach the server and writes do not leave the page.** What these tests are about is which code path a click takes, and a suite that stops the operator's services to prove a button works is worse than no suite — so `fetch` is replaced for anything that is not a `GET`, and the recorded request is the assertion. **Each test was verified by putting its defect back.** The dispatcher's exception list, the boot gating on `authenticated` rather than on a token, `[hidden]` left to lose against an author `display`, and Limits removed from `SERVICE_PAGES` — reintroduced one at a time into the running server's `wwwroot`, and each time the one test that should go red went red, with the message it was written to give. A test that has never failed is a claim, not a check. **Every test comes in a pair, and that is a rule rather than a habit.** The cheapest way to pass *Stop must not open the service* is to stop the row navigating at all; the cheapest way to pass *a cookie session is not painted as an administrator* is to paint nobody. So the opposite direction is asserted beside each: the row still opens, the token still gets in, the administrator still sees both surfaces. **And the suite found two defects in itself first, both of the family it exists to catch.** A click that waited for an element and then clicked it in a second call raced the console's own redraw — the gap was real and the failure said *cannot read properties of null* about a console that was working. And two console addresses can differ only in their hash, which a browser treats as the same document, so navigating from one folder's listing to another's returned instantly with the first folder's rows still on screen: the reachability test collected one folder's services and attributed them to the other's address, and failed about one run in three naming a different service each time. Both are fixed at the root — one atomic expression, and a blank page between opens — rather than with a longer wait. **Widened 2026-08-18 from the four defects to the surface they were on.** All four were specific — a dispatcher, a boot gate, a CSS rule, a shortcut — and what they had in common is that nothing had ever opened those screens and looked. `EveryScreenTests` now does: all four Server tabs, both Studio tabs, and all five pages of a layer's editor across the two surfaces, each asked three questions — did the view become the open one, did it paint its own heading, and **did anything throw**. The third is the one a screenshot cannot answer: the console loads each section independently so one refused endpoint cannot blank the page, which is the right design and means a section that threw leaves the rest looking finished. The plant now listens for `error` and `unhandledrejection`, and `api()` rejects on every refusal, so an uncaught one is a real signal. **Falsified**: an unawaited rejection added to the Operations screen was reported by name. All thirteen tests pass against the owner's ten layers, which is a negative result worth having. **What this suite does not cover, stated because a green tick is read as everything.** It drives Chrome only. It never checks that the server did what the button asked, which is the conformance suite's job on the other side of the same wire. And it needs a running server, so it runs in CI's conformance job and not in the unit job. **The publisher case is the one real gap:** the test removes `admin:manageServer` from the server's own `/rest/whoami` answer rather than signing in as a member who lacks it, because there is no `DELETE /admin/members` and a suite that created one would leave an account behind on every run. That covers the console's rule with the server's shape, and not the letter of [ADR-034](adr/ADR-034-server-and-studio.md) condition 1 — which is why that condition stays **PARTLY DISCHARGED**, and why [Q-116](open-questions.md) asks whether members should be removable at all. |
| D-60 | ~~**The test suite is not reliably green, and it took a week to name because it looks like nothing.**~~ **CLOSED 2026-08-18 — on the second attempt, after the first was committed as a fix and turned out to do nothing.** `dotnet test` on the solution runs the eight assemblies in parallel, and two of them share one PostgreSQL instance in two different ways: `Graticula.Platform.Postgres.Tests` migrates and queries it directly, while `Graticula.Conformance.Tests` drives a **live server** that holds its own pool against the same database. Under that load individual Postgres tests **time out at exactly 30 seconds** and are reported as failures. Measured 2026-08-17: `Filtering_in_the_database_is_what_makes_a_bbox_query_possible_at_all` and `Ring_winding_matches_PostGIS` failed at 30 s in a full run and **both passed in 1 s** when run alone. An earlier full run the same day failed two tests whose names were not captured — the same shape, and the reason this row exists rather than a shrug. | Pre-existing; the conformance suite has needed a live server since it was written, and the contention grew with the number of tests | Not chosen. Each suite is reasonable alone: the Postgres fixtures need a database and the conformance tests need a server, and one PostgreSQL for both is the same *baseline deployment* the product is built around (§6). Nobody decided they would run at once — `dotnet test` decided. | **Before the suite is used as a gate**, which is what [D-59](#)'s browser tests and [ADR-034](adr/ADR-034-server-and-studio.md) condition 1 assume it is. Three candidate fixes were listed here: give the conformance suite its own database; mark the two assemblies as not-parallel (`dotnet test` supports it and it costs wall-clock); or raise the fixture command timeout, which hides the contention rather than removing it. **The second was taken, twice** — first with a setting that does not do it and then with a lock that does. See the status column. | **A suite that fails at random teaches people to re-run it, and a re-run is indistinguishable from a fix.** This session read *2 failed* twice and reasoned past it both times — once without even capturing which tests they were. That is the real cost: the habit, not the minutes. And it lands hardest on exactly the work D-59 asks for, since a browser test is slower and shares the same server. | **CLOSED by `tests/shared/OneSuiteAtATime.cs`: an exclusive file handle, taken by each of the four suites that reach the shared database and held for the life of its test host.** **The first attempt was wrong and was committed as a fix, which is the part of this row worth reading.** `graticula.runsettings` set `MaxCpuCount` to 1 and was wired through `Directory.Build.props`; `MaxCpuCount` limits parallelism **inside one VSTest run**, and `dotnet test` on a solution does not make one run — MSBuild invokes the `VSTest` target per project on as many nodes as it has, which a verbose build shows plainly as nodes 1, 8 and 14. Two solution-wide runs passed under that setting and were taken as evidence. The third failed, with three named Postgres tests timing out at 33, 33 and 49 seconds. **Two runs is not a measurement of a thing that happens sometimes** — the row's own complaint was that a re-run is indistinguishable from a fix, and the fix was accepted on exactly that basis. The dead setting is removed rather than left in place, because a setting nobody can point at an effect of is worse than none. **What made the third run fail was the ninth suite:** `Graticula.Console.Tests` boots the whole console against the server on every page load, and the reachability test does that once per service — so it turned an intermittent problem into a reliable one, which is how the first fix was caught. **`-m:1` works and cannot be used.** One token on the command line, and the whole suite passes in 1 m 38 s with each assembly showing its solo duration. But `Directory.Build.rsp` is not read here — a verbose build with that file in place still spread across nodes — and MSBuild has no property for node count, so it exists only when somebody remembers a flag, which is the failure mode this row is about. It also serialises the build, which is not what contends. **So the lock sits where the contention is:** the five suites that need nothing outside their process still run in parallel, every build is untouched, and the operating system releases the handle when a test host dies — there is no stale lock to clear and no cleanup path that has to run. The row was also **wrong about the scope**, and that correction stands. **CI was never affected** — `.github/workflows/ci.yml` invokes one `dotnet test` per project, for a reason it states itself: three suites FAIL rather than skip without their subject, so a wildcard would make a permanently red build. The parallelism only ever existed in the local `dotnet test graticula.sln`. **That is the worse place for a flake, not the better one:** it is the command a person runs before committing, and one that goes red at random teaches its reader to run it again. This session read *2 failed* twice and reasoned past it, once without recording which tests they were — and then did the same thing again in the shape of a fix, accepting two green runs as proof. **Serialising rather than raising the timeout**, because a longer timeout makes the symptom rarer without removing the contention and has to grow again with the next suite; the cost is wall-clock, paid in seconds by a machine instead of in trust by a person. **The better fix is still not in this repository's gift:** giving the conformance suite its own database is a deployment choice — `GRATICULA_TEST_URL` names a server, not a store — and it stays the standing option for whoever sets up a job that needs both suites at once. **Verified the way the first attempt was not:** two consecutive bare `dotnet test graticula.sln` runs, 1,132 passed and 0 failed in each, plus the nine individual suite runs behind the console work. Two runs proved nothing the first time and prove little now; what carries this is that the mechanism is one anybody can point at — a handle that one process holds and another waits for. |
| D-61 | **The console edits a service's settings on each of its layers' pages, so four layers look like four configurations of one thing — and one of them wrote a value that 500'd the service.** Owner, 2026-08-17: *"bir serviste n tane katman olabilir. ama bu her katmanın kendi ayarı olacağı anlamına gelmez. her servisin kendi ayarları olur. look_earlyAlert'te böyle saçma bir şey gördüm. Bu tamamen saçmalık."* **The storage was never wrong** — `capability_ceiling`, `max_record_count`, `statement_timeout_ms`, `sharing`, `status`, `style` are all columns on `service`, and `/admin/services/{name}/capabilities` addresses a service. **The lie is the screen:** the layer editor's *Capabilities*, *Limits* and *Sharing* pages read and write the owning service, so opening `look_EarlyAlert_routes` and saving changes what `look_EarlyAlert_sites` and `look_EarlyAlert_reports` offer, under three different layer names and with no statement anywhere that they are one object. Of the layer editor's six pages, **four show a fact the layer does not own** — General's status and sharing pills too. | Since the editor was written; `service_id` and the per-service columns arrived in migration 11 and the pages were not moved | Not chosen. Every published layer was its own service until 2026-08-15, so a layer page *was* a service page and the two were indistinguishable. When a service became a container of layers, the storage followed and the screens did not — the same shape as [D-24](#) and [D-57](#), where a fact moved onto the service and one writer stayed behind. | **Now**, and it has already cost: `hosted/look_EarlyAlert` was carrying `capability_ceiling = {}` and its service document answered **500** to anonymous clients, with sixteen conformance failures behind it. An empty ceiling is what a per-layer Capabilities page submits when it draws unticked boxes for a service it did not read. The 500 is repaired separately — an empty capabilities string is a real state and now renders — but **the way that value came to exist is this row.** | **An operator changes what they did not mean to change, and cannot tell.** Nothing on those pages says *this belongs to the service*, so narrowing one layer's operations silently narrows its siblings'; and a reader comparing two layers of one service sees the same numbers and concludes they were configured alike. It also makes the console teach a wrong model of the product, which is worse than a wrong number. | **PARTLY REPAIRED 2026-08-17, and the repair broke reachability for a turn.** Capabilities and Limits are on the service page behind a left nav, the shape ArcGIS Server Manager puts on a service; the layer editor keeps its identity, endpoints, cache TTL, style and unpublish, and its Save is a no-op that says where the settings went. **Two follow-on defects the owner found within minutes, both mine:** the *Settings* link on every row of the service's layer list survived the move — the doorway to a claim whose room had already gone (*"yahu böyle bir ayar yok. tüm servisin ayarları var, o kadar"*) — and, worse, a single-layer service row still took the *skip the drill-in* shortcut added when that page was a one-row table, so **eight of nine services had no reachable Capabilities or Limits at all** (*"tüm servislerden limits ler uçmuş"*). A service row opens its service now, and its list opens a layer by its name. **Still open, and as of 2026-08-24 both are written down as questions rather than as intentions**: whether group layers belong in this model — [Q-142](open-questions.md), which the owner has questioned and which reverses their own 2026-08-15 instruction, so it is a question rather than a deletion — and whether `layer.cache_seconds` should follow the rest onto the service as ArcGIS's Caching page does, which is [Q-143](open-questions.md) and is **not** the same as the other four: cache lifetime is genuinely per layer, so moving it loses a real case rather than repairing a lie. **Both are the owner's to take**, which is why this row is blocked rather than unstarted. **The plan, unchanged:** Capabilities, Limits, Sharing and the stored style move to `#/service/{folder}/{name}`, which already exists and already hosts exactly this kind of editor for the geometry service. The layer keeps what is genuinely its own — its identity, its `cache_seconds`, its addresses, and unpublishing itself. |
| D-62 | **Two files of production source were never in the repository, and `.gitignore` was why.** `src/Graticula.Platform/Secrets/SecretProtector.cs` and its exception seal every registered data source credential — ADR-002 §4.7, AES-GCM with the key version travelling beside the ciphertext, which is independent review finding O7's repair. `.gitignore` carried `secrets/` under *Local configuration and secrets*, meant for a deployment's own credential directory. Unanchored, that pattern matches a directory of that name at any depth, and on Windows it matches case-insensitively — so it swallowed the source directory. **A clone of this repository could not build**, and the product is a gift whose whole point is that somebody else can pick it up. | Since the pattern and that directory first coexisted — 2026-08-12 at the earliest, and the file is dated within the first week | Not chosen, and not carelessness either: an ignore rule for secrets is right, and `secrets/` reads as obviously correct until a source directory is named that. What is missing is the anchor. | **Repaired 2026-08-18**, in the same hour it was found. `/secrets/` pins the rule to the repository root, where a deployment's credential directory would be; the two files are recovered and committed; and `git status --short --ignored src tests` now shows nothing but `bin/` and `obj/`. | **An ignored file is invisible in a way an untracked one is not.** `git status` says nothing about it — that is what ignoring means — and the working copy builds, so every check anybody runs passes. **CI would have caught it and did not, for a reason worse than the one first written here:** this row said no push had happened since the file appeared, and nine pushes had. Every one of their CI runs failed to start — see [D-63](#). This was found by accident: a `git clean -x` during unrelated work deleted the working copy, and the missing type was recovered by decompiling a build output that happened to be outside the repository. **Had that copy not existed, security code would have been rewritten from its call sites.** | **CLOSED, and the lesson is a check rather than a memory.** The failure has no symptom on the machine where the work happens, so noticing it cannot be the plan: what would catch it is a CI step that clones into an empty directory and builds, which is a different thing from the current job's `actions/checkout` of a branch that already has everything. Recorded as [Q-117](open-questions.md) rather than added here, because *what does CI actually prove about a fresh clone* is a question with more than this instance in it. |
| D-63 | **CI has never executed a single step, and both the register and CLAUDE.md say it runs on every push.** Measured 2026-08-18 with `gh run list`: **nine runs since 2026-08-16, every one `startup_failure`, every one 0 seconds**, across both workflows. The API reports `"path": "BuildFailed"` and an empty workflow name, which is GitHub's shape for *the run never started*. So `ci.yml` and `security.yml` have built nothing, tested nothing, audited no dependency and checked no register, on any commit, ever. **Re-measured 2026-08-23, and it is worse and more precisely known.** Runs since: many more, every one a 0-second `startup_failure`, including the nightly `schedule`. **Neither real workflow has a single run to its name** — `actions/workflows/335728860/runs` and `.../335728861/runs` both report a total of zero — and every push instead produces *one* synthetic run under workflow id 335728876, which is not a workflow this repository has. So the row's *across both workflows* was wrong too: it is one rejected run per event, not two. | The workflows were added 2026-08-16; the first run failed to start and so has every one since | Not chosen, and not visible either. A `startup_failure` shows in the Actions list as a *completed* run with a red mark, and nothing in the repository reads that list. The workflow files were written, reviewed and reasoned about as though they were running. | **Now, and the next step is the owner's — which is no longer an inference.** The row used to reason from the workflow files being locally valid to an account-level block, and *locally valid* cannot tell a bad workflow file from a blocked account: `gh run view` says *this run likely failed because of a workflow file issue*, which points the other way. **So it was measured instead.** `.github/workflows/probe.yml` is one job, one step, `run: echo`, no actions and no dependencies; dispatched directly it is identified by name and path and fails in **0 seconds** exactly like the rest. A direct dispatch of `ci.yml` does the same, and is likewise identified correctly — so GitHub reads the files, names them, registers the nightly cron from one of them, and then starts nothing. **No edit to `ci.yml` or `security.yml` can fix this**, and that is now demonstrated rather than argued. Everything in this repository's gift is already permissive: Actions `enabled: true`, `allowed_actions: all`, no runner restriction. What is left is above the repository — the Actions spending limit or billing on a **private** repository — and reading it needs the `user` scope this checkout's token does not hold. **The one lever on this side is that a public repository has no such limit, and [CLAUDE.md §7](../CLAUDE.md) already records that this project is given away** — but that is the owner's decision and a secret sweep of the whole history is its precondition, so it is named here rather than taken. | **Every claim that rests on CI is currently false, and there are four.** [D-29](#) is marked **PAID** on the grounds that the workflow files exist — the row's own words are *"now builds, audits dependencies, and runs all eight suites"*, and it has never done any of it. CLAUDE.md §1 says the status page is *"checked against the registers on every push"*. `ci.yml`'s own summary step tells a reader which coverage it reaches. [ADR-025](adr/ADR-025-governance-and-maintenance.md)'s ninety-day vulnerability promise rests on `security.yml`'s nightly scan, which has never run — so a CVE published in the last two days would have gone unreported, which is exactly the gap that file was written to close. **And it makes a claim of mine lucky rather than right:** [D-60](#) says *CI was never affected* by the test contention. True — because CI was never affected by anything. | **OPEN, and it is the owner's move — with the diagnosis now closed.** Recorded rather than worked around: adding a second provider, or running the checks from a machine here, would be answering a question that has not been asked. What this row buys is that the four claims above are marked as unproven instead of quietly assumed, and that whoever looks at the Actions tab knows what they are looking at. **What 2026-08-23 adds is that nobody need look at the workflow files again.** The minimal probe is kept in the repository for exactly that reason: `gh workflow run probe.yml --ref main` answers *does Actions work here at all* in ten seconds, so whoever changes something at the account level can check it without a twelve-minute build and without believing anybody's reasoning, including this row's. **Re-measured 2026-08-24 and unchanged:** eight pushes that day, every one a 0-second `startup_failure`, and a fresh `probe.yml` dispatch failed in two seconds; `actions/permissions` still reports `enabled: true` and `allowed_actions: all`. **The decision this row waits on is now written down as one** — [Q-135](open-questions.md): raise the spending limit, or make the repository public and pay the precondition of a full-history secret sweep. The two are not equivalent and neither is this side's to take, which is why the row stays open rather than being worked around. |
| D-64 | ~~**A generated test corpus was a different corpus on every generation, and a test pinned a number from one of them.**~~ **CLOSED 2026-08-18.** `tools/make-shapefile-corpus.py` exported fifty real polygons with `limit 50` and no `order by`, so which fifty was whatever the planner returned. `ShapefileReaderTests.A_hole_is_recognised_by_containment_rather_than_winding` asserted that exactly two of them carried a hole. | Since the corpus was written; the fixture is not committed, deliberately and correctly, so nothing compared one generation to the next | Not chosen. The corpus is generated rather than committed for a good reason — it is binary test data built from a developer's own OSM extract — and *generated* was taken to mean *reproducible* without anybody checking. | **Repaired when it fired.** The query is ordered by `osm_id`, so the corpus is now a function of the database rather than of the run. The test no longer pins the count: how many real polygons carry a hole is a fact about whichever extract is loaded, not about the reader, and the reader's actual rule is asserted exactly on the deterministic `holed` fixture by `A_ring_wound_the_other_way_is_a_hole`. What is left is what only real data can check — at least one hole is recognised, and none of the fifty became one shell inside another, which is the shape PostGIS refuses. | **A green suite that cannot be reproduced is a suite that will go red for the next person, with no change in the code.** Found by regenerating: the same database gave three holed polygons instead of two and the test failed. The cost was small here because the assertion was incidental; the same shape with a load-bearing number would have been diagnosed as a regression. | **CLOSED.** |
| D-65 | **The conformance suite's coverage was decided by row order, and it cost a real defect.** Most of the suite asks its question of `FirstServiceNameAsync()` — whichever FeatureServer the catalogue happens to list first — so an invariant written as *for every layer this server serves* was asserted about one layer and the rest went unexamined. `Pages_do_not_overlap_or_skip` was in the suite, passing, for the four days that three of the owner's ten layers were skipping rows ([D-21](#)); it went red the moment an unrelated change moved which service came first. **A test whose coverage is a fact about the data reports on the data rather than on the server.** | Since the suite was written; every test added since copied the habit, which is what made it a habit | Not chosen. Taking the first service is the obvious way to write the first such test, and it is *correct* for the tests that are about one document's shape or about the HTML query page — which is most of them. What was never separated is those from the ones whose claim is universal. | **Now for the universal ones, and it is done for four of them.** The rest are genuinely per-server — a form's parameters, a button, an Accept header — and widening those would buy nothing but wall-clock. | **A green conformance run was overstating what it proved, and conformance is the one suite whose whole job is a compatibility claim.** The compatibility promise is *an unmodified ArcGIS client keeps working against every layer*, and the evidence was *against one layer*. | **CLOSED 2026-08-24 — and partly repaired 2026-08-18 before that.** `EveryServiceNameAsync()` and `EveryLayerAndQueryAsync()` are on `ArcGisClient`, and four claims now walk every layer: the six document-versus-query consistency checks (merged into `Every_layer_is_consistent_with_its_own_document`, which names the layer in every message), paging, the advertised maximum, and polygon ring winding. Each asserts it examined at least one layer, so none of them can pass vacuously. Re-measured against the owner's ten: all four hold everywhere, which is a useful negative result — the paging fix was not a special case. Falsified by inverting one expectation and watching it name the layer. ~~**Still open:** thirteen tests in `ArcGisConsistencyTests` and five in `ArcGisDiscoveryTests` still ask only the first service~~ — **and one of them was `Pages_do_not_overlap_or_skip`, which this row says was widened. It was not. Measured 2026-08-24 and widened today.** **That is the fourth claim of the four, and it is the one this row was opened on.** The paging test is the whole story above: it sat in the suite passing for the four days that three of the owner's ten layers were skipping rows, and went red only when an unrelated change moved which service came first. The resolution said it now walks every layer; it was still calling `FirstServiceNameAsync`. **So the register recorded the intention and the file kept the habit** — which is exactly the failure the row describes, one level up: a claim about coverage that nothing checked. It now iterates `EveryServiceNameAsync()` and carries the same *examined at least one* guard the other three have, for the reason they have it: a server whose services all held one feature would pass every assertion by never reaching one. ~~**What remains is the note**: twelve tests in `ArcGisConsistencyTests` and five in `ArcGisDiscoveryTests` ask only the first service, and what is missing is a note on each saying which kind it is.~~ **CLOSED 2026-08-24, and the note is a parameter rather than a comment.** `FirstServiceNameAsync` takes `whyOneIsEnough`, so **the compiler asks the next person which kind their test is before it will build** — which is the note this row wanted, in the only form that cannot be skipped. Sixteen call sites across the two suites now carry a sentence: *the form's parameter list is the server's, not a layer's*; *content negotiation is one decision made before any layer is looked at*; and for the four `Step` tests, *this is a walkthrough of one service on purpose*. **A comment would have been optional and a parameter is not**, which is the whole difference — the four days this row is about were four days in which nothing at the call site distinguished a universal claim from a per-server one. **And a compiler cannot check that a string says anything**, so `A_test_that_asks_one_service_says_why_one_is_enough` refuses a reason too short to be a sentence, which is the shape a placeholder takes. Falsified by replacing one with *per server*: it named the file and the string. |
| D-66 | **The one owner column nothing reads has a foreign key; the two that are read have none.** `layer.owner_principal_id` — vestigial since migration 11 moved ownership onto the service, and named as dead by [D-33](#) — carries `references principal on delete set null`. `service.owner_principal_id` and `folder.owner_principal_id`, which the catalogue reports and the console displays, carry **no foreign key at all**. Measured 2026-08-18 against the live schema, both counts zero. So a plain `delete from principal` cascades the credential, the sessions, the roles and the api keys, sets the dead column to null, and leaves every service and folder that member owned pointing at a principal that does not exist — with nothing raised. | Migration 11 moved ownership to the service and did not move the constraint with it; migration 18 created `folder` with the same column and no key | Not chosen. The `layer` key was written when the layer was the owned thing, and it is still correct for the column it guards — the column simply stopped mattering. Nobody wrote a key for the columns that took over, because nothing deleted a principal, so the hole had no way to show itself. | **Now, and it is why [ADR-015](adr/ADR-015-authentication.md) §6c makes the disposition mandatory rather than optional.** A member delete is the first operation that can reach this, so the delete refuses without a disposition and the two dispositions each leave the columns consistent by construction. That closes the hole for the path that exists; **the key itself is still owed**, because the next writer to delete a principal will not have read §6c. | **A dangling owner is invisible.** The catalogue reports an owner name by joining to `principal`, so an orphaned service reports no owner — indistinguishable from one published before ownership was recorded. Nothing refuses, nothing logs, and the service keeps serving; the fact is simply gone. **REPAID 2026-08-24, migration 33.** Both live columns carry `references principal on delete set null` now — matching the key the dead column already had, and chosen over `restrict`, which would refuse a delete an administrator has to be able to complete, and over `cascade`, which would delete somebody's services because their account was removed. ADR-015 §6c's dispositions remain what keep the rows valid; this is the floor under that rule, for the next writer who has not read it. **An expand, and worth saying why a constraint is one**: nothing is dropped, and a previous release runs unchanged because the only owner ids it has ever written are principal ids. **Orphans are cleared in the same migration** — there were none here, measured, but a deployment that deleted a principal by hand before this shipped is exactly who a migration that fails on their data would strand. `OwnerKeyTests` covers the delete on both tables and the invented id that `on delete set null` does not reach; all three fail with migration 33 out of the set. | repaid 2026-08-24, migration 33 |
| D-47 | ~~**The publish endpoint accepted three documented fields and discarded them, so multi-layer services were unreachable through the admin API.**~~ **CLOSED 2026-08-16.** `POST /admin/layers` takes `serviceName`, `parentLayerId` and `cacheSeconds`. `TryReadPublication` built its `LayerPublication` with the first ten arguments and stopped; all three of the record's remaining parameters default to `null`, **so the short call compiled** and the endpoint answered `201` having thrown the operator's intent away. Naming an existing service produced a layer in a service of its own, and the response's `service` field reported that new name, so the answer looked correct. **The catalogue underneath was right the whole time** — `ServiceName ?? Name`, `parent` and `cache` are all bound in the SQL — and `PostgresAdminCatalogTests` asserts the multi-layer behaviour at that level. So the owner's 2026-08-15 correction that *"a service is a combination of layers"* was implemented, tested, and unreachable. `POST /admin/featureservices` was meanwhile answering with a note instructing the operator to *"publish with serviceName set to this service"*, which is an API documenting its own broken path. | 2026-08-15 (shipped), found and closed 2026-08-16 | Nothing. Optional parameters on a record turned a missing argument into valid code, and no test spanned the request-to-record mapping. | — closed | — closed | **CLOSED.** The three fields are passed through, with `serviceName` trimmed and blank treated as absent. Verified end to end against a running server: publishing into an existing service now answers `service: probe_svc, layerId: 1`, and the service document shows that layer nested under the group at 0. **What is still missing is the test, and its absence is the finding:** there is no in-process host harness anywhere in `tests/`, so nothing exercises an admin endpoint's mapping — the layer below is covered and the layer above is not. That gap is [D-48](#)'s neighbour and the reason this survived. |
| D-48 | ~~**A service and a group layer can be created through the admin API and cannot be removed, and unpublishing a layer leaves its service behind.**~~ **CLOSED 2026-08-17, except for one half that turned out to be a different debt — see D-54.** `POST /admin/featureservices` and `POST /admin/services/{name}/groups` have no `DELETE` counterpart — the only deletes on the surface are `/admin/layers/{name}` and a style. Measured 2026-08-16: publishing four layers and deleting all four left **five empty services** in the catalogue, three of them created implicitly by the layers themselves. They remain in the services directory, so a client sees a FeatureServer with no layers, and the only way to remove them is SQL against the platform store. | Pre-existing; measured 2026-08-16 while building the console's create surface | Not chosen. Creation was built for the case the owner asked about — several layers in one service — and removal was never the question in front of anybody. Single-layer publishing hid it, because deleting the layer left a service nobody looked for. | **Before the console's create surface is used on a real deployment.** An operator who can make structure and not unmake it either stops using it or edits the database, and the second is worse. Concretely: `DELETE /admin/featureservices/{name}` refusing while layers remain, `DELETE /admin/services/{name}/groups/{id}` refusing while children remain, and unpublish removing a service it implicitly created. | Empty services accumulate in every deployment that experiments, and each one is advertised to clients. It also makes the create surface untestable in a suite that has to clean up after itself, which is part of why D-47 had no test. | **CLOSED for removal, and the listing was the bigger half.** `GET /admin/featureservices` reports every service with what it holds; `DELETE /admin/featureservices/{name}` and `DELETE /admin/services/{name}/groups/{index}` remove them. **Both refuse while occupied, with 409 and a count** — `'look_EarlyAlert' still holds 3 layers and 1 group layer` — because a cascade would unpublish layers as a side effect of a call about their container, and unpublishing purges tiles and forgets a remembered shape. Children are not reparented for the same reason: it would move them in every saved map that points at them. **404 and 409 are kept apart**, since absent means the name may be wrong and occupied means the order of operations is. **Writing it found the half nobody had stated:** an empty service appeared in no listing anywhere — the layer list holds layers and `/admin/services` is the system-services table — so the residue this debt is about was *invisible*, and the missing delete had nothing to be missing from. The console now has a Services panel with layer and group counts, a Delete disabled with its reason where it would be refused, and a group list in the drawer that creates groups. Six tests, and measured end to end against a live catalogue: occupied service 409, empty service gone, absent 404, occupied group 409 with its child count, empty group gone, and **the freed index is not reused** — the service document returns to `[0,1,2,3]` and the deleted index answers 404 rather than somebody else's layer. |
| D-53 | ~~**The importer accepts invalid geometry, and nothing on the publish path checks.**~~ **CLOSED 2026-08-18 — both halves, and neither of them repairs anything.** Measured 2026-08-17: `hosted.tr_ilce_511f6767`, imported from OpenStreetMap by our own importer, holds **18 invalid geometries out of 25,280** — `ST_IsValidReason` says *"Too few points in geometry component"*, so they are degenerate rings rather than self-intersections. Neither `PostGisImporter` nor `POST /admin/layers` looks: import writes what it parsed, and publish records a table it never validates. **Found by another server refusing to publish it** — the peer's publish path validates the source table first and answered *"Table validation failed (invalid-geometries): Source table contains invalid geometries"*, which is a capability we do not have, exercised against our data. | Pre-existing; the OSM import ran 2026-08-16 and the layer has been served since | Not chosen. Validity was never asked about: the importer's job was read GeoJSON and write PostGIS, and PostGIS accepts an invalid geometry happily — `geometry` has no validity constraint, so nothing refuses on the way in. | **Before the publish surface is used on data nobody inspected**, which is the case it was built for. Two separate repairs: the importer reports invalid counts (and decides whether to refuse, repair with `ST_MakeValid`, or record), and publish validates the source the way the peer does. The second matters more for registered tables, where the data is somebody else's and we did not import it. | An invalid geometry is not a stored problem, it is a query-time one: `ST_Intersects` against a degenerate ring can error mid-scan, so a filter that touches one of those 18 rows fails a request that succeeds everywhere else — the hardest class of fault to reproduce. It also silently propagates into every tile and every export. | **CLOSED. Both paths ask, both report, and neither repairs.** 18 of 25,280 was verified with `ST_IsValid` directly against the table rather than taken from the other server's word, and that number is now what the endpoints answer with. **`GeometryValidity` counts and names the reason, and deliberately does not call `ST_MakeValid`.** Repairing is a change to somebody's data made by a process they asked to *store* it, and the repair is not neutral: it can drop a degenerate ring, split a polygon into a multipolygon, or turn an area into a line. A server that hands back different geometry from what it was given is one nobody can reconcile against their source. Nor does it refuse by itself — a registered table belongs to somebody else, and 18 bad rows in 25,280 is usually still worth serving. **Import reports it in the response and in the audit record.** Measured end to end: an upload of the 50-polygon corpus answered 201 with `{"valid": true, "invalid": 0, "note": "All 50 geometries are valid."}`. Asked after the commit, because a validity scan is a full sequential pass and holding a write lock for the length of one would be a worse defect than the one being fixed — and it cannot fail the import, because an import that wrote everything and then could not *count* still wrote everything. **Publish asks the source's own database, over the registered credential.** The table may live in somebody else's server entirely, so reading it through the platform store's pool would answer about a table of the same name in the wrong place. `requireValidGeometry` is a new flag, **off by default and that default is the decision**: the count is always reported and the refusal is asked for. Measured end to end against the real table — with the flag, **422** and *"18 of 25,280 geometries are invalid: Too few points in geometry component"* with no row created; without it, 201 and the same count in the response. A source PostGIS is happy with answers *"All 12 geometries are valid"*, which is what tells an operator the question was asked at all. **Two corrections to this row's own description, both found by measuring.** `hosted.tr_ilce_511f6767` is **25,280 `ST_LineString` rows**, not polygons — district boundaries imported as lines — so the 18 faults are lines with fewer than two *distinct* points, and *"degenerate rings"* was wrong. And **PostGIS's WKT parser refuses the shape outright** (`XX000: geometry requires more points`), so the invalid rows exist because our importer writes WKB and the binary path performs no such check; a test written only from WKT would have concluded the database was protecting us. **Held by three tests** (`GeometryValidityTests`): the count and a readable reason on a fixture that reproduces the real fault, the pair on a table PostGIS is happy with, and an identifier with a space and an embedded quote — because there is no parameter form for an identifier in SQL and the composition uses `format(… %I …)`, PostgreSQL's own quoting rather than ours. **One defect on the way in, caught by the database:** the validity was appended to the audit detail as text, and that column is `json`, so an import that had already written its data answered 503. It goes inside the object now. |
| D-52 | ~~**The startup compatibility check says "compatible" to a build that cannot read the store, and then the server does not start.**~~ **CLOSED 2026-08-18, and the diagnosis in this row was wrong.** Found 2026-08-17 while adding migration 16. A build whose reader selects a column the store does not have yet passes the check — it printed *"server at schema 15 is compatible with the platform store (schema 15, minimum reader 1)"* — and then died with an unhandled `Npgsql.PostgresException 42703: column s.serves_features does not exist`, having bound no port. ~~**The check is one-directional:** it asks whether a store's *minimum reader* is new enough for this build, which protects an old reader from a newer store, and never asks whether this build needs a schema newer than the store has.~~ **That was false when it was written.** `SchemaCompatibility.Check` has had a `ComponentTooNew` branch — `componentSchemaVersion > stamp.Applied` — since 2026-08-13 (commit `5b77ee1`), four days before this row was recorded, and `UpgradeAndRollbackTests` has covered it since. The row was written from the symptom without reading the check. | Pre-existing; any column-adding migration has had this property. Made visible by migration 16, which is the first one whose column is read on the service path that runs at startup | Not chosen. The compatibility model was written for the direction that loses data — an old build writing to a store it does not understand — and the direction that merely fails to start looked harmless until the failure was a crash with no port bound and a message about a column. | **Before a deployment upgrades a binary without migrating**, which is the ordinary order of operations for anyone who does not read release notes. Concretely: compare `PlatformMigrations.ComponentSchemaVersion` against the store's applied version at startup and refuse with a sentence naming `migrate --apply`, instead of selecting a column and letting Npgsql explain. | An operator upgrades, restarts, and gets a stack trace about a column name in a table they did not know existed, with the server down. The correct action — run the migration — is not mentioned anywhere in the failure, and the line immediately above it says *compatible*, which sends them looking for a connection problem instead. | **CLOSED 2026-08-18, and the real defect is one the row could not see from the symptom.** **The check was right; the number it was given was stale.** Migration 16 was written and `PlatformMigrations.ComponentSchemaVersion` was left at 15 — which is why the log read *schema 15 is compatible with … (schema 15)*: the comparison was 15 against 15 and correctly concluded *compatible*, and the reader then selected a column that migration 16 adds. **Verified by measurement rather than by reading**, which is what this row's first version failed to do: a schema-23 build pointed at a schema-15 store answered *"server is built for schema 23, and the platform store is at 15. The migration has not been run. Run it, or start the version of this component that matches the store."* — no column exception, no stack trace, no port bound. **What is added is the check that catches the stale constant:** `UpgradeAndRollbackTests.The_declared_schema_version_is_the_highest_migration_this_build_carries` asserts `ComponentSchemaVersion == All.Latest`, with a pair proving the assertion can fail. Nothing else could catch it — a stale constant is invisible on a developer machine, where the migration has been applied and the column exists. **The lesson is the row itself.** It named a mechanism from a symptom, and the named mechanism already existed and worked; a day of work aimed at it would have added a second check beside a correct one and left the real cause. Reading the code the row accuses is cheaper than the row. |
| D-51 | **A session exists in two forms and the console can only clear one, so a failed sign-out looks like a dead button.** Login returns a bearer token *and* sets a `gis-session` cookie — `httponly`, `secure`, `samesite=strict` — carrying the same session id, and `/rest/whoami` accepts either. The console keeps the token in `sessionStorage`, which it can clear, and cannot touch the cookie by design. So **only the server can end a session**, and the console's sign-out used to swallow the failure of the one request that does it (`catch { /* already gone */ }`) and reload anyway: the cookie then signed the operator straight back in and the button appeared to do nothing. Reported by the owner 2026-08-16 — *"it does not sign out, it comes back to the same page."* | Pre-existing; the cookie was added for the browser sign-in flow, the token for clients | Reasonable separately: a form post needs a cookie, an API client needs a header. What was never decided is which one the console's own state is a copy of. | **Whenever a client holds a copy of session state.** The rule that follows: local state is cleared only *after* the server has confirmed, never before and never regardless. | ~~A sign-out that silently does not sign out is worse than one that refuses — the operator walks away from a live session believing it closed.~~ **Neither half is silent now**: the refusal is reported and the local session is left intact behind it, and a check holds both. | **CLOSED 2026-08-24, on measurement rather than on confirmation — and that is the stronger of the two.** Two measured faults were fixed 2026-08-16 and the structure is still two-formed, which this row already calls reasonable: a form post needs a cookie, an API client needs a header. **Both ends are verified.** Server-side, a valid logout revokes the session and returns `gis-session=; expires=Thu, 01 Jan 1970`, and `LogoutAsync` clears the cookie on its early-return path too — arriving there means the session is already gone *and the browser is still holding a cookie for it*, which was the one path that left a stale credential in place. Client-side, the console asks first, stays and reports if refused, and leaves with `location.replace` so the console is not behind the back button. **And the rule this row states is now held by a check rather than by the edit that once obeyed it.** `A_refused_sign_out_does_not_clear_the_console_s_own_session` reads `console.js` from source and asserts the shape of the **failure** path — the `catch` must leave, must tell the caller, and must touch neither `sessionStorage` nor the in-memory token — while the success path must still clear, because a handler that never cleared would sign nobody out and would pass a check written only against the failure. **Falsified by putting the original defect back**, the `catch { /* already gone */ }` that started this row. **What this row was waiting for was the owner saying the symptom is gone**, and that standard was set before either end was verified or guarded. Measurement is the better evidence and it is now on both sides of the wire. **If the symptom recurs it reopens on evidence**, which is how this register works and is why closing it is not the same as deciding it cannot come back. |
| D-49 | ~~**A hosted layer's VectorTileServer declares Web Mercator on an extent that is in degrees.**~~ **CLOSED 2026-08-16.** Measured 2026-08-16 on `hosted/tr_ilce`, stored in EPSG:4326: the tile service document reports `fullExtent` `{xmin: 24.7, ymin: 34.9, xmax: 45.6, ymax: 42.8}` with `spatialReference {wkid: 102100, latestWkid: 3857}`. The numbers are degrees; in Web Mercator that box is a few tens of metres off West Africa. **The FeatureServer for the same layer is correct** — the same extent, declared `4326`, and `/query` returns `4326` coordinates — so this is isolated to the tile service's metadata, which appears to stamp Web Mercator regardless of what the layer is stored in. Tiles themselves serve correctly at every zoom tried. | Pre-existing; found 2026-08-16 | Not chosen. Hosted layers were expected to be stored in Web Mercator — the console's own copy says *"stored in Web Mercator so the layer can serve tiles"* — and the imported `tr_*` layers are in 4326, so the assumption and the data disagree and only the tile document repeats the assumption. | **Before any client is told to trust a tile service's `fullExtent`.** Either the document reports the layer's actual spatial reference, or hosted layers are genuinely always stored in Web Mercator and the import path is what needs fixing. Which of the two is the real decision has not been taken. | A client that zooms to the declared extent goes to the Gulf of Guinea instead of Turkey. **This is the same error, on the server side, that this project made in its own viewer on 2026-08-16** — a centre in degrees read as metres — which is the argument for fixing the class rather than the instance. | **CLOSED on the second attempt, and the first attempt is the part worth keeping.** The first fix made `fullExtent` declare the reference it was actually in — honest, and **non-conformant**: a vector tile service's tiling scheme *is* Web Mercator, and a client reads `fullExtent` in the scheme's reference. Handed a document whose `fullExtent` said 4326 while `tileInfo` said 102100, the ArcGIS JS `VectorTileLayer` fetched the service document, the style and the sprites — and then **requested no tile at all**, settling nothing and reporting nothing. Found only by counting what the server log received, after the owner said the Tiles button did nothing for that layer. **So the extent is projected, not relabelled**: `VectorTileEndpoints` transforms it to Web Mercator through `IProjector` — the datastore's PROJ, per ADR-022 §4 — and the writer now **refuses** any other reference rather than declaring it, so the next caller is told instead of producing a document that loads nothing. A failed projection falls back to no extent, which the writer reports as the whole world: safe for a client, unlike degrees labelled as metres. Verified numerically against a running server — all four corners of `tr_ilce`'s extent match the Mercator arithmetic exactly, and the 3857-stored `look_buildings` is unchanged. **Two silent-hang sites closed with it:** `layer.when()` now races a clock the way `view.when()` does, because a client that stops after the metadata never rejects, and until that clock existed no `catch` could have reported this. |
| D-50 | **The capability probe cannot inform the one publish field the operator must decide.** `POST /admin/layers` requires `identityColumn`, and Q-57 makes that a nomination rather than an inference — *"declared, not inferred"*. The probe reports `objectIdColumn` and nothing else about candidate keys: no primary key, no unique constraints, no column list. So a UI can offer exactly one suggestion, and only when an ArcGIS-shaped object id already exists. | Pre-existing; found 2026-08-16 | Reasonable while publishing was done by whoever knew the table. It stops being reasonable the moment a console offers a table list, because the list implies the server knows enough to help. | **When the console's publish form is used against a table nobody present designed.** Concretely: the probe reporting each table's primary key and unique integer columns, so the nomination is a choice among named candidates rather than a typed guess. | The operator types a column name from memory and a typo becomes a `400`, or worse a wrong-but-existing column becomes the feature identity — which decides which row an edit lands on. | **CLOSED 2026-08-24: the probe answers the question now, and the nomination is a choice among named candidates.** The console still asks for it explicitly, empty and required, and still offers a one-click nomination rather than prefilling it — because prefilling would present the server's inference as the operator's decision, which is what Q-57 refuses. What changed is what there is to choose from. **`SourceTable` gained two fields.** `PrimaryKeyColumn` is the table's own single-column key **whatever its type**, reported even when it cannot be an object id; `IdentityCandidates` is every single-column unique `int2`/`int4` column, the primary key first. `CandidateObjectIdColumn` stays and is now the first of the candidates by construction, so the button and the list it sits beside cannot disagree — two views of one fact is [D-46](#)'s shape and this one is closed by construction rather than by care. `GET /admin/datasources/{id}/capability` reports both. **Measured before and after, against four table shapes built for it**, and the third is the one this row is about. A table with a **text** primary key and two unique integer columns — `ref`, `legacy`, `parcel` — was reported by the old probe as the single word `legacy`: no sign that `parcel` existed, and no mention that the table's own key is `ref`. So an operator either accepted a suggestion that is not the table's key or typed `parcel` from memory, which is exactly the *typo becomes a 400, or worse a wrong-but-existing column becomes the feature identity* this row was opened on. The new probe reports `ref` as the key and `{legacy, parcel}` as the candidates. An ArcGIS-shaped table went from `objectid` to `{objectid, code}`; a composite key and a keyless table report nothing, because **half a key is worse than no key**. **Four tests, and they were falsified**: emptying the candidate array failed two of them by name. 355 Postgres integration tests pass against a live store. **And the first run of all four failed identically for a reason worth keeping** — `NpgsqlDataSource.ConnectionString` redacts the password, so probing with it is a connection attempt with no credential, which the probe reported as `CannotConnect`. It was right and the test was wrong, which is the better way round. |
| D-46 | **A behaviour is fixed in one of the several places that carry it, and the others keep the bug.** Six instances on 2026-08-16, all found by the owner rather than by us, and a seventh the next day found by looking at a screenshot of my own work. **(1)** [D-44](#)'s Content-Security-Policy fix moved `index.html`'s script to a file; `map.html` stayed inline, so the same policy killed the same way one edit later. **(2)** The rule *imported tiles replace the vendored ground* was written into `view.js`; the console's map panel and the SDK page kept drawing Natural Earth, so "it is still showing" was true in two of three places somebody might look. **(3)** The ground picker persisted a choice; the `?ground=` link that documented it did not, so following the documented route left the setting empty. **(4)** A URL was built from `layer.ServiceName` in one place after the same class of guess had just been fixed in another ([D-45](#)). **(5)** `map.js` waits for `view.when()` before calling `goTo`, with a comment saying why — *"after the view is ready, and the failure is said out loud"* — and `console.js` never did, so clicking **Tiles** raced the view's initialisation and failed with *"Cannot read properties of undefined (reading 'animation')"*, intermittently, which is the worst version of it. Reported by the owner on 2026-08-16 with the detail that made it diagnosable: *"sometimes on the first click."* Holding the view in a variable rather than a promise made a second click return the half-built view as though it were ready, so the guard that looked like protection was part of the fault. **(6) The fix for (5) then produced the same button doing nothing at all**, and this one is the clearest instance in the list: `#view` sits inside a panel that is `display: none` until shown, and both callers revealed the panel *after* asking for the view. Awaiting `view.when()` on a hidden, zero-sized container never settles, and memoising the promise made it permanent — every later click joined the same pending promise. No error, no status, nothing logged. Reported as *"I press it and nothing happens."* The tiles branch of the delegated click handler was also the one branch with no `try`, while its sibling had one, so even a rejection would have been silent. Fixed as a class rather than an instance: the panel is revealed inside `ensureMap` where every caller passes, the whole handler is guarded so no branch can fail quietly, and `view.when()` is raced against a clock — because **a hang is not a rejection**, and until that race existed there was no arrangement of `catch` that could have reported this. **(7) The console's stylesheet, 2026-08-17, and this one is D-44's own fix reaching one resource kind and not the next.** The `<style>` block moved out of `index.html` into `console.css` so the console and its new layer page could not drift apart — and the console's policy read `style-src 'unsafe-inline' https://js.arcgis.com`, with no `'self'`. So the server sent every rule and the browser applied none. It did not look like a refusal: unstyled, `.view { display: none }` never arrived, so all five screens stacked into one document and the tab strip lost its layout — a layout bug, which is where I went looking. Both existing guards passed, because both name the resource kind they were written for: one asks whether `script-src` permits `'self'`, the other whether any page carries inline script. **The fix is the enumerating form**: `Every_file_a_console_page_asks_for_is_permitted` walks whatever a console page references — script, stylesheet, whatever is added next — and requires the governing directive to permit this origin and the file to be served. It needs no editing when the next kind arrives, which is the property the other two lack. **(8) A settings page built twice, 2026-08-17, and the second one was ugly enough for the owner to say so:** *"arayüz konusunda çok kötü olduğunu biliyor musun? benim gösterdiğim bile daha güzeldi."* The geometry service's new limits panel was written with `.row` — a wrapping flex — so four labelled boxes landed on two unaligned rows with the actions floating in the middle of the second, the hints wrapped into a four-line ribbon beside two thirds of empty panel, and an empty layers table sat underneath explaining that there were no layers. **Everything it needed already existed one section below in the same file**: the layer editor's Limits page has `.setting` (sentence, box, unit outside), `h4` section groups, and Save/Cancel in the head bar. This instance is not a fix applied in one of N places — it is a *shape* reimplemented rather than reused, which is the same debt from the other direction, and it is the one instance a reviewer could have caught by looking at the screen. **(9) `hidden` did not hide, 2026-08-17, and it had been false the whole time.** `drawSurfaces` sets `hidden` on the Server/Studio switch for a reader allowed only one surface, and the switch stayed on screen: the attribute's own rule is `[hidden] { display: none }` at **user-agent** weight, which any author rule with a `display` beats — and `#surfaces { display: flex }` is one. **Nothing found it for a day**, because there was no second account to look with, and the claim *the switch hides* was written into a commit message and an ADR from reading the code. It is this debt's shape in a stylesheet: the fix is `[hidden] { display: none !important }` once, not `#surfaces[hidden]`, because the next element given both a `display` and a `hidden` would fail the same way and nobody would connect the two. **(10) A container reused because it was convenient, 2026-08-17.** The issued-password panel was written *inside* the new-member form, so **Set password** on a row revealed the *create a member* screen on its way to showing one field. The owner: *"set password diyince neden yeni kullanıcı ekranı açılıyor. modal bir pencerede şifreyi verebilirdi."* Two unrelated things shared a parent for no reason but the order they were written in, which is this debt's shape in markup rather than in behaviour. It is now a `<dialog>` — the element gives the backdrop, the focus trap, Escape and an inert page, and two of those matter here: the password exists once, so a stray click must not dismiss it, and *Set password* must not stay clickable behind a dialog showing the password it just issued. **The shape is always the same:** a behaviour lives in N places, a fix is applied to the one being read, and the reader who finds the others cannot tell a partial fix from no fix. | Recurring; four instances recorded 2026-08-16, ten by 2026-08-17 | Not a compromise anybody chose — an artefact of how a fix is scoped. Each individual instance looked complete from inside the file being edited. | **Whenever a behaviour is about to be fixed**, and the fix is not done until the question *what else carries this?* has an answer. Not a trigger to wait for: it is a step in the work. | Every instance costs a round trip with whoever is using the thing, and it erodes their reason to believe the next fix. Three of the four were reported by the owner in consecutive messages, which is the cost made visible. | **OPEN, and it is a process debt rather than a code one.** Two mitigations exist so far and both were built the same day: shared decisions moved into one file (`ground.js` decides which ground for all three pages), and tests asserting the **invariant** rather than the instance — `No_console_page_carries_an_inline_script` is a `[Theory]` over pages precisely because naming one page protects one page. **Neither helps with instance 8**, and nothing in `tests/` could: *is this screen built out of the idioms the last one used* is not a question a passing test answers, and it is the same absence [D-59](#) records. The cheap discipline that would have worked is a question rather than a tool — *what does the nearest existing screen do* — asked before the markup rather than after the owner. **(10) 2026-08-24, and it was inside this row's own named remedy.** This row calls the enumerating form the repair, quoting `Every_file_a_console_page_asks_for_is_permitted` as the one that *"needs no editing when the next kind arrives, which is the property the other two lack"*. It does have that property for **resource kinds** — and it carries a hand-typed list of **pages**, as does `No_console_page_carries_an_inline_script` beside it, and the two had drifted: four paths against three, with `/studio/view.html` checked for a permitted subresource and never checked for an inline script it must never carry. So the fix for D-44 reached three of the four places that carry it, which is this row's sentence applied to this row's own answer. **Measured before repairing:** `view.html` carries four `<script>` tags and every one of them has `src=`, so nothing was broken — what was missing was any reason to believe it would stay that way, and the same policy that killed `map.html` in instance 1 governs this page. **Repaired as a class.** `Every_console_page_is_covered_by_every_guard_that_claims_all_of_them` reads the console's page set from `wwwroot` rather than from a list, because `Program` serves **one** physical directory under two request paths — so the set of console pages is the set of `.html` files in it, and a guard whose name claims *every console page* must cover each one under either spelling. It is scoped by name deliberately: `The_console_reads_its_session_before_it_paints` is about `session.js`, which only the two shells load, and its two-page list is correct rather than short. **Falsified in both directions**: before the repair it failed naming `view.html` and the guard that had forgotten it; with a fourth page dropped into `wwwroot` it named that page in **both** guards, which is the property this row has been asking for since instance 1 — the next page is covered when it is added, not when somebody remembers two `[Theory]` attributes. Seventeen architecture tests pass with it. **And the row cannot be closed, which is a fact about its shape rather than about the work.** Its trigger says so in its own words — *"Not a trigger to wait for: it is a step in the work"* — and §62 says an entry whose trigger never falls due is not debt but a permanent decision in disguise. Ten instances have been paid one at a time and an eleventh is the expected state, so what this register holds under D-46 is a standing practice wearing a debt row. Whether it stays here or becomes a rule somewhere it can be read before the work rather than after it is [Q-136](open-questions.md), because reclassifying a register entry is a decision and §2 says decisions are recorded rather than taken in passing. |
| D-45 | ~~**`/admin/layers` does not say which service holds a layer, or at which id, so no client can build its URL without guessing.**~~ **CLOSED 2026-08-17.** The listing carries name, table, status, sharing, owner and `arcGisServable`. It does not carry the service or the layer index.
