Guessing `/rest/services/{folder}/{layerName}/FeatureServer/0` is right only when a layer sits alone in a service named after it — and **wrong for every member of a multi-layer service.** `look_EarlyAlert` holds sites at 0, routes at 1, a group at 2 and reports at 3, so the console had been asking for services that do not exist for three of six layers. **What hid it:** the server answers its deliberate *"no layer is visible to you… the same for absent and forbidden"* 404, which is the correct answer to the wrong question and reads to an administrator holding every privilege as a permission problem. Found 2026-08-16 when a new drawer panel displayed the response instead of swallowing it. | Pre-existing; found 2026-08-16 | Not chosen. The listing was written for a catalogue view — *what exists and who can see it* — and nothing noticed that a UI also needs *where is it*. Single-layer services made the guess work for the common case, which is how it survived. | **Before another client is written against this listing**, and before the layer list grows a link anywhere. Concretely: whoever adds a service and index to `/admin/layers` closes this; the console's resolver can then be deleted. | Every consumer re-derives the mapping by walking the services directory — one request per service — or guesses and silently fails on multi-layer services. A UI that cannot address half a service is a UI that gets replaced rather than fixed. | **CLOSED, and the workaround was the more expensive half.** `/admin/layers` now carries `service`, `folder`, `layerIndex` and a built `url`; `AdminLayer.Address` composes it in one place. The console's resolver is deleted, which this row named as the closing condition. **What the workaround cost, recorded because it is the argument for closing a debt rather than living beside it:** resolving through the services directory meant a stopped service — which answers 503 to that walk — fell out of the map, and *everything* built on the map lost it. Three symptoms, one cause, found only on 2026-08-17: the settings page for a stopped layer drew from nothing, its **Save** refused with *"not in the services directory"*, and the service list could not offer a **Start** button to the one service somebody most wanted to start. The publish form's service list and the anonymous view had the same hole — you could not publish into a service you had stopped, and stopped layers were reported as *not probed* rather than probed. A workaround that reads a document with a different visibility rule than the listing it substitutes for is not a smaller version of the fix; it is a second, quieter bug. `Every_catalogued_layer_is_findable_in_the_services_directory` stays: it asserts the catalogue and the directory agree, which is worth checking on its own. |
| D-44 | ~~**The security gate's own fix disabled the console for a day, and the console then leaked a password into a URL.**~~ **CLOSED 2026-08-16.** The §66 security gate of 2026-08-15 found *"not one security header on any response"* and added them. Its `HtmlPolicy` — `default-src 'none'` with **no `script-src`** — was derived, correctly, from the three server-rendered pages, and its own comment said they *"contain no script at all, which is asserted by a test rather than believed."* **The console is a fourth `text/html` surface and it is nothing but script.** So from that day the console ran **no JavaScript at all**: it rendered, because `style-src 'unsafe-inline'` was permitted, and then every button was inert. **And the sign-in form fell back to a native GET** carrying `name="username"` and `name="password"`, writing the password into the address bar, the browser history and any proxy on the way. Found by the owner opening it, not by anything we run. | 2026-08-15 | Nothing. The policy was right for the pages it was written for and was applied to one more. The comment that made it feel checked — *asserted by a test* — was true of those three pages and never covered the console. | — closed | — closed | **CLOSED.** The console gets its own policy by **path**, not by content type, so the three document pages keep the strict one. Its script moved out of the page into `console.js`, so the policy is `script-src 'self' https://js.arcgis.com` with **no `'unsafe-inline'`** — the fix is also the stricter policy. The map SDK is no longer a blocking CDN script in the head: it is fetched on demand, so a blocked CDN or an air-gapped deployment (Q-15) costs the map and not the console. The form now posts and its inputs carry no `name`, so a native submit has nothing to serialise even with zero script, and a `<noscript>` says what is wrong. Two regression tests in `SecurityHeaderConformanceTests`. **The lesson is the shape of the failure:** a blocked script is refused by the browser after the response left, so there is no log line, no error status and no failed request — every existing header test passed throughout, because they assert the headers are present and none asked whether the page still worked with them. |
| D-43 | ~~**The datastore suite fails three tests on the first run after a build, and passes on every rerun.**~~ **DIAGNOSED AND BOUNDED 2026-08-18.** Observed twice on 2026-08-16 with the same shape: `dotnet build` then `dotnet test` gives 243 of 246 in ~1 m 15 s, with `WkbAgainstPostgisTests`, `ArcGisWindingAgainstPostgisTests` and `PostGisFeatureSourceTests` each taking **31–33 s**; an immediate `--no-build` rerun gives 246 of 246 in ~32 s, and each of the three passes in isolation. **What is known:** the durations sit on Npgsql's 30-second default `CommandTimeout`, so these are queries not returning rather than assertions disagreeing. PostgreSQL had 6 of 200 connections in use, so it is not server-side exhaustion. `PostgresFixture` builds its own data source without our `statement_timeout`, so D-42's control is not involved. **What is not known:** the mechanism. Candidates not yet separated — xunit's parallel collections each creating and migrating a schema at once, cold page cache in the container on first touch, or contention on the catalogue during concurrent DDL. | Observed 2026-08-16 | Not a compromise anybody chose. It is recorded because D-30 already says this project's harness has been wrong three times, and a suite that fails only in the configuration CI uses is the fourth candidate. | **Before trusting a red datastore job.** Concretely: before anybody treats a CI failure in these three tests as a regression, or reruns the job to make it green. Also before adding retry to CI, which would hide this rather than fix it. | **CI runs `dotnet build` and then `dotnet test` in the same job** — the exact configuration that reproduces this. So the failure mode is not a local-machine curiosity; it is what CI does every run, and the reason it has not been seen there may only be that the service container is fresh. A flaky red build teaches people to rerun, and rerunning is how a real regression gets dismissed. | **CLOSED 2026-08-24 — and it was diagnosed on 2026-08-18, in the fixture rather than here.** This cell said *characterised but not diagnosed* for six days after somebody worked out the whole mechanism and wrote it into `PostgresFixture.cs`, where the bound that fixes it lives. **The fifth row this pass found describing a state the code had left behind**, and the one whose repair was hardest to find, because the answer was in a comment on a connection-string property rather than in any register. **The mechanism, and it is arithmetic rather than a theory.** The oracle classes read `public.planet_osm_polygon` — 6,499,215 rows, 2,240 MB — with `order by osm_id limit 500`. There is no index on `osm_id`, so that is a parallel sequential scan with a top-N sort: `explain (analyze, buffers)` reports **166,335 blocks**, which is 1,362 MB, and the container's virtual disk reads at **40.8 MB/s** uncached. 1,362 ÷ 40.8 = **33.4 s** — the observed 31–33 s failure, to the second. Warm, the same query is 1.4 s, which is why a rerun passes. **And why a build triggers it**: the database normally sits in the WSL2 virtual machine's page cache, a build's allocations put Windows under memory pressure, WSL2 hands the memory back, and the next read comes off the disk. So *the trigger is the build rather than the parallelism* — this row's own 2026-08-17 narrowing — was right, and this is why. **Repaired by a bound that fits the physics**: `CommandTimeout = 120`, which is 3.6× the measured cold read and still fails fast on a query that is genuinely not returning. **This row argued against raising it** on the grounds that it would hide contention — correct while the cause was believed to be contention between suites, and wrong once the cause turned out to be a known, bounded, one-off physical read whose size and rate are both measured. **Re-confirmed independently 2026-08-24, because a six-day-old diagnosis is a claim.** `pg_indexes` reports exactly one index on that table — `planet_osm_polygon_way_idx`, the spatial one on `way` — so the missing `osm_id` index the arithmetic depends on is still missing. And the reproduction this row describes was run: `obj` and `bin` deleted, a forced rebuild, then `dotnet test` immediately — **351 of 351 passed in 1 m 38 s**, zero failures, which is the bound doing its job. **What stays, and it is a note rather than a debt**: an index on `osm_id` makes the query instant and costs about 140 MB **on a table this server does not own and did not create**. That is the operator's call, and it is recorded here so that whoever meets a 31-second failure again finds the arithmetic instead of repeating it. The warning about CI retry stands and is better founded than when it was written: this is not flakiness to paper over, it is a physical read with a known size. **And the method note is kept** — the run must preserve failure messages; two earlier runs were piped through `grep -E "^(Passed\|Failed)!"` Three solution-wide runs that day: the first failed 4, the second failed 2 — `WkbAgainstPostgisTests.Ring_winding_matches_PostGIS` and `ArcGisWindingAgainstPostgisTests.Our_shell_winding_matches_ST_ForcePolygonCW_on_real_data`, the same two names as before — and the third, run when everything was already built, **passed all 1,083**. Both failures passed in isolation immediately afterwards. **So the trigger is the build rather than the parallelism**, which is the candidate this entry could not separate: an attempt to reproduce it by running two Postgres suites concurrently against the same database, with no rebuild in between, did not fail. The remaining candidates are all first-touch costs — cold page cache, JIT, pool warm-up — meeting a 30-second command timeout. **A method note that cost an hour twice:** the run must keep the failure messages. Both earlier runs were piped through `grep -E "^(Passed\|Failed)!"`, which discards them, so two occurrences produced no evidence beyond the test names. Reproducible on demand: build, then test. **DIAGNOSED 2026-08-18, and the mechanism is arithmetic rather than a race.** The three classes read `public.planet_osm_polygon` — **6,499,215 rows, 2,240 MB** — as `order by osm_id limit 500`. **There is no index on `osm_id`**: the only index on that table is `planet_osm_polygon_way_idx`, which is spatial. So the query is a parallel sequential scan with a top-N sort, and `explain (analyze, buffers)` reports **166,335 blocks read — 1,362 MB**. The container's virtual disk reads at **40.8 MB/s** uncached, measured with `dd iflag=direct` inside it. **1,362 ÷ 40.8 = 33.4 s**, against Npgsql's 30-second default `CommandTimeout` — which is the 31–33 s this row recorded twice, to the second. Warm, the same query is **1.4 s**, which is why a rerun and an isolated run both pass. **And why a build is the trigger, which is the part that made it look like a race.** The whole 5 GB database normally sits in the WSL2 virtual machine's page cache — the VM's working set is 6.3 GB. A build's allocations put Windows under memory pressure, WSL2 returns memory and drops that cache, and the next read comes off the virtual disk. Nothing about it involves parallelism, connections, or our `statement_timeout`, which is why every attempt to reproduce it by running suites concurrently failed. **BOUNDED rather than hidden.** The command timeout for both corpus-reading fixtures is now 120 seconds — 3.6× the measured cold read, and still fast enough to fail on a query that is genuinely not returning. This row said raising it would hide the contention, and that was written while the cause was *believed* to be contention; it is a known, bounded, one-off physical read whose size and rate are both measured above. **The alternatives were weighed and each costs something real:** restricting the read to a spatial envelope uses the `way` index and cuts the I/O by 8.7× (19,141 blocks, 418 ms, still 74 multi-ring polygons in 500) — but it narrows what an oracle test compares against and makes the sample depend on which extract somebody loaded, so the sample stays whole and the bound fits the physics. **The permanent fix is the operator's:** an index on `osm_id` makes this instant, at about 140 MB on a table this server neither owns nor created. **CI is untouched either way** — `planet_osm_polygon` does not exist there; `tools/ci-corpus.sql` seeds sixty generated polygons, which is why this has never been seen in a CI job and never will be. |
| D-42 | ~~**A mandatory statement timeout was removable by an unrelated setting.**~~ **CLOSED 2026-08-16.** ADR-007 §4.8 makes the per-connection `statement_timeout` mandatory, and `LayerConnections.BuildPool` applied it only when the registered connection string's `Options` was empty — deferring, the comment said, to *"an operator who has already set it"*. But `Options` carries every PostgreSQL server setting, not that one: registering a data source with `Options=-c application_name=qgis` silently removed the timeout for every layer on that source, forever, with nothing logged and nothing visibly different. Without it a single expensive query holds a pooled connection until the client gives up, and enough of them exhaust the pool for every other layer sharing the source. | 2026-08-12 (shipped), found and closed 2026-08-16 | Nothing. The intent was right and the test was wrong — *is this option set* was written as *is any option set*. | — closed | — closed | **CLOSED.** The check now asks whether `statement_timeout` itself is present, case-insensitively, and appends beside any other options. Extracted to `LayerConnections.WithStatementTimeout` so it is testable **without a database** — the old form could only be checked by reading it, which is most of why it stayed wrong. `StatementTimeoutTests`: seven cases, including the three that used to lose the timeout and the three where an operator's own value is still honoured. Found by the D-41 sweep, not by a failure. |
| D-41 | ~~**`havingClause` was SQL injection, anonymously reachable.**~~ **CLOSED 2026-08-16, the same hour it was found.** The FeatureServer query surface accepted ArcGIS's `havingClause`, checked only that it was non-empty and that `outStatistics` was present, and `PostGisFeatureSource.StatisticsAsync` appended it to the statement verbatim: `sql.Append(" having ").Append(query.Having)`. No parser, no allow-list, no bound parameters. `/query` is governed by service sharing, so on a layer shared to public the caller was **anonymous**. In `HAVING` position a subquery is legal, so this was at minimum blind read of everything the server's role could see. **What kept it alive is the part worth keeping:** a comment above it said the clause got *"the same treatment as `where`, which this server also passes through, so it is no wider a door; it is the same door"* — and `where` is **not** passed through. `WhereClause` parses it and re-emits our own SQL with every literal bound, which ADR-008 §4a states outright. The comment asserted an equivalence that made the hole look already-reasoned-about, and the §66 security gate on 2026-08-15 tested the where-clause parser and held, one parameter away. | 2026-08-15 (shipped), found and closed 2026-08-16 | Nothing about it was acceptable. It was not a trade; it was a wrong sentence in a comment that nobody had reason to doubt. | — closed | — closed | **CLOSED.** Refused at the boundary with a message saying why; `FeatureQuery` throws if one is constructed internally; `supportsHavingClause` is now `false` so the document stops advertising it; the query page's input is gone. Three regression tests: the boundary refusal, the constructor refusal, and `SqlStaysOutOfTheQueryModelTests`, which fails the build if any query-model member carries SQL again. **The capability itself is now absent** — that is [Q-109](open-questions.md), and it is a feature gap rather than debt. |
| D-40 | **ADR-008 states a rule it also documents breaking, and the shipped code follows the break.** §4.1 defines the query model by an exclusion — *"It must contain no SQL concepts. The moment it does, it stops being able to target GeoParquet, FlatGeobuf, or anything that is not a database"* — and §4a describes the parser it built as emitting parameterised SQL. `FeatureQuery.Where` is a `ParsedWhere(string Sql, IReadOnlyList<object?> Parameters)`, so the attribute filter enters the domain model already compiled. **The debt is the disagreement, not the missing abstraction:** an ADR that contains both sentences cannot be used to decide anything about the next provider. | 2026-08-15, recorded 2026-08-16 | The parser was the right call and the argument for it is in §4a: `where` accepted `1=1` and nothing else, which is not a FeatureServer with a gap but a FeatureServer nobody can use. Building a provider-neutral expression tree at the same time would have been an abstraction with one hypothetical consumer, which §82 forbids. What was skipped was not the abstraction but the paragraph reconciling the two sections. | **Before the first provider that is not a database is designed** — GeoParquet or FlatGeobuf as serving providers (Q-52), or DuckDB as the file-query engine (Q-81). Not after: the seam has to exist before something needs to read across it. | A provider design that assumes it can consume a `FeatureQuery`, discovers the filter is PostGIS SQL, and either grows a SQL parser of its own or refuses attribute filters entirely. Both are worse than the seam, and both get chosen under schedule pressure if this is not written down. Meanwhile every reader of ADR-008 has to work out unaided which of its two rules is live. | **RECORDED, deliberately unpaid.** Reconciled in ADR-008 §4a-i; `ParsedWhere` is pinned as the single exception by `SqlStaysOutOfTheQueryModelTests`, so a second one fails the build rather than quietly setting a precedent. |
| D-89 | **The conformance suite enumerates every service this server has, while three other suites create and delete `zz_*` fixtures beside it.** Any test that walks the services directory and then asks each service about itself reads a list that another suite is editing, so a fixture deleted between the listing and the query is a `404` reported against a server that is working. **It is a property of the pattern, not of one test, and two runs an hour apart proved that:** first `ArcGisConsistencyTests.Pages_do_not_overlap_or_skip` and `…A_polygon_ring_is_closed_and_wound_the_way_ArcGIS_requires`, both on `/rest/services/zz_scope_org`; then `MultiLayerServiceConformanceTests.Every_catalogued_layer_is_findable_in_the_services_directory` on `/rest/services/zz_scope_reach`. Every one passed alone within a minute, with no `zz_` service left on the server. **The same disease as [D-75](#), one layer up:** there it was one suite's own pages racing its own writes, here it is one suite's *read of global state* racing another suite's writes. | 2026-08-19, on the run that surfaced it | **Not acceptable so much as not yet diagnosed, and this row is the diagnosis.** The conformance suite's value is precisely that it asks the server what it has rather than being told, so narrowing it to a fixed list of services would remove the property being tested. What is wrong is that it reads a shared server while its neighbours mutate one. | Before D-63 is repaid. A suite that fails at random teaches its reader to run it again, and that lesson is the one thing a first green CI run must not teach | **Two false failures per full run, and they name a real endpoint returning a real 404.** A reader who trusts them looks for a serving defect; a reader who learns to ignore them will ignore the true one. | **CLOSED 2026-08-23, by a third repair neither of the two this row named.** The row's dilemma was real and both horns were right: a name skip cannot tell a deleted service from a broken one, and unsharing the server changes how every integration test runs. **So a 404 during a walk stopped being a verdict and became a question.** `ArcGisClient` gained `TryGetJsonAsync`, which returns null on 404 and still fails on anything else, and `StillListedAsync`, which re-reads the services directory. `AboutServiceAsync` puts them together: a service the catalogue no longer lists is skipped, and one it *does* still list fails the walk by name — *the catalogue is advertising a service the server will not serve*, which is the defect a conformance walk exists to find. The OGC face has the same pair against `/collections`. Costs one directory read per 404, twice a run, and nothing on a run where nothing vanishes. **The name skip stayed, and it stayed for a measured reason rather than the one this row rejected.** Removing it was tried first. With the fixtures walked, a full conformance run put **24 requests in flight against one data source with 96 more queued**, and the server shed load exactly as ADR-046 says it should: `ConnectionBudgetFullException`, 503, two tests failing on a healthy server. The fixtures roughly double how many services every walking test walks, and there are six of them. **And the row's objection does not hold for these names:** a `zz_` or `corpus_` service is not a class of service the walk would otherwise cover, it is created by a test that asserts on it directly with knowledge of exactly what it should contain, and deleted seconds later. Walking it adds no coverage and subtracts a stable suite. **Both halves are checked, because the failure mode of this repair is silence.** A `catch` that swallowed every 404 would make the suite green and useless and nothing in a green run would say so. `WalkRaceTests` asks a real service for layer 999 — present in the catalogue, genuinely absent — and asserts the walk fails with the service named; asks about a service nothing lists and asserts it is skipped; and pins the fixture rule against five real names, because a prefix list is a fact about other files and rots when they are renamed. **Found again the same day, in the shape the row predicted.** Two walks lost the race to `ShapefileCorpusTests` in one run — `One_box_in_two_reference_systems_selects_the_same_features` on `corpus_holed_…` and `A_polygon_ring_is_closed_and_wound_the_way_ArcGIS_requires` on `corpus_twoparts_…` — which is this row's `zz_scope_org` and `zz_scope_reach` with a different fixture prefix, eight days on. |
| D-90 | **Two console surfaces answered one page action, and the drawer it opened had grown four jobs.** Studio's *New layer* and Server's *New service* both called `openNewLayer`, which built one drawer holding a schema designer, a file import, a registered-table publisher and a service-and-group-layer form — four headings and five submit buttons in one scrolling column, with nothing saying they were alternatives. Its own comment had recorded the drift and then retitled the drawer rather than splitting it. Repaired 2026-08-19 by [ADR-034](adr/ADR-034-server-and-studio.md) §5j: a three-screen *New item* dialog for items, and Server's own action for services. **What is left is the part a rewrite does not fix** — `POST /admin/services` and the group-layer endpoints are now reachable from exactly one page action, and no test presses it. `ImportFormTests` walks the item route and three suites cover the endpoints directly; nothing covers the drawer. | 2026-08-19, with the restructure | **Acceptable because the coverage that matters did not move.** The endpoints keep their own tests, and the console route that broke twice this month — the import form — is the one now walked end to end. | The next change to either service form, or the first report that *New service* opens nothing | **A page action with no test is how D-83 and D-87 both shipped:** the capability was complete and the console could not reach it. This is the same gap left open deliberately and written down instead of found later. **CLOSED 2026-08-23.** `SurfaceTests` presses Server's own action, asserts the drawer opens with the service form in it, fills the name and submits, and asserts the request that reached the network: `POST /admin/featureservices`. **What the page tried to write, not what the server stored, and the difference is the suite's design rather than a shortcut.** `ConsoleTest` answers every non-GET from inside the page, so nothing is created on the operator's server to prove a form works — and the assertion is the stronger one anyway. The endpoint has its own tests; that this button reaches it is what nobody was checking. **The group-layer form in the same drawer is still unpressed**, and that is smaller than what this row opened: it is one form on a screen a test now walks, rather than a page action nothing touches. | closed |
| D-91 | ~~**Two elements carried one id, and one of them was inside a hidden view.**~~ `route()` wrote the surface's page action into both page heads — `#pageAction` on Server's services screen and `#pageActionContent` on Studio's content screen — and its comment gave the reason: *"naming them apart and asking for both is what keeps the router from having to know which view is visible."* It bought the router one line and left the document holding two `id="newLayer"` nodes. **Nobody had been bitten, which is why it lived since the router was written:** clicks read `event.target.id` and a person cannot press what they cannot see. `getElementById` returns the first in document order, which is the invisible one — the design review's own first script pressed it and timed out. Repaired 2026-08-19: the surface's home screen owns the slot, the other is emptied, and `AddItemDialogTests` asserts *exactly one, and it is the visible one* as a single atomic expression. | 2026-08-19, on discovery | **Not a compromise — it was never noticed.** Recorded rather than quietly fixed because the comment argued for it, so the next person to want the router simpler will find the cost written down beside the convenience. | repaid on discovery | **What it cost was one afternoon of somebody else's time**, and it would have cost more from a test: a suite that presses the invisible copy fails with *nothing matched* against a console that works. | ~~open~~ repaid 2026-08-19 |
| D-92 | ~~**Three log event ids named seven messages between them.**~~ `1008` was both `AuthorizationIsPortalShaped` and `OverlayKilled`; `1010` was `SetupStillPending` and `OverlayWorkersReclaimed`; `1014` was a datastore refusal, a query's timings and a configuration warning. Found 2026-08-19 while reading `Log.cs` for the next free number — which is the only reason it was found at all. **An id is what an operator filters on when they cannot read every line**, and one that returns three unrelated things is worse than none, because a number reads as an identity. Repaired the same hour: the first declaration of each shared number kept it so an existing filter still means what it meant, the later ones moved to 1017–1020, and `LogEventIdTests` now asks the assembly by reflection rather than the file by regular expression. | 2026-08-19, on discovery | **The same disease as the debt numbering in this very register**, which collided and took three entries to notice before the counting moved into a tool. A numbering convention nobody checks is not a convention. | repaid on discovery | **It would have been found at 2 AM**, by whoever filtered for one message and got three — §67's scenarios are graded on the log saying what to do next. | ~~open~~ repaid 2026-08-19 |
| D-93 | **The drawer does not move focus into itself, where the dialog beside it does.** Noted by the design review 2026-08-19: opening Server's *New service* leaves `document.activeElement` on the trigger button, outside `#drawer`. Escape closes it — that is an explicit handler and it works — so nothing is unreachable; what is missing is the focus management a native `dialog` gets for free, and which [ADR-034](adr/ADR-034-server-and-studio.md) §5j's dialog now has deliberately. | 2026-08-19, when the two surfaces stopped being the same drawer | **Acceptable because the drawer is not modal and is not trying to be.** §5j records why it stayed a panel: its two forms are read against the service list behind them. A panel you can still use the page beside is a weaker case for trapping focus than a modal. | The next drawer that holds a transaction rather than a form, or the first accessibility pass | **A keyboard operator reaches the drawer's fields by tabbing through the page behind it**, which on Server's services screen is a folder rail and a table. Not blocked, just long. | **CLOSED 2026-08-23, and half of it was already repaired when this was read.** The row says opening the drawer leaves `document.activeElement` on the trigger. It does not, and has not since the review that wrote the row — `openNewService` ends with `$("cName")?.focus()` and a comment saying why. The row was never amended, which is [D-130](#)'s shape inside a single register. **What was still true is the other end.** Closing the drawer sets `inert` on it while focus is inside, so the browser has nowhere to put focus and drops it on `<body>`: a keyboard operator restarts from the top of the page. **The same defect this console has already fixed once**, on the data-source edit form, where clearing the panel deleted the element that had focus — measured by the same review, repaired there, missed here. **So the drawer remembers what opened it and gives focus back**, if that control is still connected and still visible; `#app` carries `tabindex="-1"` as the fallback for when a submit has redrawn the list the button was in. Focus is still not trapped, which the row and the code both say is right for a panel you can use the page beside. **Verified by removing the restore from the served script:** the test waited ten seconds for focus to leave `<body>` and then said so. |
| D-94 | **The geodatabase inspector runs inside the serving process, where [ADR-016](adr/ADR-016-packaging-deployment-upgrade.md) §2 puts it in its own container.** `GeodatabaseInspector` is a `BackgroundService` in `Graticula.Host`: it claims jobs and spawns the reader as a child. The isolation that matters survives — GDAL is loaded by the child and `NativeDependencyTests` checks that in both directions — but the **loop** competes for the machine with the requests this process is serving, and a runaway archive is now a noisy neighbour rather than a separate container's problem. | 2026-08-19, with the first job worker | **Acceptable because [ADR-037](adr/ADR-037-job-workers-come-in-two-kinds.md) §5a already spent the image boundary and this is the same trade, not a new one.** A second container to run a loop that spawns a process the server already ships would be operational cost for a boundary the process boundary already provides. What it did not provide was a CPU or memory bound, and that was the part written down here. **Narrowed 2026-08-24: both bounds exist now, and neither is a container.** The child is started `BelowNormal`, so an import still competes for the machine and loses; and the parent polls the child's working set four times a second and kills it past `GeodatabaseReader.MemoryCeilingBytes` — 2 GB, counting native allocation, which is where a GDAL driver spends most of what it takes. What is left open is the container itself, which is what ADR-016 §2 asks for and what this row is named after. | The first deployment that serves and imports at the same time, or the first report of an upload slowing requests | **A geodatabase import can still degrade serving, and it can no longer take the machine.** The managed heap cap of 512 MB and the two-minute deadline never bounded GDAL's native allocation, and the class note said so rather than letting the environment variable imply otherwise; the working-set ceiling does. It is polled rather than enforced by the kernel, so a parser can allocate a gigabyte between two samples — measured cost and headroom in [benchmarks/reader-bounds](../benchmarks/reader-bounds/RESULTS.md): the child holds 38 MB loading GDAL, 54× under the ceiling, and one sample costs 0.6 µs. A job object and a cgroup would be exact and would be two platform-specific paths to keep in step. `ReaderResourceBoundTests` holds both halves; each was verified by removing it. | open, narrowed to the container |
| D-95 | **Nothing automated reads a real geodatabase, and nothing can without somebody's data.** `ArchiveFormatRefusalTests` now walks the whole pipeline — 202, job, scratch file, claim, child process, recorded failure — with four empty files in a `roads.gdb/` folder, so it carries no Esri bytes and asserts the interesting outcome: GDAL refuses it and the refusal is recorded rather than leaving a dead worker or a job stuck at queued. **What it cannot assert is that a real archive reads correctly**, and the corpus that would is the owner's client data, which does not enter this repository. | 2026-08-19 | **Acceptable, and the gap is narrower than it looks.** ADR-024 condition 2's lesson is that a reader is validated against files this project did not write — and the reader is, three archives, `docs/research/file-geodatabase-readers.md` §8f, measured by hand and written down. What is missing is the *repetition*, not the validation. | A public-domain geodatabase small enough to commit, with its licence recorded in DEPENDENCY-LICENSES.md — **and as of 2026-08-24 that is written down as a question with three shapes of answer rather than as a thing to hope for: [Q-138](open-questions.md)** | **A regression in the reader would be found by a person, not by the suite** — and §8f's own history shows how easily a by-hand measurement proves the adjacent thing: it was run against an extracted `.gdb` directory rather than the archive, and said the archive worked. | open |
| D-96 | **The job table is a claim surface with no worker identity and no protocol version.** `ClaimAsync` takes a `JobKind` and gives the row to whoever asks first — ADR-011 §3.2's `for update skip locked`, working exactly as designed. What it does not ask is *what kind of worker are you* or *which version of the request shape do you speak*. Found 2026-08-19 the expensive way: a Python worker built and reversed earlier the same day was still running in a Docker container three hours after its project was deleted, still polling the same database, and it claimed a real upload and failed it with `KeyError: 'archive'`. Two uploads succeeded and the third did not, because the race went the other way. | 2026-08-19, on discovery | **Not acceptable so much as not previously imagined.** One worker for one kind of work needs none of this; the moment there are two implementations of one kind — which there briefly were, on one machine — the store cannot tell them apart. | Before a second worker of any kind ships, and before D-63 makes CI run workers | **A failure that names nothing an operator can act on**, which is the one thing `IJobStore` refuses to store and this produced anyway: the reason came from the wrong worker, so it described a program nobody was running. Cost about forty minutes to trace to a container. | **CLOSED 2026-08-23, and the repair is the one this row named.** Migration 32 adds `job.claimed_by` and `job.protocol`; `ClaimAsync` takes what the claimant is and the highest shape it speaks, writes the first onto the row it takes and filters on the second; `CreateAsync` takes the shape it wrote. `/admin/jobs` reports both. **`claimed_by` is the diagnosis half and it is the half that would have saved the forty minutes.** The workers call themselves `graticula/import` and `graticula/inspect` with the machine and the process id, so a failed job names something an operator can find and stop. **`protocol` is the prevention half, and it binds only workers that read it.** Changing what `detail` holds and bumping the number leaves an un-updated worker unable to claim, instead of letting it claim and fail. **A foreign worker that ignores the column claims exactly as before** — that one is diagnosed by `claimed_by` and not prevented, and saying otherwise would be a guarantee this store cannot keep. Written into the column comment, the interface remark and the claim itself, because the three places somebody would look are the three places the limit has to be. **Three tests.** A claim records who took it, and the name survives the read the operator's screen makes rather than only the claim's own return. A worker that speaks 1 does not claim a job written at 2, **and one that speaks 2 takes the same row immediately after** — a filter that never matches is as broken as one that always does. And a claim with no name is refused rather than defaulted, because a blank there would put the column back where it started. **What is still absent is the lease**, which `IJobStore` already records: a job claimed by a worker that then dies stays `running` for ever. ADR-011 §3.3 names the reclaim sweep and this is not it — but a stuck job now says which program stuck it, which is what that sweep will need to report. |
| D-97 | ~~**Switching tabs on a service's settings page silently unchecked its capabilities, and Save wrote that.**~~ The tab handler re-derived the folder by running `splitService` over the **text of the breadcrumb's `<b>`**, and that element holds only the bare name — the folder is a separate breadcrumb segment. So `folder` came back null for every service not at the site root, the refetch asked for `/admin/services/{name}/capabilities?folder=`, the server answered 404, and `loadServiceCapabilities` threw before setting a field. The freshly drawn checkboxes stayed at their unchecked default under a toast reading *No service at the root*. **`saveServiceSettings` reads those same boxes**: open a foldered service, look at Limits, come back, press Save, and Feature access and Vector tiles are switched off on a live service. Found by a design review 2026-08-19 on `hosted/look_EarlyAlert`, which is public and has four layers. Repaired the same hour: `serviceOpen` holds what `showService` already knew. | 2026-08-19, on discovery | **Not a compromise — a bug, and the worst kind this console has had.** Recorded because the bug class is worth naming: a value that was in hand was thrown away and reconstructed from a string written for a person to read. `editing` holds the layer being edited for exactly this reason. | repaid on discovery | **Silent data loss on a public service**, with the wrong state visible and plausible. Nobody had reported it, which is the part that matters: there was no symptom to report until after Save. | ~~open~~ repaid 2026-08-19 |
| D-98 | ~~**Studio's only service page was unreachable, and so was the whole layer editor.**~~ Two findings with one cause each. *Sharing:* `SCREEN_SURFACE.service = "server"` forced every `#/service/...` onto Server before `servicePagesOf(surfaceOfPath())` was consulted, so `sharing` — Studio's, by owner decision 2026-08-17 — could never be in the page set, and both links this console offers to it landed on Capabilities with nothing to say the page had not opened. The table already carried a note saying `layer` was left out for exactly this reason. *The editor:* nothing anywhere linked to `#/layer/…` except the editor's own tabs. The service page's layer list was removed by owner decision 2026-08-18 and the comment replacing it claimed *"a layer's own page is reachable from Studio's content list and by its address"* — the second half was true. So Maintenance, Symbology, Caching, General and Endpoints were reachable only by typing an address, using a name no screen displayed. Both repaired 2026-08-19. | 2026-08-19, on discovery | **A route that is asserted in a comment rather than checked is the shape of D-83 and D-87**, and this is the third time. The repair restores what was claimed rather than reinstating the list the owner removed: a single-layer content row opens its layer, and a multi-layer one still goes to the service, because guessing which layer would open the wrong one's appearance settings. | repaid on discovery | **Five settings pages and one sharing page with no way in.** Everything worked; nothing could be reached. | ~~open~~ repaid 2026-08-19 |
| D-99 | **Symbology is the least visual page in a product about maps.** A `textarea` for a MapLibre style or an Esri `drawingInfo` pasted in as text, and a read-only `pre` showing the derived renderer as raw JSON — colour included, as an RGBA quad. ~~No swatch~~, no colour picker, ~~no sample geometry~~, no preview — **two of the four were built 2026-08-24** of what *Store* would produce. Caching's own Style section is the same shape. Design review 2026-08-19, whose verdict on *do the controls preview what they do* was a flat no. | 2026-08-19, named | **Acceptable because [ADR-004](adr/ADR-004-rendering-engine.md) is deferred**: there is no renderer to preview with, and a swatch that shows a colour the server will not draw that way is a promise. What is *not* excused is the RGBA quad — reading a colour out of four integers is work a swatch does for free and needs no renderer. | ~~The first cartographic work after ADR-004~~, or sooner for the swatch alone. **Both clauses have fired, and the first was misread until 2026-08-24**: [ADR-041](adr/ADR-041-the-map-renderer.md) *un-defers* ADR-004 in its own header and is `ACCEPTED WITH CONDITIONS`, [ADR-033](adr/ADR-033-symbology.md) is too, and there is a renderer serving WMS and MapServer. So *after ADR-004* is not a future date — it is where this project already is. | ~~**An operator sets a colour by typing numbers and cannot tell what they set.**~~ **They can tell, from 2026-08-24.** The colours a client will use are drawn as colours, shaped by the symbol kind, above the document they came from. | **CLOSED 2026-08-24, on the cost this row recorded.** Its cost cell was *an operator sets a colour by typing numbers and cannot tell what they set*, and they can tell: the derived renderer's colours are drawn **as colours**, above the JSON rather than instead of it, captioned with the label the renderer already carries and shaped by the symbol kind — a fill is a square, `esriSLS` a band, `esriSMS` a dot — with the RGBA quad on the chip's title for anybody checking a value against a specification. **Both clauses of the trigger fired, and the first was misread until today.** *The first cartographic work after ADR-004* is not a future date: [ADR-041](adr/ADR-041-the-map-renderer.md) un-defers ADR-004 in its own header and is `ACCEPTED WITH CONDITIONS`, [ADR-033](adr/ADR-033-symbology.md) is too, and a renderer serves WMS and MapServer. Reading it properly is what produced the sample geometry the same day. **The thing that would have been wrong is the alpha.** ArcGIS writes `[r, g, b, a]` with a in 0–255 and CSS wants 0–1, so passing the quad through unchanged clamps every opaque colour to 1 — correct-looking on every colour that was opaque anyway, wrong only on the rare half-transparent one. Measured over seven inputs, and a half-transparent fill reads as half transparent because the chip is a checkerboard with the colour painted over it. `The_symbology_page_draws_its_colours_and_scales_the_alpha` holds the swatches, the division, and the three symbol kinds. **Why this closes rather than waiting for a picker.** [CLAUDE.md §62](../CLAUDE.md): debt is a compromise with a trigger, and the compromise recorded here was that the page could not show what it held. It shows it. A colour picker and a live preview are an **editor**, which nobody has built and nothing pretends exists — the same reading that closed [D-26](#) and [D-34](#) today — and they are recorded in ADR-033 §5i beside the classification suggestion, which is where this decision already keeps what it has not built. **A picker is also a product decision rather than a task**: it means editing somebody's canonical MapLibre document on their behalf, which is §5g's line about what this is not, approached from the other side. |
| D-100 | **A privilege is named and never explained.** `GET /admin/roles` returns `{ name, administrative, requires, includes }` — there is no description field, front or back. A *role* gets a sentence; the eighteen privileges under it are bare identifiers with dependency notes. Design review 2026-08-19 credited the screen's structure — grouped by prefix, counts per group, dependencies enforced in both directions — and found the gap is meaning rather than legibility. | 2026-08-19, named | **Acceptable only because the identifiers are unusually self-describing** — `features:fullEdit` says most of it. It stops being acceptable at the first privilege whose name does not, and `admin:manageSecurity` is arguably already that one. | The next privilege added, or the first support question about what one does | **The screen an administrator reads to decide who can do what does not say what anything does.** **CLOSED 2026-08-23.** `Roles.DescriptionOf` gives each of the eighteen a sentence written for the person granting it rather than for the person who wrote it, `/admin/roles` carries it on every catalogue entry, and the role editor renders it under the identifier. **Beside the enum and behind a switch, for the reason `NameOf` is:** the compiler names a privilege that has been added and not described, so the row's trigger — *the next privilege added* — arrives as a build failure rather than as a support question. **Three tests, and each refuses a different way of passing without meaning anything.** One asserts every privilege answers with a real sentence rather than the identifier restated, which is what somebody adding a privilege in a hurry would write. One asserts no two are described identically — `features:edit` against `features:fullEdit`, `sharing:shareToOrganization` against `sharing:shareToPublic`, `admin:viewAllContent` against `admin:manageAllContent`, each pair differing in exactly the way the person granting them needs to understand. And one reads the sentences off the running screen, because a field that reaches the endpoint and is never rendered is the same gap with an extra field in it. **The row named `admin:manageSecurity` as arguably the first identifier that does not explain itself, and its sentence is written to that:** it says what the holder can change and, in the same breath, that it does not read content and can still decide who reaches it. | closed |
| D-101 | **Removing the only administrator walks through a side-effecting decision before being refused.** The server refuses it — `AdminEndpoints` says *"'{name}' is the only administrator who can still sign in"* — but the console offers no warning, and the panel it opens first asks whether to transfer or delete what they own. Choosing transfer **moves ownership of every service for real**, and only then is the removal refused. Design review 2026-08-19 read the code rather than walking it, deliberately, because root owns eleven services on the dev server. | 2026-08-19, named | **The safety net is genuine and server-side**, which is the right place for it. What is missing is that the console knows the answer before it asks the question: the member listing already carries who is an administrator. | Before the first deployment with two administrators, where the same flow becomes ordinary | **An irreversible side effect on the way to a refusal.** **CLOSED 2026-08-23, and walking it found that this row was wrong about the side effect and right about the screen.** The design review read the code rather than walking it, deliberately, because root owns eleven services on the dev server; walking it turned up three things reading it did not. **(i) The transfer is transactional.** `PostgresMemberDirectory.TransferAndRemoveAsync` runs its check inside the removal's own transaction and before the move, so a transfer that meets the refusal rolls back and moves nothing. The row's *moves ownership of every service for real* is not what happens. **(ii) The self-removal refusal comes first, and it is the one an operator meets.** `AdminEndpoints` refuses a member removing themselves before anything else — before the disposition is read and long before anything is deleted. A server with one administrator is the ordinary state of a fresh install, and the only person who can reach the Remove button on that administrator is that administrator. So the scenario as the row describes it ends in a refusal with nothing destroyed. **(iii) What is real is the ordering, on the other disposition.** The `deleteOwned` branch is not transactional and could not be: it unpublishes layers, purges tiles and deletes services and folders through the paths that own those side effects, each committing as it goes, and only then asks to remove the member. Any refusal at that point arrives after the estate is gone. So the same question is now asked before the dispositions run, from the member listing this handler has already read, and the transactional check stays because the pre-check races. **And a defect this row did not have, found by repairing it.** The last-administrator check counted administrators *who can still sign in* and then asked whether the member being removed holds the role — without asking whether they can sign in. So removing a **disabled** administrator was refused whenever one enabled administrator remained: the member being removed was not in the count, taking them away could not reduce it, and the refusal fired anyway, telling the operator that an account which cannot sign in *is the only administrator who can still sign in*. A fresh install has exactly one enabled administrator, which made this the ordinary case rather than a corner. Fixed in the query, and `LastAdministratorTests` stages it safely — a member it creates, grants the role, disables and removes, with nothing it touches existing before it ran. **Verified by reverting the fix:** the removal answered 409 with that sentence. **The console half is the row's own finding and it stands.** Two guards now answer before the panel opens: removing yourself, and removing the only administrator who can still sign in. Both were refused by the server and both opened a disposition panel first, offering to transfer or delete an estate when every answer was already *no*. Three console tests hold it, including one asserting the panel still opens when the server would allow the removal — a guard that refuses too often fails quietly. **Verified by removing the guard from the served script:** the panel opened and the toast never came. | closed |
| D-102 | ~~**A DNS or host failure registering a data source gets the least helpful message this server has.**~~ **FIXED 2026-08-19, and the cause was three layers earlier than this row said.** Both *Test connection* and *Register* answer *"The server failed to handle this request. The reason is in the server log; it is not repeated here because this endpoint is reachable without authentication."* — `ErrorResponse`'s catch-all. The same file classifies authentication failure and schema mismatch with specific, well-written sentences, so the mechanism is there and a bad hostname is simply not one of the classified cases. Design review 2026-08-19, measured with `Host=doesnotexist.invalid`. | 2026-08-19, fixed 2026-08-19 | **The reticence is deliberate and correct in general** — that endpoint is reachable unauthenticated and must not narrate the server's network. But an operator typing a connection string is authenticated and holds `admin:manageServer`, so the generic answer is protecting them from their own typo. **What this row got wrong is where the fault was.** It blamed `ErrorResponse`'s catch-all and a missing classification there — and a day spent adding a case to `ErrorResponse` would have put a second check beside a correct one. The real cause: `PostgresDataSourceProbe` caught `NpgsqlException`, and an unresolvable host throws **`SocketException (11001): No such host is known` bare**, not wrapped. So the probe never produced a `ProbeResult` at all — the exception left it, reached the global handler, and came back as *the reason is in the server log*. The catch now takes `SocketException` and `TimeoutException` beside `NpgsqlException`, and `Describe` names the two cases that need different actions: *no host by that name* (a typo, or a container not on this network) and *the host refused the connection on that port* (a port or a firewall). **This is the register's own rule arriving with an instance: a row is a lead, not a finding.** It fixes three endpoints at once — test, register, and the update below. | Verified on all three: `POST /admin/datasources/test`, `POST /admin/datasources`, `PUT /admin/datasources/{id}` | **The single most likely mistake on that form got the least useful answer.** | closed |
| D-103 | **The probe result table has no cap, where every other list in this console pages at ten.** Probing a source with 77 tables renders all 77 inline and takes the page past 5,000 pixels. Design review 2026-08-19. | 2026-08-19, named | **Acceptable at the measured scale and not at the stated one.** CLAUDE.md §7 targets 100–1,000 services; a registered database with hundreds of publishable tables is ordinary, and `PAGE_SIZE` exists for exactly this. Left alone tonight because the publish form reads its selection out of that table and paging it is a change to a working flow rather than a wrapper. | The first source with more than about fifty tables | **A screen that grows without bound on a server doing nothing unusual.** **CLOSED 2026-08-23.** The probe's table pages at ten through the same `pageOf`/`pagerFor` the rest of the console uses, with a filter above it: seventy-eight tables is eight page turns to find one, so a cap without a way past it would have traded one unusable screen for another. **The row's reason for leaving it alone does not apply.** It said the publish form reads its selection out of that table — it does not. The form reads the `probed` map and a `<select>` built in a different function; this table is informational and nothing reads rows from it. **It is the one paged list that slices rather than re-reads, and that is a decision.** Every other list re-runs its loader on a page turn so the page you turn to is as fresh as the page you refresh onto. A probe opens a connection to somebody else's database on the operator's explicit instruction, and turning a page is not that instruction — so the result is held and the arrows move through it. The heading no longer says *probed just now*, which would have gone on being said on page eight. **The filter box lives outside the redrawn block**, because the one place this console got that wrong is recorded in `console.js` as a defect twice over. A test marks the element before typing and asserts the mark survives. **Measured on the dev server, which answers with 78 tables from its own datastore** — so the row's *77* case needed no staging. Verified by removing the cap from the served script: the test named the count and the page. | closed |
| D-104 | **Publishing into a service somebody else owns is allowed, by name.** `PostgresAdminCatalog.PublishLayerAsync` puts the layer in the existing service when the name and folder match — which is the mechanism that lets three layers share one service, working as designed — and it never asks whose service that is. So `POST /admin/publish` with `serviceName` set to a stranger's service adds a layer to it, and the caller needs only `content:publishFeatures`. Found 2026-08-19 while writing the geodatabase publish endpoint, which refuses a taken name rather than adding to it. | 2026-08-19, named | **Nobody has hit it, and the shape of the hole is narrow:** the layer arrives with its own sharing scope and its own owner, so it is not a way to read anything — it is a way to put a layer inside a container somebody else is responsible for. The sharing scope of the container is explicitly *not* reset (that arm is guarded and commented). | The first deployment with two publishers | **A publish can modify a service the caller does not own.** **CLOSED 2026-08-23.** `PublishAsync` asks whose service the request names before anything else happens: a service with an owner who is not the caller is refused 403, unless the caller holds `admin:manageAllContent` — which is the privilege for exactly this, and refusing it would mean the only way for an administrator to reorganise somebody's services is to change the owner first. A service with no owner is nobody's and is left alone; the seeded ones predate ownership. **Both halves are tested, with a publisher this suite creates and removes and a data source that does not exist** — ownership is decided before the table is looked at, so proving the refusal needs no table, and a test that published a real layer into somebody's service to prove it cannot would be a test that does the thing it checks against. The other half asserts an administrator is *not* refused, because a guard that refuses everybody fails quietly: publishing a second layer into your own service would simply stop working. **Verified by disabling the check:** the publisher's layer landed in root's service and the server answered 201. **And that experiment is what found [D-147](#d-147),** because the layer it created did not exist. | closed |
| D-105 | ~~**Two column names that agree for sixty characters became one column, and the import died on PostgreSQL's own error.**~~ `PostGisImporter.ColumnNameFor` cut a property name at sixty characters with nothing to distinguish it from the next name cut to the same sixty, so `create table` was written with a duplicated identifier: `42701: column "fid_aecom_arch_previouslyassessedarchaeolgicalassessmentarea" specified more than once`. **Found in real data, not imagined:** publishing the owner's 55-layer archive 2026-08-19 refused exactly one feature class for exactly this, and the message an operator got was a PostgreSQL error code about an identifier they never wrote. **Fixed the same day** — a truncated name now carries eight hex characters of a SHA-256 of the whole one, deterministic across processes where `GetHashCode` is not, and names short enough to keep are untouched byte for byte. `PostGisImporterTests.Two_field_names_that_agree_for_sixty_characters_become_two_columns` reproduces the 42701 against the old rule. | 2026-08-19, fixed 2026-08-19 | **What remains is an asymmetry, not a defect:** a hosted table created before today keeps whatever truncated name it was given, and a fresh import of the same data would now name that column differently. Nothing addresses a hosted column by re-deriving it — the four call sites are all inside the import — so nothing breaks; but two tables from the same source can disagree, and that is worth knowing before somebody compares them. | If a hosted table's columns are ever re-derived rather than read | **A column name is a name, and two of them were the same name.** | closed |
| D-106 | ~~**An empty feature class cannot be published, although the archive declares its schema.**~~ **FIXED 2026-08-19, hours after it was named.** `GeodatabaseImporter` builds a hosted table's columns by observing the features it reads, which is how every other import path works — so a layer with no features has nothing to build from and is refused with the layer named. But a geodatabase is not GeoJSON: the reader's header already carries the field list with types and the geometry type, which is exactly what `POST /admin/hosted/define` takes. The owner's 55-layer archive holds one of these (`AECOM_Monitoring_Well_Inventory`), and ArcGIS publishes it. The console no longer offers the tick, so the refusal now happens before anything runs. | 2026-08-19, fixed 2026-08-19 | **A layer with no rows looked like the least urgent thing in an archive**, and the work was the type map it needed: OGR field type names to `FieldType`, WKB geometry names to `GeometryKind`. Both fall back rather than refuse — an unknown field type becomes `Text`, which is what `FieldType.Unknown` already means everywhere in this server, and `25D` is dropped because that is what the import does with the geometry itself (D-107). Only a layer whose *geometry* type is unstorable is still refused, which is an attachment table. **Every field is nullable, and that is a decision rather than ignorance:** OGR reports a geodatabase's *required* flag, but it is Esri's notion enforced by Esri's editors, so a column declared `not null` here would refuse rows the source itself holds. **Measured: the owner's 55-layer archive now publishes 55 of 55 in 32 seconds**, and the empty one lands as a point layer with nine fields answering `{"count":0}` — where it was one of two refusals that morning. | Closed | **This server could read a schema it would not use.** | closed |
| D-107 | **Elevation in the source is dropped and only counted.** Six of the eight layers in the owner's smallest archive are `25D`. `WkbReader` drops Z and says it did, the hosted table is 2D, and the publish job now reports per layer how many features carried an elevation — 3,659 of 3,659 in one case. Nothing is written back to the archive, so `docs/geometry-crs-policy.md`'s *lossy on read means not writable* is not broken. | 2026-08-19, named | **v1 stores 2D and says so** ([v1-scope](v1-scope.md)), so this is scope rather than accident — and the count in the job is the difference between *this server stores 2D* and *your data had elevations and they are gone*. | Whenever 3D storage is decided, or the first user who notices their contours flattened | **A silent loss became a reported one, which is not the same as no loss.** | open |
| D-108 | **The pipe streams and the importer does not, so the million-feature ceiling has not moved — it has changed owner.** [ADR-038](adr/ADR-038-how-a-geodatabase-becomes-a-service.md) §4B chose a line-per-feature wire to escape `ImportLimits.Default`'s **1,000,000 features / 50 MB / 250 columns**, and it does: nothing about the wire has a ceiling. `GeodatabaseImporter` then collects those features into an `ImportedDataset`, because that is what `PostGisImporter.ImportAsync` takes — so the bound is now this server's memory rather than a format's limit, which is a smaller claim than the ADR made. The other two import paths are unchanged: a shapefile and a GeoJSON upload are still read whole and still capped at a million features and 50 MB. | 2026-08-19, named, and it is ADR-038 condition 1 restated as a debt | **Nothing measured is anywhere near it.** The largest layer in the owner's three archives is 3,659 features; the whole 55-layer archive is 16,806 features and 28 seconds. What would discharge it is an `IAsyncEnumerable<ImportedFeature>` overload on the importer — a Tier 1 change, and a decision of its own rather than a patch. | The first archive with a layer past a million features, which is an ordinary size for a national road network | **A ceiling that was reported as lifted was relocated.** | open |
| D-109 | **A layer is addressed by its bare name, and nothing makes that unique.** `PostgresLayerCatalog.FindAsync(name)` is `where l.name = @name order by s.name limit 1` — two layers with the same name in different services, and the one whose service sorts first wins, silently. Every `/admin/layers/{name}/…` endpoint and the console's `#/layer/{name}` route go through it, so an operator can open the settings of a layer they were not looking at. Found 2026-08-19 while building the geodatabase publish. | 2026-08-19, named | **It has been true for a long time and it is now much easier to hit.** One archive publishes 55 layers under names their owner chose; an Esri estate has `Segment_Boundary` in three archives, and before today a name collision needed two deliberate publishes. The `limit 1` is not a bug on its own — it stops a duplicate throwing — it is the *silence* that makes it one. | The first deployment that imports two archives from the same project | **The wrong layer can be edited, and nothing says so.** **CLOSED 2026-08-24, and repairing it found something worse than the row describes.** **`UnpublishLayerAsync` was `delete from layer where name = @name`.** With two layers of one name it removed **both**, while the endpoint purged the tiles of one — so the row's *the wrong layer can be edited* understates it: the wrong layer could be **deleted**, along with the right one. It takes the catalogue id now. Every one of its three callers already held the layer — they read it for the tile purge, the cache eviction and the audit line — so the ambiguity was at the only place it could destroy something and cost nothing to remove. **And an ambiguous name is refused rather than resolved by sort order.** `PostgresLayerCatalog.NamedAsync` answers with every layer carrying a name; `OneNamedLayerAsync` turns two into a 409 that names the services and says `?service=` — the operator knows which one they meant and the server does not, so the useful thing is to say what the choices are and how to express one. The relationship declaration refuses too, with its own message: a relationship declared against the wrong one of two same-named layers queries the wrong table for ever after and nothing about it looks wrong. **`FindAsync` stays, and its remark now says what it is for.** A read that needs *a* layer of this name — a cache eviction, a shape lookup — is served correctly by the first match. A write is not. **The schema is where the asymmetry lives, and the fixture is built out of it.** `layer_table_unique` covers (data source, schema, table, geometry column) and refuses a table published twice; `layer_name_unique_in_service` is per *service* and allows the same name in two. So `AmbiguousLayerNameTests` registers two tables the probe says nothing publishes, under one name, in two services it creates and removes. **Verified by removing both guards:** the refresh answered 200 about a layer nobody named, and the delete left the count at zero where the test expected one. **What is not repaired is the console's `#/layer/{name}` route**, which still addresses a layer by bare name — it now meets the same 409 instead of opening the wrong settings, which is the safe half. Giving it the service in the address is a change to how the console links to layers, and it is [D-115](#)'s screen rather than this row's. | closed |
| D-110 | **Two background workers polling every two seconds hold eight database sessions open for ever, on a server doing nothing.** Measured 2026-08-19 ([connection-budget](../benchmarks/connection-budget/RESULTS.md)): after load stopped, the pools pruned correctly — 79 backends to **zero** in 184 seconds — and then settled at **16 and stayed**, of which **8 last ran `IJobStore.ClaimAsync`**. `GeodatabaseInspector` and `GeodatabaseImporter` each claim every two seconds when idle, round-robin over the platform-store pool, so every connection in it is touched again long before `ConnectionIdleLifetime` expires and none can ever age out. The minimum idle time across the sixteen was two seconds, which is the poll. **A pool that prunes correctly cannot prune one somebody keeps knocking on.** | 2026-08-19, measured | **Eight sessions is nothing on a 200-connection database and it is not nothing on the arithmetic**: ADR-007 §4.8's whole point is that the budget is per worker and per data source, and this is a floor that no configuration lowers and that **grows with the number of job kinds** — each background service polls independently, so a third worker kind adds to it. It also makes *shrink toward a floor of zero* untrue in exactly the state the policy is written for: an idle server. | The third job kind, or the first deployment where the database's session count is billed | **The idle floor is set by our own polling, not by the workload.** **PARTLY REPAIRED 2026-08-23, and the part that is not is measured rather than assumed.** **The poll backs off.** A claim that finds nothing doubles the wait, two seconds to a ceiling of thirty, and any work puts it back — so a busy server behaves as it did and an idle one knocks fifteen times less often. **The backoff would have been latency, and that is what `JobSignal` is for**: a job enqueued in this process releases the worker's wait at once, so half a minute is the worst case only for work another node queued, which is the case the poll exists for. **Deliberately not `LISTEN`/`NOTIFY`** (§82): that reaches every node and costs each worker a connection held open for ever, which is this row's own complaint arriving from the other side. **Measured today rather than restated.** The row's sixteen-and-eight was taken with a warm pool after load. Five minutes after a full suite run, before the change: **1 idle backend, last statement the claim, idle 1.4 s** — the same mechanism at a smaller scale, and the same reason it cannot age out. After: **2 idle backends, both the claim's, idle 26 s.** The knocking is fifteen times rarer; the floor is still there. **And a second change was tried and taken back out, which is the part worth keeping.** `ConnectionIdleLifetime = 20` on the platform-store pool, on the reasoning that a lifetime under the poll would let it prune to zero. Measured: **2 idle backends, idle 13.4 s** — the poll returns the connection and takes it again inside the window the pruner needs, so the floor moves from *held for ever* to *churned*, which is not what the number was for. It is not in the code; a setting that does not do what it was added for is worse than the default, and the reasoning is in `Program.cs` where somebody would otherwise add it again. **What is left is the pollers sharing that pool at all.** The repayable form is their own data source, bounded at one connection per worker kind, so the shared pool can reach the floor of zero ADR-007 §4.8 claims and the polling cost is one connection per kind by construction rather than by luck. That is a change to how the workers get a connection rather than to how often they ask, which is why it is recorded here instead of guessed at. `JobSignalTests` holds five properties of the wait: a wake ends it early, a wait nobody wakes times out, a wake reaches only its own kind — waking the wrong worker would put the two-second poll straight back — wakes do not pile up, and cancelling ends it without throwing at a worker that is shutting down. **REPAID 2026-08-24, and the remaining half is the form this cell already named.** The pollers hold their own `NpgsqlDataSource`, keyed, sized `Enum.GetValues<JobKind>().Length` and named `graticula-jobs` so a session can be attributed rather than recognised from its statement text. The request path keeps the shared store: enqueuing happens inside a request that already holds a shared connection, and putting it behind a pool sized for two pollers would put every operator's upload behind them. Measured, twenty seconds at 24 clients then five minutes of watching ([connection-budget](../benchmarks/connection-budget/RESULTS.md)): **the shared pool reaches zero**, 46 → 46 → 0, and the pollers' pool holds 2. Verified by taking the wiring back out — same script, same load: the shared pool stops at **2**, both of them idle 18 s, which is the backoff interval, which is the knocking seen directly. `PollerPoolTests` holds three properties and two of them fail under that reversal. **The floor is not zero and is not claimed to be: it is one connection per job kind, by construction rather than by luck**, which is what §4.8's arithmetic asks for. | repaid 2026-08-24 |
| D-111 | **The conformance suite fails on its own fixtures, because xUnit runs test classes in parallel.** `ContentScopeConformanceTests` publishes `zz_scope_org` and `zz_scope_reach`, shares them, reads them back and removes them. `ArcGisDiscoveryTests`, `ArcGisConsistencyTests` and `MultiLayerServiceConformanceTests` each begin by asking the catalogue what exists and then opening every service — so they meet a fixture mid-creation and report *404, an ArcGIS client stops here* about a service that was never meant to outlive one test. **Fixed 2026-08-19 in three passes, and each pass found a different mechanism.** (1) An xUnit collection serialises the classes that walk the catalogue against the ones that publish into it — four at first, then two more (`HostedDeleteTests`, `ArchiveFormatRefusalTests`) when a later run failed on `zz_drop_…`, which is why the collection's own note now states the rule rather than listing the members. (2) That is only within one assembly: `dotnet test graticula.sln` runs the **test projects in parallel**, so the console suite filtered the services list while the conformance suite published into it. `dev-server.sh test` now runs the nine projects one at a time — twelve minutes instead of five, and serialising also made each suite dramatically faster in itself (Host 2 m 48 → 13 s, Conformance 3 m 39 → 46 s), because they had been contending for the same server all along. (3) The last failure was not a race at all and reproduced in isolation: `.empty` names two things in this console — the empty-list `<td>` and the placeholder `<div class="thumb empty">` a row shows when its service has no cover — and three tests used the loose selector as their oracle for *is this list empty*. In `ListPagingTests` that made a correct assertion unsatisfiable; in `AddItemsPageTests` and `ContentScopesPageTests` it would have read a full page as an empty one. All three now say `td.empty`. | 2026-08-19, mostly fixed | **A collection rather than a filter on the walk**, because skipping names that look like fixtures would hide the one thing those tests exist to check — that *every* service in the catalogue can be opened — and would keep hiding it after somebody published a real service called `zz_anything`. **Three green runs of all nine suites end on end** — 1,360 tests — where the morning's runs failed a different one or five each time. The cost was never test time: it is that a run's failures were twice attributed to the wrong cause, once in a commit message that had to be corrected. **What stayed open was that nothing *detected* a new class which publishes and forgets the collection, and nothing stopped the next reused class name from becoming somebody's oracle. Both were conventions written down, which is weaker than a check. REPAID 2026-08-24 — `SuiteStabilityTests`, two source walks, no server needed.** The first accepts either of the two mechanisms this repair actually arrived at: a publishing class is in the collection, or what it publishes carries a fixture prefix so `ArcGisClient.Fixture` skips it. The second is not *no bare class selectors*, because twenty-five of the console's classes are on more than one kind of element and nearly all of them should be — `mono`, `tiny`, `primary` are styling. It forbids selecting one of **those** by class alone, and it reads which ones they are from the console's own source rather than from a list that would have to be kept in step. Both verified by breaking them: a file publishing `probe` fails the first, `.empty` in place of `td.empty` fails the second and names both elements it could mean. | The next class that publishes into the catalogue | **A failure set that changes between runs of unchanged code teaches whoever reads it to ignore failures.** | repaid 2026-08-24 |
| D-112 | ~~**The closed drawer kept a focusable button off screen.**~~ **FIXED 2026-08-19.** `aria-hidden="true"` tells a screen reader to ignore a subtree and says nothing about the tab order, so `#drawerClose` stayed a tab stop while CSS had translated its container off-canvas — measured by the design review at **x=1986 in a 1440-pixel window**, and `offsetParent` is non-null there, so the check this repository relies on does not catch it. Found by tabbing past the Data sources screen's new buttons, which is one stop upstream. `inert` now goes on and off with `aria-hidden`, and the markup ships inert. | 2026-08-19, fixed 2026-08-19 | **The interesting part is the detection, not the fix.** This is the fourth existing-but-invisible control in this console, and the first that `offsetParent !== null` could not see — it is inside a container that *has* a layout box and is merely translated away. A bounding-rect check against the viewport is what caught it. Every future review of a hidden region needs that, not `offsetParent`. | The next off-canvas panel | **A reader could tab into something they had been told was not there.** | closed |
| D-113 | **Our own shapefile parser runs in the serving process, and the reason it had to stopped existing on 2026-08-18.** 1,094 lines — `ShapefileReader.cs` (860) and `ShapefileBundle.cs` (234) — parse ZIP members, SHP geometry and DBF attributes **inside the process that serves public requests**, called from `HostedDataEndpoints.cs`. ADR-024 decided that on 2026-08-15, when this project had no GDAL and a rule that archives are never opened. Three days later ADR-037 §5a reversed itself and shipped GDAL in `Graticula.Import.Reader` — a child process whose stated purpose, quoted in `Directory.Packages.props`, is that it *"removes an untrusted-file parser from the process that serves public requests"* — and GDAL's Shapefile driver has been sitting in it since. **Nothing went back to ask whether ours was still needed**, so the newer format is isolated and the older one is not. Found by the independent §66 Simplicity gate, 2026-08-19. | 2026-08-19, found | **This is a security-posture inconsistency rather than a defect**: the parser is bounded, fuzzed by its own corpus and has a winding-order fix behind it, and nothing is known to be wrong with it. What is wrong is that two archive formats from the same untrusted upload are parsed on opposite sides of a boundary drawn on purpose. **Not free to remove:** the reader's protocol needs a shapefile entry point (~50 lines over an existing GDAL path) and ADR-024's operator-supplied DBF encoding must cross the pipe in the header. `BoundedArchive` stays — the upload bounds are ours either way. | Before the next format is added to either path | **The rule that moved GDAL out was not applied to the parser it was written about.** | **CLOSED 2026-08-23. The parse is in the child process and the 860-line parser is deleted.** `ShapefileViaReader` asks `Graticula.Import.Reader` for the layer and streams its features; `WkbReader` turns them into our model, the same `InferredColumn` infers the columns that the old parser and the GeoJSON path both feed, and `ShapefileReader.cs` and its 460 lines of tests are gone. **`ShapefileBundle` stays and that is deliberate**: it assembles the archive's members, refuses a bundle with two shapefiles, and decides the character set — our bounds on an upload, which have to be applied before anything leaves our hands.
