It reads two bytes of the DBF header to find the record area for that decision, and that is the whole of what still parses here. **Three things had to be measured rather than assumed, and each changed the design.** *(i)* The operator's character set has to cross the pipe per request. `SHAPE_ENCODING` as a GDAL config option: no effect, `turkish_undeclared.zip` came back as mojibake with it set and without. The same name in the process environment at start: works, and is useless, because this process is long-lived and shared — and setting it from inside does not work either, CPL reads the environment first. `ENCODING` as an **open option** is per open, so `Ogr.Open` became `Gdal.OpenEx`. *(ii)* The layer's geometry type is what the features are, not what the header declares. GDAL reports a shapefile polyline layer as `wkbLineString` and emits a `MultiLineString` for a multi-part record, so three of eight corpus archives were refused by PostGIS with *Geometry type (MultiLineString) does not match column type (LineString)*. The widest kind seen wins now. *(iii)* **`OGR_ORGANIZE_POLYGONS=DEFAULT`, and this one is the finding.** GDAL's fast default for shapefiles trusts the ring winding; some real files wind the OGC way, and read that way a hole becomes a second overlapping shell. Measured on the corpus: PostGIS reported **47 of 50 valid** — *hole lies outside shell; nested shells* — which is the exact failure the deleted parser was written to avoid and whose number its own tests recorded. With the containment analysis it is 50 of 50. **Without that measurement this repair would have shipped a correctness regression while closing a debt.** **One behaviour changed, deliberately and visibly.** A polygon record holding a single shell used to publish as `MultiPolygon`, because the old parser wrapped every record; it publishes as `Polygon` now. Both are true readings, the narrower one is better, and it is a visible change to a layer document — so `ShapefileCorpusTests` names it twice, on `holed` and on `osm_real`. **That class is where the corpus lives now**, asserted through the live server rather than against a class that no longer exists: eight archives with their geometry type, row count and field list, the undeclared-encoding refusal, and the three adversarial archives. Writing it found two more defects of its own — a cleanup that used a route that does not exist and left 54 services behind, and a hole in `OgcExtentConformanceTests` that blamed the filter for a feature with no geometry not being inside a box. |
| D-114 | **`GeoCoordinateString.cs` is a second coordinate engine in Tier 1, and ADR-022 §4 refuses one by name.** 963 lines implementing DD/DDM/DMS/UTM/MGRS/USNG, including a hand-written transverse Mercator forward and inverse to sixth order. §4 rejected a .NET projection library in terms that could not be plainer — *"two coordinate engines with two EPSG datasets and two sets of grids, differing by metres on exactly the cadastral and survey work where metres are legally significant — and differing silently, because both would answer"* — and §2c ships one anyway, reclassified as *"a closed-form series"* so that §4 appears not to apply. Found by the independent §66 Simplicity gate, 2026-08-19. | 2026-08-19, found | **The problem it solves is a checklist gap, not a workflow:** two operations returned 404 in a feature comparison against another product, and nobody had reported wanting them. Correctness was verified once against PostGIS on ten points and nothing re-checks that the two engines still agree — which is precisely the silent divergence §4 predicted. **The honest form is the one §4 chose for `project`:** push the UTM leg to `ST_Transform` into EPSG:326nn/327nn (what the tests already compare against) and keep only the ~150 lines of MGRS/USNG lettering and DMS formatting PostGIS has no function for. A 6:1 reduction with the second engine deleted. | Before anything else in the repository computes a projection | **A decision's central argument was set aside by reclassifying the thing it forbids.** | **CLOSED 2026-08-23. The second engine is deleted and there is one left.** 92 lines of transverse Mercator forward and inverse are gone, with `F`, `K0` and `FalseEasting`, and the UTM leg goes through `IProjector` into the EPSG:326nn/327nn codes the tests already compared against. `GeoCoordinateString` holds a *notation* now: it says which zone a coordinate falls in, it letters and packs and parses, and it is handed a `GridPosition` for the grid notations rather than computing one. A grid notation with no position is refused by name — **inventing one there would put the engine back a line at a time.** **Verified end to end on the running server, both directions.** Washington `-77.0353, 38.8895` writes `18S UJ 23478 06483` and reads back to `-77.035295, 38.889502`; Sydney round-trips through the southern codes with the ten-million-metre northing intact; Ankara's UTM string returns to six decimal places. Sub-metre in every case, which is the precision the string itself carries. **This row's arithmetic was wrong and the correction matters.** It said 1,094 lines and a 6:1 reduction to *~150 lines of MGRS/USNG lettering and DMS formatting*. The file was 963 and is 954: the engine was 92 lines of it and the notation work is about 850, not 150. **What was removed is not measured in lines** — it is that two engines answered the same question and could differ silently, which is exactly what ADR-022 §4 refuses. **One number remains from the old file and it is documented as a trap.** `A`, the WGS84 semi-major axis, is used in one place: picking which two-million-metre repetition of the MGRS row cycle a latitude falls in, which only has to be right to within a million metres. Its remark says that a second use of it means the engine is growing back. **The consequence ADR-022 §4 accepted is now real here too.** These two operations need the datastore, where they did not before — A-060's shape, and the endpoint says so in the refusal rather than failing with a database error. **And the tests changed subject rather than being deleted.** `Utm_matches_PROJ_to_the_millimetre` retired with the series it watched — PROJ is the answer now, so pinning our answer against it is pinning it against itself. What replaced it is a test of the zone rule with its Norway and Svalbard exceptions, which is the part that stayed ours and the part a converter written from the definition alone gets wrong. The round trips now walk the whole path, projection included. |
| D-115 | **The Server/Studio surface split is the single cause of four recorded console defects.** ADR-034 gave the console two surfaces with three parallel screen-ownership tables (`SCREEN_SURFACE`, `SERVICE_PAGES`, `LAYER_PAGES`) and a router that must consult the right one per path. D-90 (two surfaces answered one page action), D-91 (two elements carried one id, one inside a hidden view), D-93 (the drawer does not move focus) and D-98 (*Studio's only service page was unreachable, and so was the whole layer editor*) all trace to it, and three consecutive code comments in `console.js` warn about forgetting to add a screen to the table — one of them recording that the failure had already happened. Found by the independent §66 Simplicity gate, 2026-08-19. | 2026-08-19, found | **The gate's argument is that §5b already concedes it:** the split was justified on jobs-not-permissions (*"the operator watches a server, the publisher shapes content"*) with no user evidence, while §5b makes the actual gate **a privilege the API already enforces** — and if the gate is a privilege, ADR-034's rejected Alternative A is the same product with one router. ADR-034 also states Alternative B's counterargument correctly (*"two places for the same fact to live, which is D-46's recurring debt"*) and chose it anyway. **But this one is not the gate's to settle:** the two surfaces are an owner decision, taken with screenshots in hand and corrected twice since, and *"sana verdiğim ekranların tamamını studio gibi davran"* is a sentence about which surface owns what. So it is recorded as a cost with a named alternative rather than as work to do. | The next defect whose cause is the split | **Four defects, one cause, and a design that concedes the cause in its own text.** **Amended 2026-08-23: the split stays and the failure it keeps producing is now a test.** Unifying the surfaces would reverse an owner decision, which is what this row already says and why it is not work to do. What was work to do is the *shape* of the four defects: a screen missing from one of the three tables does not throw, it falls through to its surface's home, so the address is accepted and the wrong page draws with nothing said. `ScreenOwnershipTests` reads `SURFACES`, `SCREEN_SURFACE` and the page's own `.view` ids out of the running console and asserts three things: every tab a surface offers is owned by that surface, every entry names a screen the console can actually draw, and `service` and `layer` stay out of the single-owner table because their ownership is per page. **Each of the three was checked by breaking it.** Removing `groups` reproduces D-98's sibling and the failure names the address — *`/server/#/groups` falls through to that surface's home with nothing said*; adding `service: "server"` reproduces D-98 itself; an entry for a screen with no view is named. **And the first draft of the second check was wrong in a way worth keeping written down.** It asserted every entry is a tab somewhere, and failed on `group` — correctly present, correctly not a tab, because `#/group/planning` is an address without one. The rule is whether `openScreen` can show `view-{screen}`, not whether a tab points at it. **The row stays open**: three tables still have to agree, and a test that catches the disagreement is not the same as not having it. | open |
| D-116 | **The repository holds more prose than code, and the same fact is restated in three registers.** ~46,000 lines of prose against ~31,000 of code: 18,786 of `src/`'s 56,493 C# lines are comment (33%), with `IAdminCatalog.cs` at 73% and `Privilege.cs` at 53%, beside `docs/` at 27,039 of which 15,197 is ADRs. Found by the independent §66 Simplicity gate, 2026-08-19, which judged most of it load-bearing and named the pattern rather than the volume: a decision now typically appears in an ADR body, an inline `<remarks>`, and a debt row. | 2026-08-19, found | **The prose is why this project is readable by whoever picks it up, and the gate said so.** The cost is not length; it is **restatement**, which fails the same way duplicated code does and has already: CLAUDE.md records a tally that *"was wrong for a day"* and moved the number out of itself for exactly this reason, `tools/registers-check.py` exists to fail the build when a count reappears there, and D-46's pattern has six recorded instances. One fact, one home, with links from the others is the rule; nothing enforces it beyond that single check. **A second one exists as of 2026-08-24, and it is the row's own trigger made mechanical.** The trigger reads *the next time two documents disagree about the same number*; `registers-check.py` now reads every document under `docs/` and fails on a present-tense condition tally that disagrees with what the ADRs actually say. **Narrow on purpose.** It matches only `N of M` where M is the number of conditions there are, so *three of four findings* is left alone — a checker that guessed at which numbers were claims would be turned off within a week. And **dated history stays legal**, for the reason `remembered_numbers` already gives: CLAUDE.md's account of the count being 22 when the truth was 24 is a record of an event, and deleting it to satisfy a checker would destroy the reason the tool exists. Verified both ways: a stale present-tense tally fails, the same number in a dated sentence does not. **The trigger fired the same day the check was written, on the register the check did not cover.** `a_condition_tally_that_disagrees_with_the_conditions` matches `N of M` where M is the number of *conditions*, so a §66 gate tally — a different register with a different total — went unread. **Four documents were saying `0 of 9 run` in the present tense while seven of the nine had run**, and CLAUDE.md §1 already says that tally *"is maintained in one place, the §66 table in architecture-completeness.md, and is read there rather than restated here"*. Four documents were restating it. **Repaired 2026-08-24, and the four were not the same kind of wrong.** One was live: [ADR-030](adr/ADR-030-reading-the-reference-implementation.md) listed the gates among *live debts* at `0 of 9`, which a reader has no reason to read as history. The other three — [phase-0-exit-plan](phase-0-exit-plan.md), [contradiction-sweep-2](reviews/contradiction-sweep-2.md) and [independent review 3](reviews/independent-review-3-architecture.md) — were true when they were written and had simply never been dated; in the sweep's case a single word, *remain*, was doing all the damage. **So the record is kept and the tense is fixed**, which is what [architecture-assessment.md](architecture-assessment.md) already did at document scale: rewriting a snapshot to today erases what was believed before anything was built. Each now says when its number was true and points at the table that owns it. **And the check is the second one, `a_gate_tally_that_disagrees_with_the_gates`**, reading the §66 table for truth — a gate has run when its `Run` cell carries a date — with the same narrowness as the first: only `N of 9`, only on a line that mentions a gate, only when the number is neither those run nor those outstanding, and never when the line says when it was true. `architecture-completeness.md` is exempt because a count read off its own source is not a restatement, and an empty table is a failure rather than a pass, because a check that cannot fail is worse than no check. **Falsified in both directions**: before the repair it named all four files and the number each one claimed; with `3 of 9` written back into the sweep in the present tense it named that file again; `gate_counts()` reads 7 of 9, which is what the §66 table and the status page both say. **The row stays open** — two registers are now checked and the page still publishes counts of questions, assumptions, ADRs and tests that nothing compares against a restatement, and the gate's finding was about the pattern rather than about any one of them. | The next time two documents disagree about the same number | **A fact in three places is three places to be wrong.** | **CLOSED 2026-08-24: the board's other published counts are checked too.** `a_register_tally_that_disagrees_with_the_register` reads the debt register and the ADR directory and refuses a present-tense *N open debts* or *N ADRs* that disagrees with them, using **the same arithmetic `status-page.py` publishes** — so a document is checked against the board's own numbers rather than against a second implementation that could drift from them, which would be this row's defect one level up. **Its first run found six restatements and every one was legitimate**, which is the more useful result. Three review documents say *all 17 ADRs*; the phase-0 plan quotes *12 ADRs* inside a correction of itself; ADR-030 counts **the reference's** 74. So the check gained three narrowings rather than the documents gaining six edits: `read`, `said` and `quoted` join the past-tense vocabulary, because that is how this repository records what a document used to hold; a sentence naming the reference or a peer is about somebody else's repository; and **a document that stamps its own date in its first fifteen lines is a record of that date** — *Produced 2026-08-13*, *Written 2026-08-13* — which is the discipline [architecture-assessment.md](architecture-assessment.md) already uses and the reason it was repaired with a header rather than a rewrite. **The window is a sentence rather than a line**, because ADR-030's subject and its number sit on different lines and reading one line at a time asks the question of half a sentence. **Falsified** by writing *12 open debts and 31 ADRs* into the completeness matrix: both were named. **What is not checked, deliberately**: open questions, assumptions validated, and the test count. The first two have no stable total to compare against — a question is *answered* rather than closed and both states live in one file — and the third moves on every commit, so a check on it would fail more often than it caught anything. Three of the board's seven numbers are now defended and the three that are not are the three where a checker would be noise. |
| D-117 | ~~**The where parser has no expression tree, and its own remark says it does.**~~ `WhereClause`'s class note describes *"an expression tree and the emitter rebuilds the statement from it"*; `Parser` appends SQL into a `StringBuilder` as it descends, so parsing and emitting are one pass with no tree between them. **The security property is intact** — identifiers are matched against the layer's real columns and re-quoted by us, operators come from a fixed table, every literal is bound — so this is not [D-41](#) returning. What is missing is the *seam*: there is no shared emitter for a second front end to target. | 2026-08-15, with the query surface and the where clause that was refusing everybody | **Acceptable with one front end, and right by §82.** A tree with one producer and one consumer is a layer nobody asked for, and building it in advance is the overengineering this repository refuses by rule. | **[ADR-039](adr/ADR-039-wfs-is-the-first-surface-after-v1.md) §5, and it has already fired.** A second filter language — Filter Encoding 2.0 — needs the same target, and the only alternatives are a second emitter or a compatibility adapter that emits SQL, which §51 exists to prevent. | **A remark that describes a design the code does not have.** That is the Correctness gate's C1 shape in a second file: the comment was true of the plan and false of the build, and the next reader plans against the comment. Here it cost nothing until a second caller arrived, which is exactly when it was going to. | **RESOLVED 2026-08-19**, the day it was opened, as ADR-039's first work item. `AttributePredicate` is the tree, `PredicateSql` is the one place a predicate becomes a statement, and `WhereClause` is now the grammar alone. **The falsification asked for is the one that ran: all 40 where-clause tests pass unchanged**, including the four that assert exact SQL text — so the extraction moved no behaviour. Two things came out of it that were not the debt. **The emitter re-matches every column against the layer itself**, which the parser also does; the duplication is the point, because it is what makes the emitter safe without trusting its caller, and the second front end is the caller that does not exist yet. **And it carries its own depth bound**, since a tree can now arrive from XML rather than only from a grammar that counted its own brackets. One behaviour did change and no test asserted it: redundant brackets are no longer echoed back — `(a and b) or c` emits without them and means the same, while `(a or b) and c` keeps them because without them it would not. The class note that started this row now describes what is there. |
| D-118 | **Every `GetFeature` counts the whole result before it writes a page of it.** WFS 2.0 makes `numberReturned` a required attribute on `wfs:FeatureCollection`, so it must be known before the first feature is written — and the two ways to know it are to buffer the page or to ask the provider how many rows match. So `WfsEndpoints` calls `CountAsync` on every request and derives both numbers from it. On a filtered query that is cheap; on an unfiltered read of a large table it is a full count beside the page. | 2026-08-19, with [ADR-039](adr/ADR-039-wfs-is-the-first-surface-after-v1.md) | **Acceptable, and the alternative was the one this repository refuses.** Buffering the page means holding up to `MaximumRecordCount` decoded features in memory before writing any of them, and [A-037](architecture-assumptions.md) measured allocation as the binding constraint. The specification permits `numberMatched="unknown"`, which would remove the count — and it also removes the paging metadata every client uses to draw a scrollbar. | **A measurement, and it does not exist yet.** The trigger is the feature-query benchmark extended to the WFS path: if the count is a material share of an unfiltered `GetFeature` on a table the size of the 6.5-million-row corpus, `numberMatched` becomes `unknown` for the unfiltered case and exact for the filtered one. **Do not change it before measuring** — the performance gate has now been wrong five times about where this server's cost is, and four of those were arguments rather than numbers. | Two round trips where one would do, on the one surface a QGIS user will hammer with pans. Unmeasured, so the size of the cost is unknown, which is itself the finding. **MEASURED AND REPAID 2026-08-24, in that order, because the trigger cell said to** ([wfs-count](../benchmarks/wfs-count/RESULTS.md)). On the 6.5-million-row corpus an unfiltered `count(*)` is **577 ms** where the page it is written beside is **7.6 ms** — seventy-five times — and the filtered case is 14 ms, cheap exactly as this row guessed. On the largest layer this deployment actually publishes, 46,041 rows, it is 3.9 ms of a 44 ms request: **immaterial, which is why the row could not be settled by looking at the deployment in front of us.** The count is `O(table)` and the page is `O(page)`, so the share is not a constant — it is whatever the deployment's largest table makes it. **`IFeatureSource.CountUpToAsync` counts to a ceiling**, `select count(*) from (select 1 from t where … limit n) s`, with no `order by` because the question is *how many*. `numberReturned` stays exact always; `numberMatched` becomes `\"unknown\"` — a value WFS 2.0 defines for this — only above the ceiling. **The tight bound was written first and measured wrong**: `startIndex + limit + 1` made a page of ten from a 1,421-row layer answer *unknown*, which is this row's own warning about the scrollbar arriving. The ceiling is 100,000 instead, **17.9 ms on the corpus against 577 ms**, flat in the table's size, and above every layer here. **`resultType=hits` still counts the whole table**, because that is the question hits asks. `BoundedCountTests` holds the contract and the shape of the cost — two ceilings ten times apart on one table, fastest of three interleaved. | ~~open~~ repaid 2026-08-24 |
| D-119 | ~~**The route audit reported *0 ungoverned* about a surface it was not looking at.**~~ `/admin/routes` filtered on `/rest/services`, which was every data route until [ADR-039](adr/ADR-039-wfs-is-the-first-surface-after-v1.md) put WFS at `/wfs`. Both WFS routes carried their `SharingGoverned` marker; nothing read it. The endpoint answered `ungoverned: 0` and the conformance test that asserts it passed — about 67 routes, with the two new ones outside the frame. | 2026-08-19, the moment a data route was published outside `/rest/services` | **Not acceptable, and the failure is the one `SharingGoverned`'s own remark predicts in as many words:** *applying the marker without the check would be a lie that is one keystroke away*. This is that lie, arrived at from the other direction — the marker was applied and the check was the thing that moved. | Found by asking the running server what it had audited, rather than by reading the endpoint. The number was right and the denominator was wrong. | **A green check about the wrong set is worse than no check.** ADR-018 condition 5 is kept by this endpoint alone; for the WFS surface it was keeping nothing while reporting success. | **RESOLVED 2026-08-19**, same day. The filter is a named predicate listing every prefix that serves somebody's data, and the conformance suite's *audit covers every kind of route* test now names `/wfs` among the families it requires to be present — so a surface forgotten in that predicate fails as a missing family instead of passing as silence. Verified against the running server: 69 routes audited, both `/wfs` routes governed *by filtering*, `ungoverned: 0` about a set that now includes them. **REOPENED AND RE-RESOLVED 2026-08-20 — the same defect, on the next three surfaces.** WMS, ArcGIS MapServer and OGC API Features shipped that day at `/wms`, under `/rest/services/…/MapServer` and at `/ogc/features`. The predicate this row's repair introduced named `/rest/services` and `/wfs`; the three new prefixes were outside it, so the audit again answered `ungoverned: 0` about a frame that excluded the newest doors. **The repair was a named predicate and it was still a list somebody had to remember to extend**, which is the lesson this row failed to draw the first time. Now `AdminEndpoints.Served` is one array, the predicate loops over it, and the audit returns `filteredOn` so a caller reads the denominator instead of trusting it. The conformance test keeps its own independent list — that suite references no repository assembly by design — and compares the two, so publishing a face without adding it to `Served` fails as a mismatch rather than passing as silence. |
| D-120 | **An ArcGIS token travels in the query string, and this server writes the query string into its log.** Esri clients send a session token as `?token=` on ordinary requests — it is how every one of them has always worked — so `Authentication.EsriToken` accepts it, and ASP.NET's request logging records the full URL. A token in a log is a credential in a log, readable by anyone who can read the log and by any proxy in front of this server. | 2026-08-19, with the ArcGIS token endpoint | **Acceptable, because the alternative is not supporting Esri clients.** The channel is the client's choice rather than ours: ArcGIS Pro and every SDK put the token there, and refusing it means the surface v1 is built on cannot be signed into. The `X-Esri-Authorization` header is the better channel, is tried first, and is what a client that offers the choice will use. | ~~**Before any deployment whose logs leave the machine.**~~ **Done 2026-08-20, and not before it was needed: the security gate found live `root` session tokens in this server's log, harvested one and replayed it against a private layer.** The sentence *it is not done today because the logging is the framework's own and redacting it needs a filter rather than a line* was the reasoning that let it wait, and it was wrong about the shape of the fix: the framework's logging is silenced and this server writes its own line through `QueryRedaction`, which is a code path rather than a setting. What is left of this row is the part that was never ours. | A credential with the session's full lifetime, sitting in a text file that nothing treats as secret. On a single-machine development server that is a small circle; behind a reverse proxy or a log shipper it is not. | **CLOSED 2026-08-24, and it was much narrower from 2026-08-20 before that.** It was recorded at the moment the channel was opened rather than after somebody found a token in a log — and then somebody found a token in a log anyway, five days later, because recording a debt is not the same as paying it. `QueryRedaction` replaces the value of `token` and seven other credential parameters before anything is written; seventeen unit tests cover the function and `TokenIsNotLoggedTests` sends a sentinel and searches the live log file, which is the form [ADR-015](adr/ADR-015-authentication.md) condition 2 asks for because *§4.1 fails silently otherwise, and silently is the only way it fails*. **What stays open is outside this process**: the credential is still in the URL, so every reverse proxy, browser history and load balancer between a client and here may write it down, and nothing we ship reaches those. The header channel is tried first and is what a client with a choice uses. ~~This row closes when the query channel can be switched off in a deployment whose clients do not need it.~~ **CLOSED 2026-08-24: it can be.** `Graticula:AcceptTokenInQueryString`, **true by default and deliberately so** — off by default would refuse every unmodified ArcGIS client the first time somebody upgraded, which is the whole of Q-17's promise. With it false, `EsriToken` does not read the parameter at all, so a caller sending only `?token=` is anonymous and meets the **ordinary** refusal. **The distinct refusal was the thing not to build**: *your token channel is disabled* tells whoever guessed a token that they guessed a real one. **One seam, because there is only one** — the header is tried first and the query string is the fallback, in a single method, which is why this was a switch rather than a sweep. **Three tests, and the first is the one that matters**: the default is asserted from a configuration with the key *absent*, not from the record's own default, because absent is what an upgrading deployment has. The third asserts the legacy `GisServer:` prefix reaches it, per [ADR-032](adr/ADR-032-the-product-is-named-graticula.md) §5 — a setting added after the rename is exactly the one likely to be wired to the new name only. **What does not change is the honest part of this row**: with the channel on, the credential is still in the URL and still reaches proxies and histories this server does not own. That is now a deployment's choice with a switch beside it rather than a property of the product. |
| D-121 | **`HEAD` answers 405 wherever `GET` answers 200.** ArcGIS Pro sends `HEAD /rest/services/{name}/FeatureServer/0?f=json&returnPbfFeatureEncodings=true` before it sends the `GET`, and gets Method Not Allowed. It recovers by falling back, so nothing visible broke — but HTTP says a resource that answers GET answers HEAD, and a client that has to discover otherwise by being refused pays a round trip for the lesson. | 2026-08-19, found the first time a real ArcGIS Pro was pointed at this server | **Acceptable and small.** Every route is mapped with `MapGet`, which does not register HEAD; no client has been blocked by it, and Pro's fallback is immediate. | Any client that treats 405 as fatal rather than as a probe result, or any measurement that counts the extra round trip. Fixing it is one call shape across the route table rather than a change to any handler. | One wasted request per layer added, and an answer that is wrong about HTTP rather than about GIS. **REPAID 2026-08-24, and not the way this row prescribed.** The row called the repair *one call shape across the route table*, and that is the version that goes wrong: it is right sixty-three times and then somebody adds the sixty-fourth `MapGet`. One middleware in front of routing rewrites the method instead, so the property holds for routes nobody has written yet. **In front of routing is the whole of it** — `WebApplication` inserts `UseRouting` before every middleware when it is not called, and the 405 is routing's own answer, so the first attempt rewrote a decision that had already been made and measured no change; `UseRouting` is explicit now. **Kestrel drops the body**, deciding from the method it parsed rather than from the property this rewrites. **The log remembers what arrived**: it reads the method behind the rewrite, so a client's HEAD would otherwise have been recorded as a GET. Eleven addresses across every face measured before and after ([head-requests](../benchmarks/head-requests/RESULTS.md)), and what is asserted is that HEAD and GET **agree** rather than that they say 200 — a route with no GET still answers 405, which is correct and is what a blanket repair gets wrong. `HeadRequestTests` holds ten cases; seven fail when the rewrite is removed, and the three that do not are the two portal routes that already had it and the POST-only refusal. | ~~open~~ repaid 2026-08-24 |
| D-122 | ~~**One anonymous POST killed the server, and the guard that was supposed to stop it counted only half the tree.**~~ `GmlGeometryReader.TryShape` recursed with the document — a `gml:MultiSurface` may hold members that are MultiSurfaces — and `FilterReader`'s depth counter stopped at the boundary between the two readers. Three thousand levels in a 223 KB unauthenticated request produced a `StackOverflowException`, which .NET cannot catch: no refusal, no log line, no process. A thousand levels survived, so nothing degraded first. | 2026-08-19, with the filter reader | **Not acceptable, and it was reachable by anyone who could reach the server.** The surface is read-only, so no data moved; what went was availability, from a single request needing no credential. | Found by [wfs-filter-review-1](reviews/wfs-filter-review-1.md), the independent review [ADR-039](adr/ADR-039-wfs-is-the-first-surface-after-v1.md) condition 2 required. The author's own tests covered filter nesting and stopped where the geometry began. | **The size bounds held perfectly and bought nothing.** One megabyte of characters and four of bytes were both intact; the crash arrived at 223 KB. A bound on the wrong dimension reads as protection and is not, which is the finding under the finding. | **RESOLVED 2026-08-19**, same day. The depth budget is now shared across both readers rather than counted per reader — a filter thirty levels deep around a geometry thirty deep is one stack, and two counters that each allow the maximum allow twice it. Falsified against the server with the reviewer's own 223 KB payload: it now answers an exception report naming the limit, and `/healthz/ready` and a live query both answer afterwards. **And the remark in `SafeXml` is corrected**, which is the other half: it said depth *is counted by the reader that walks the tree*, singular, while there were two and one of them did not count. That sentence was the coverage claim the hole went through, and it is [D-41](#)'s shape written by the same author on the same day. |
| D-123 | ~~**A face the operator switched off stayed open on the new door.**~~ A service with `servesFeatures` false answers 404 at `/rest/services/{name}/FeatureServer` — ADR-031 condition 2, asserted in `ServiceLookup.LayerAsync` and again on the service document — and `/wfs` read its layers anyway. `WfsEndpoints.VisibleAsync` applied the other three checks the rest of the server applies (running, sharing, privilege) and not this one, so an operator who had turned the feature face off could serve every one of its rows over WFS to the same callers. | 2026-08-20, while adding the WFS link to the REST directory | **Not acceptable.** It is not a WFS decision — it is the ArcGIS decision, unenforced. What leaked is the operator's *configuration* choice, which is the thing the limit exists to express. | **RESOLVED 2026-08-20**, same day, one check in `VisibleAsync`. Asserted rather than remembered: `WfsConformanceTests` switches the face off against the live server, requires the type to disappear from the capabilities document *and* the ArcGIS door to answer 404, and switches it back. |
| D-124 | **Four ArcGIS nouns live in Tier 1.** `LayerDefinition.ObjectIdColumn`, `LayerDefinition.IsArcGisServable`, `FeatureQuery.ObjectIds` and `FeatureEdits.ObjectId` are one protocol's vocabulary in the domain every protocol reads. A speculative protocol-neutral interface would have cost more and been exercised by nothing. |

ADR-004's own file was not edited — status `DEFERRED`, §5 reading *Pending* — and neither were v1-scope §3b, `protocol-surface.md`, `product-context.md`, `competitive-position.md` §6a, `architecture-completeness.md`, or Q-26 and Q-47, each of which restated the deferral as a fact. | 2026-08-20, the day the four surfaces shipped | **Not acceptable, and the comparison that makes it a process finding rather than an oversight is one day old.** On 2026-08-19 one surface shipped (WFS) and four capability documents were amended for it, one of them carrying a paragraph explaining why it was deliberately *not* amended further. On 2026-08-20 four surfaces shipped and none of those documents was touched. **The repository has a working propagation discipline and nothing that notices when it is skipped.** | Found by contradiction sweep 3, which is the instrument, and the trigger is that the instrument is a person reading everything rather than a check. The repayable form is mechanical: an ADR that amends another names it, and a tool asserts the named file mentions the amending ADR. `tools/registers-check.py` is where that belongs, beside the count checks it already runs. | **A reader who trusts the register is told the opposite of what the code does.** Eight documents said WMS did not exist while a CITE run against it sat in `docs/reviews/`. That is the specific failure this repository's whole documentation discipline exists to prevent, so it costs more here than the same staleness would cost elsewhere. | **CLOSED 2026-08-23. The check is written and it caught a violation on its first run.** `registers-check.py` reads each ADR's `Amends`, `Supersedes` and `Superseded by` declaration — the header row and the blockquote form, which is how the ADRs actually write them — and fails the build when the named ADR does not mention the ADR that named it. **The asymmetry is the whole defect**: an amending ADR names what it changes because the author is looking at it, and the amended one says nothing because nobody opens a file to record that somebody else has just contradicted it. So the citation exists in one direction and a reader arriving from the other finds a decision that reads as current. **The violation was ADR-046 amending ADR-007 §4.8 while ADR-007 had never heard of it** — written the same day as the check, by the same hand, which is the argument for the check rather than against it. ADR-007 now carries the amendment note and records N3 as built. Verified in the failing direction too: removing the mention makes the check report it by name. |
| D-127 | **Four of seven protocol faces have no degraded path when the catalogue database is unreachable.** [ADR-026](adr/ADR-026-serving-through-a-platform-store-outage.md) answers Q-95 with a fallback: a service already resolved stays servable while the catalogue is down. The FeatureServer routes take `CatalogFallback` directly, `MapServerEndpoints` and `VectorTileEndpoints` take it through `ServiceLookup`, and attachments and relationships inherit it. ~~`WfsEndpoints`, `WmsEndpoints`, `OgcFeaturesEndpoints` and `PortalEndpoints` name it nowhere.~~ **All four were given it on 2026-08-23 and this sentence was never struck — measured 2026-08-24: WFS 4 references, WMS 6, OGC Features 7, Portal 5.** | 2026-08-19 for WFS and the portal, 2026-08-20 for WMS and OGC Features | **Acceptable only because the shape of the gap was measured before it was recorded.** `CatalogFallback` exposes `FindServiceAsync` and nothing else — it resolves one named service and cannot list. The four faces without it all begin by *enumerating*: WFS and WMS build capabilities documents over every published layer, OGC Features answers `/collections`, and the portal searches. **So this is not four missing call sites. There is no degraded listing capability at all**, and adding the resolve-one path to those four would make `GetCapabilities` fail while `GetMap` succeeded, which is a worse answer than failing consistently. | Any deployment where the catalogue and the data are separately reachable — which is the case ADR-026 is about, and is normal when the catalogue is a separate database or a separate host. The repayable form is a cached listing beside the cached resolve, with the staleness stated in the document it produces. | **A capability that is claimed in an ADR and true on two faces of seven.** Q-95's answer reads as a property of the server; it is a property of two endpoints. | **CLOSED 2026-08-23, and it was wider than it was opened before that — the verdict moved to the front on 2026-08-24, because it had been buried in the middle of this cell and the board was counting the row open.** Found by the consistency gate on 2026-08-20 by counting call sites. **The failure gate then stopped the database and watched**, which turned it from a call count into behaviour and found two further narrowings this row did not have. *(i)* The difference is visible from outside: same service, same instant, its MapServer legend serving 200 while its WMS `GetMap` refuses 503, with the `X-Catalog-Age` header present on exactly the responses that degraded. *(ii)* **The three covered faces degrade for about thirty seconds, not the advertised fifteen minutes.** There are two caches and only one has a fallback: `CatalogFallback` remembers the catalogue for fifteen minutes, and `ServiceContexts`' described-shape memory lasts thirty seconds with no fallback at all — `/admin/health` states the number itself. Every document that needs a field list dies at thirty seconds regardless of how fresh the catalogue memory is. **So ADR-026 answers Q-95 on two axes more narrowly than it reads: three faces of seven, and thirty seconds of fifteen minutes.** **The thirty seconds is fixed, 2026-08-23, and the measurement is the whole of the claim.** `ServiceContexts` keeps the last shape each layer was *known* to have, separate from the thirty-second freshness memory, and serves it when the source cannot be reached at all. Before: the layer document answered 200 for thirty seconds and refused after. After: **200 at 45 and at 100 seconds into an outage, carrying the real field list and the real name.** The two memories are separate rather than one longer window because they answer different questions — *how long before I ask again* is bounded by how long a DBA's `ALTER TABLE` may go unnoticed, and lengthening that would serve a stale field list to a **healthy** server. `/admin/health` reports both counts, because during an outage *how much is fresh* and *how much can still be served* are different numbers and an administrator needs to know which one they are reading. **And a resilience mechanism silenced another one, which is the part worth keeping.** The breaker from [D-131](#) refuses an unreachable source instantly with its own exception type, and this fallback asks *was the source unreachable* before serving what it remembers — so for one build the answer was no, and 45 seconds into an outage the first request served a document in 8 s while the next seven refused in 12 ms. Each mechanism was correct alone. Found by measuring, not by reading. **What is left is measured too, and it is not small.** Concurrently, 45 seconds into an outage: the authentication path answers 19 of 20 requests in 13 ms — one prober, the rest refused from memory — but the **folder listing serves 0 of 20 instantly** and the **layer document 0 of 20**, each paying about one four-second blackholed connect per request. The listing is this row's first axis and is unchanged: `CatalogFallback` resolves one named service and cannot list, so there is still no degraded listing capability at all. The layer document's remaining wait is one catalogue call this row had not separated out. **So the second axis is closed and the first is narrower than it was: documents survive, and they still cost four seconds each to produce.** [failure-gate-2.md](reviews/failure-gate-2.md) F2 and F3. **CLOSED 2026-08-23. Every enumerating face now degrades, and the four seconds are gone.** `CatalogFallback` grew the other half it never had: it remembers the last listing beside the last resolve, under the same fifteen-minute window and the same rule that only a connectivity failure falls back. WFS, WMS, OGC Features and the portal take it, and so does a fifth face this row had not named — `/rest/services`, the REST directory, which is where the gate's *folder listing* measurement actually came from. **Null and empty are different answers, and the whole repair turns on it.** A remembered listing is a document; an empty one is a claim that this server publishes nothing, and a client that believes it stops asking. So a blind fallback with nothing remembered returns null, every face refuses 503 in its own dialect, and the compiler makes each of them handle it — an empty list would have flowed through the filters and out into a capabilities document that says the server has no layers. **Measured, at t+45 s into an outage, twenty concurrent per face.** Before: 0 of 20 instantly, about four seconds each. After: `/rest/services` 19/20 under 500 ms at a 10 ms median, WFS `GetCapabilities` 20/20 at 14 ms, WMS 19/20 at 13 ms, OGC `/collections` 20/20 at 14 ms, the portal search 20/20 at 11 ms, every one carrying `X-Catalog-Age`. A folder's own directory answers **20 of 20 under 500 ms and 20 of 20 actually list the service**, in a document identical to the healthy one. The remaining 4-second worst case is one prober per cooling window, which is the breaker working rather than the failure it replaced. Recovery: fresh again on every face 10.8–10.9 s after the store returned, one cooling window, no restart. [benchmarks/catalogue-outage](../benchmarks/catalogue-outage/RESULTS.md). **And the listing repair alone was not enough, which is why the intermediate measurement is kept.** With the listing served from memory, WMS still cost 4.0 s on all twenty and OGC Features never answered inside thirty seconds. The remainder was not the catalogue: `EX_GeographicBoundingBox` is mandatory on a WMS 1.3.0 named layer, so a capabilities document makes one projection call per distinct spatial reference and each one waited out a connect nothing would answer. `BreakingProjector` puts `IProjector` behind the breaker — a decorator in the host, because a Tier 2 adapter that knew about the server's circuit breaker would be the port leaking in the direction it exists to prevent (`CLAUDE.md` §4). Every caller already caught that failure and left the layer without a geographic extent; they now reach the same place immediately. **Two consequences are recorded rather than hidden.** A folder nothing knows about answers 200 with an empty directory while blind where a healthy server answers 404 — the register that says which folders exist cannot be read, and a 404 would be a claim; this is the reasoning `FolderExistsAsync` already had for its own failure. And a blind directory omits the system services and the image services, whose catalogues have no memory in front of them: a directory missing `Utilities` is smaller than the truth, and one that takes four seconds to say so is worse for every client that browses it. **What this did not fix, measured in the same run:** `/rest/generateToken` costs about four seconds and refuses, twenty times out of twenty. Refusing is right — a token minted from a remembered password hash is exactly the stale authorization this class exists to prevent — but paying a blackholed connect for each one is D-131's shape on a path the breaker is not consulted from. It measured the same before this work and is not this row's. |
| D-128 | **The connection budget sheds load on the rendering faces and not on the query faces.** **PARTLY REPAIRED 2026-08-23 by [ADR-046](adr/ADR-046-admission-control-bounds-the-queue-not-the-wait.md), and the row's diagnosis was half wrong.** Re-measured: `query` is never refused at any concurrency — confirmed — but **`export` is never refused either**, forty of forty admitted at 4.5 seconds a render, which is slower than the four seconds this row cites as the case that does refuse. So it is not about faces. A refusal needs one caller to wait the *whole* five seconds, which needs a queue deeper than the wait divided by the service time; for any workload fast enough to matter that is a queue thousands deep. The control does not fire late, it very nearly never fires. The queue is bounded and observable now — peak waiters measured exactly at the configured depth — and a cold flood sheds 15% at concurrency 240. **A warm flood at 480 still sheds nothing and its latency is still growing**, which is ADR-046 condition 1 and is not discharged. [ADR-007](adr/ADR-007-service-runtime.md) §4.8's admission control refuses with a 503 once a data source has 24 requests in flight and the caller has waited five seconds. Measured: `MapServer/export` and WMS `GetMap` refuse correctly — 35% of 40 concurrent renders — and ArcGIS `query`, WFS `GetFeature` and OGC `items` refuse **never**, at concurrency 60, 100 and 150, and not even when the client is throttled to hold 50 connections open for 23 seconds. | 2026-08-15, when the budget was written; found 2026-08-20 by the second performance gate | **Not acceptable, and it was believed to be working.** The budget is wrapped around every data source identically by `LayerConnections.SourceFor`, so nothing in the code says one face is exempt and nobody would read one there. **The inferred cause is that the timer bounds the wait for a slot and not the hold after admission**: a render costs about four seconds of its own, so competitors wait past the window and are refused; a query is fast per request even while the aggregate collapses 18×, so a slot keeps freeing inside the window and nobody is ever turned away. Inferred from `pg_stat_activity` and code reading, not from a profiler. | **Before any deployment that expects to survive a query flood** — which is every deployment, since panning is what GIS clients do. The repair is a hold-time bound and it is a concurrency design change rather than a patch: a request killed mid-stream is a truncated document, which is the failure `ErrorResponse` already cannot answer cleanly. **It needs an ADR, and that is why this gate did not fix it.** **Written down as one 2026-08-24: [Q-139](open-questions.md)**, together with [D-144](#), because the two are the same question from two sides and neither row said so — **and this row was then closed as the duplicate, D-144 keeping the substance** — and both depend on [Q-91](open-questions.md), since a request ended mid-response is the truncated document nothing can report. | **~~An operator reads §4.8 and believes the server sheds load. On the surface clients actually hammer, it does not.~~ **§4.8 carries an ADR-046 amendment saying so in its own words** — *that fires almost never* — so the document no longer misleads the reader this cost was about.** Everybody degrades together — measured at 0.67 s alone against 12.4 s at concurrency 60 — with no status code to trigger client-side backoff, so a slow server looks to every client exactly like a working one. | **CLOSED 2026-08-24 as a duplicate, with its measurement kept — the substance is [D-144](#) and the decision is [Q-139](open-questions.md).** **Its claim was withdrawn by the row itself.** *The budget sheds load on the rendering faces and not on the query faces* was measured and found half wrong: `query` is never refused, and **`export` is never refused either** — forty of forty admitted at 4.5 seconds a render, slower than the four seconds this row cited as the case that does refuse. It is not about faces. The control does not fire late; it very nearly never fires, because a refusal needs one caller to wait the whole window, which needs a queue deeper than the window divided by the service time. **Its cost is discharged.** That cost was a reader of ADR-007 §4.8 believing the server sheds load, and §4.8 now carries an ADR-046 amendment that says *that fires almost never* and names what replaced it: arrivals are refused when too many are already waiting, with the wait kept as a backstop. **What is left is not this row's.** A hold-time bound is [D-144](#) — *nothing bounds how long one request may hold a permit* — and both were pointed at [Q-139](open-questions.md) on 2026-08-24 precisely because they are one question from two sides. **Two rows for one question is the shape [D-116](#) is about**, and keeping this one open would mean the pile counts the same work twice. The queue is bounded and observable, peak waiters measured exactly at the configured depth, and a cold flood sheds 15% — all of which stays in [performance gate 2](reviews/performance-gate-2.md) and in ADR-046 rather than being lost with the row. |
| D-129 | ~~**The accepted-and-ignored list told the operator three things that were not true.**~~ `FeatureServerQueryParameters.IgnoredParameters` pairs each tolerated parameter with a sentence saying why ignoring it is harmless, and `Log.QueryParameterIgnored` writes that sentence out on every request that uses one. Three had rotted: `maxAllowableOffset` said *geometry is returned ungeneralised* while the parser threaded the tolerance into the query and PostGIS generalised — the same full-table request measured 26.2 MB without it and 4.8 MB with it; `token` said *authentication is by header* while `?token=` authenticated on every route; `datumTransformation` said *no reprojection happens* while `outSR=3857` returned Web Mercator metres. | 2026-08-17 for the generalisation, 2026-08-20 for the token; found 2026-08-20 while verifying the performance gate's F4 | **Not acceptable, and the mechanism is worth naming because it is invisible by construction.** The list exists so that tolerating a parameter is a decision somebody wrote down. Nothing re-reads the sentence when the code beside it changes, so each entry is a claim that was true once and is checked never — the exact failure mode the list was built to prevent, arrived at from the other side. | Found by measuring a parameter the table said was ignored. The repayable form is a test per entry: send the parameter, assert the answer does not change. That is cheap for the seven that really are ignored and is what would have caught all three. | **A false entry is worse than a missing one.** The operator's log said a parameter was dropped that was honoured; a client reading the same claim would build a workaround for a capability it already had; and `datumTransformation`'s sentence hid a real limit — the transformation is the database's default and cannot be chosen, which is metres on some datum pairs. | **RESOLVED 2026-08-20**, same day. `maxAllowableOffset` left the table — it is honoured, and it was already in `Known`, so it stays accepted. The other two kept their key, because `TryUnknown` refuses anything absent from the table, and had their sentence corrected to what the code does. **The per-entry test is not written**, and that is the part of this row that is only closed by the three repairs rather than by the guard: [D-130](#). |
| D-130 | **This repository's documentation of its own behaviour is unchecked, and it was wrong five times in two days.** The pattern is one shape: a remark, a table entry or an ADR sentence states what the code does; the code changes; nobody re-reads the sentence. `Authentication`'s remark said `?token=` was *not accepted yet* while it authenticated everywhere ([security gate 2](reviews/security-gate-2.md)); `ConnectionBudgetFullException`'s said the refusal carries a `Retry-After` that nothing set ([performance gate 2](reviews/performance-gate-2.md) F2); and three entries of `IgnoredParameters` described behaviour the code had grown past ([D-129](#)). ADR-004 reading `DEFERRED` past its own un-deferral ([D-126](#)) is the same shape one level up. | 2026-08-20, when the fifth was found | **Not acceptable, and it is the cost of this repository's own method.** A codebase that carries its reasoning in prose beside the code is more readable than one that does not — and every sentence is a claim that can go stale silently, which a codebase with no prose cannot do. **The method is not the problem; the absence of any check on it is.** | Now. The repayable form differs per kind and none of it is exotic: a test per `IgnoredParameters` entry that sends the parameter and asserts the answer does not change; an assertion on the headers of each refusal shape; and `tools/registers-check.py` asserting that an ADR named as amended mentions the ADR that amended it. **All three are cheap, and each would have caught the failure that named it.** | **Five wrong claims in two days, all found by people rather than by tools, and two of them only because an independent reviewer was looking.** The next one is found the same way or not at all — and the ones that stay unfound are the ones nobody has a reason to test. | **CLOSED 2026-08-23. All three checks are written, and two of them found something.** *(i)* `registers-check.py` refuses an unacknowledged amendment, which is [D-126](#), and it caught ADR-046 against ADR-007 on its first run. *(ii)* `IgnoredParameterTests` sends each knowingly-ignored parameter and asserts the answer is **byte for byte** the same — anything looser would have missed the defect that named this, because `maxAllowableOffset` changed a response from 26.2 MB to 4.8 MB with every feature still present and every attribute unchanged. All ten probed parameters are genuinely ignored. *(iii)* `RefusalHeaderTests` asserts the retry signal per refusal shape, in both directions: a refusal this server will lift on its own says when, and one whose recovery it cannot predict promises nothing — a `Retry-After` on an unreachable database would turn ignorance into a schedule a client follows. **The retry rule became a function to make that testable**, and writing it that way found something worth having: the breaker's refusal is the one 503 whose end this server actually knows, so it now carries its own cooling window as the retry time. **And the second check needed a check of its own.** The conformance suite may not reference our assemblies, so its list of parameter names is a copy — which is this row's exact defect one level down. `IgnoredParameterCoverageTests` reads that file and asserts it names every parameter the server ignores, so an entry added to the server and forgotten in the test fails. A test that quietly covers less than it claims is the thing this row is about. |
| D-131 | **A refused request costs 8–12 seconds during a database outage, because the connect attempt happens twice.** Authentication resolves a principal ahead of every route and the endpoint then reads the data; with the store down each pays a full Npgsql connect timeout, so a refusal that *early in the outage* takes 21–30 ms takes 8–12 s once the pool has drained its already-broken connectors. Measured on FeatureServer query, WMS `GetMap`, OGC `items` and a warm tile. | 2026-08-20, found by the second failure gate while measuring F1 | **Acceptable only because the liveness half — the part that gets a container restarted — is fixed.** The rest is a refusal that is correct and slow rather than an answer that is wrong, and the fast path demonstrably exists: the same requests refuse in tens of milliseconds before the pool turns over. What is missing is anything that remembers the store was unreachable a second ago. | **Any deployment with real request rates.** Each refusal occupies a connection for its whole 8–12 s, so under load this is a queue collapse rather than a degradation — the outage's cost grows with traffic instead of staying flat. The repayable form is a circuit breaker in front of the pool: one failed connect marks the source down for a few seconds and everything behind it refuses immediately. | **An outage that is survivable at one request per second and is not at fifty.** It also makes the outage look like a hang rather than a refusal, which is the harder thing for an operator to diagnose and the slower thing for a client to give up on. | **CLOSED 2026-08-23 by the circuit breaker [ADR-007](adr/ADR-007-service-runtime.md) §4.8 asked for as N3**, which was the last of that section's four requirements still absent. `SourceBreaker`: a source that failed within the last ten seconds is refused without being asked, and the refusal is the 503 this server already writes. **Measured, before and after, against the same stopped container.** Before: 8.0 s a refusal, six of six. After: **4.0 s for the first request and 10-21 ms for the next eleven** — and over 45 seconds of sustained outage at one request every 0.4 s, **51 of 55 requests were instant** and four were the ten-second probe that notices recovery. A recovered database is served again in 100 ms. **Three things about it are worth keeping.** First, the eight seconds were *both* the platform store — four for the principal and four for the catalogue read — so the fix needed the breaker in two assemblies, and the catalogue is a tier below the host: it asks through `IStoreHealth`, an interface where the catalogue lives, implemented where the runtime lives. Second, **only a socket failure trips it**; a `PostgresException` means PostgreSQL received the request and said no, and tripping on those would take a service down over one malformed filter. Third, **the cooling window was three seconds in the first attempt and that did nothing at all** — a window shorter than the four-second failure means a serial caller finds it cooled every time, and eight measured refusals were unchanged at 8.0 s. Ten seconds now, with a test that asserts the window outlasts the measured failure. [failure-gate-2.md](reviews/failure-gate-2.md) F1, both halves. |
| D-132 | **A client that hangs up is never recorded as 499, and the access log says the response succeeded.** `ErrorResponse.Classify` maps a cancellation to 499 with a remark saying it *exists so the access log distinguishes 'they left' from 'we broke'*. `grep -c ' - 499'` over a 6.6 MB log returns **1**, and that one is ArcGIS's own *Token Required*, which uses the number for an unrelated meaning. The disconnect 499 has never been written. The FeatureServer access log records `- 200` for a response nobody received. | 2026-08-19, with the access log; found 2026-08-20 by hanging up on twenty requests | **Acceptable in that nothing breaks, and not in what it costs to diagnose.** The branch is unreachable rather than wrong: a disconnect happens after the response has started, and the handler's first act is to log the truncation and abort, so the mapping below it never runs. The resources are released correctly — twenty forced resets left `/admin/health` byte-identical. | **The next time somebody asks whether a client-reported truncation was the client leaving or the server failing.** The repayable form is small and has two halves: emit the 499 from the truncation branch itself, and stop the access log claiming 200 for a response that was aborted. A third question is whether one access log should carry two unrelated meanings of 499 at all. | **An access log that reports success for failed transfers.** Every measurement of this server's reliability that reads that log is wrong in the optimistic direction, which is the direction nobody checks. | **CLOSED 2026-08-23, and the cause was one step past what this row described.** The row says the access log records `- 200` for a response nobody received, which is true when the whole document fits a socket buffer the client never drains. **Measured: the commoner case is worse.** Two connections reset during a 4000x4000 `GetMap` produced *Executing endpoint* and *Executed endpoint* from the framework and **no access line at all** — the middleware logged after `await next()`, and an aborted pipeline unwinds straight past that. A failed request the log does not mention is worse than one it mislabels, because a reader counting requests never sees a gap. **Both halves are fixed and both were verified live.** The line is written in a `finally`, and `ResponseOutcome` decides what it says: the two resets now log **499** where they logged nothing. **`RequestAborted` alone cannot answer it**, which is why this is a marker rather than a one-line check — aborting a response cancels that token, so a naive read reports every one of this server's own failures as the client's fault, which is 200's error with a convincing number on it. The marker is set before the abort, while the answer is still knowable, and a server-side truncation logs 500. **The stored row carries the same number as the text line**, because the Logs screen is what an operator reads now and a row saying 200 would put this row back with a nicer interface on it. `ResponseOutcomeTests` asserts the three-way distinction, including the deadline case: both it and a departed client throw `OperationCanceledException` and they deserve opposite answers. [failure-gate-2.md](reviews/failure-gate-2.md) F7. |
| D-133 | **Two background workers write a full stack trace every three seconds for as long as the platform store is down.** `GeodatabaseImporter` and `GeodatabaseInspector` each retry a job claim and log the complete Npgsql stack on every failure. Measured over one 17-minute outage: **338 warnings, 1.68 MB** — about 100 KB/min, roughly 145 MB/day of sustained outage. | 2026-08-20, found by the second failure gate | **Acceptable, and the message is not the problem.** It says the loop deliberately does not exit and why, which is the right thing to say; a silent retry loop would be worse. It is the stack trace that does not need repeating 338 times, since every repetition is identical to the first. | **Any outage long enough to matter**, which is the only kind worth reading a log about. The repayable form is the standard one: log the exception in full the first time and the sentence alone thereafter, with a count when it recovers. | **A store outage is exactly when an operator opens the log**, and this is what they find: 1.68 MB of the same stack between them and whatever they were looking for. On a disk-constrained deployment it is also a second failure arriving during the first. | **CLOSED 2026-08-23.** `RepeatedFailure`: the exception in full the first time, the sentence and a count every twentieth repetition, and a line on recovery saying how many there were and over how long. **Measured against a real outage rather than reasoned about.** 130 seconds with the platform database stopped wrote **21,366 bytes — about 10 kB a minute against this row's 100** — and the retry interval is two seconds rather than the three this row assumed, so the same outage would have written closer to 260 kB a minute before. Four full traces (two workers, two message phases), two summaries at the twentieth attempt, and two recovery lines reading *claiming work again after 21 failures over 2.1 minutes*. **The recovery line is the part that closes an incident** and the easiest to leave out: a log that says a worker started failing and never says it stopped leaves the reader to infer it from an absence. **And the dedup key is the message, not the exception type, which the outage itself argued for.** Stopping and starting PostgreSQL produced four distinct messages in sequence — *Exception while reading from stream*, *Failed to connect*, *the database system is starting up*, *not yet accepting connections* — and each is a different fact about the outage's progress. One counter for all of them would have reported the fortieth repetition of something that had just happened once, and hidden the two that say the database is on its way back. [failure-gate-2.md](reviews/failure-gate-2.md) F9. |
| D-134 | ~~**The OpenLayers viewer was dead, and every *View in: Map* link on every service page led to it.**~~ `view.js` declared `const OSM_TILES` while `ground.js`, loaded before it on the same page, declared the same name in the same global lexical scope. That is a `SyntaxError` raised while parsing, so **not one line of the file executed**: the page painted its static header and nothing else — no map, no facts, no picker, and no message anywhere a person would look. The same line was wrong twice over: it read `window.OSM_TILES`, and a top-level `const` in a classic script never becomes a property of `window`, so even without the collision the value would have been `undefined`. | 2026-08-17 at the latest, when both files carried the declaration; found 2026-08-21 | **Not acceptable, and the interesting part is why nothing said so.** `EveryScreenTests` exists for exactly this class — *nothing had ever opened those screens and looked* — and it opens the console's tabs, which are hash routes inside one document. The two viewers are separate documents with their own scripts, so they were outside the loop and nothing else opened them. **A page that fails at parse time looks exactly like a page that is still loading**, which is why a person clicking the link might not report it either. | Found by pointing a headless browser at the page while adding the MapServer face to it. The guard is the repayable form and it is written: `ViewerPageTests` opens both viewers on both faces and asserts that the element the script fills in stopped being empty. | **Every link from the REST directory to a map led to a blank page for days, and the suite was green throughout.** This is the third time in this repository that a control which exists has been invisible, and the third time the tests were green — the lesson each time is that a green suite is not a loaded page. | **RESOLVED 2026-08-21**, same day. The name is used rather than re-declared, guarded with `typeof` because an absent `ground.js` makes it a `ReferenceError` rather than `undefined` — which is the failure the existing guard was written for and could not previously detect. **The regression test was checked against the defect**: reintroducing the collision failed three of its six cases, removing it passed all six. |
| D-135 | ~~**The one face whose purpose is to draw a map was the only face with no viewer.**~~ FeatureServer service pages offered *View in: Map · ArcGIS SDK*, two real viewers. The MapServer page offered *View in: Export · Legend*: a single 800x600 PNG of the full extent, and a JSON document. Neither is a map — a PNG does not zoom — so the feature face had two viewers and the map face had none. | 2026-08-20, the day the MapServer face shipped | **Acceptable for a day and not longer.** Both viewers were written when FeatureServer was the only face, and each builds its URL as `/FeatureServer/{id}` and its layer as a feature layer. The new face reused the directory's link slot with what was cheap rather than what was a viewer, which is a reasonable thing to ship on the day and a bad thing to leave. | Asked by the owner on 2026-08-21: *why does MapServer not have it*. The answer was that nobody had gone back. | **A face nobody can look at is a face nobody checks.** Correctness gate 2's four best findings all came from asking whether two faces agree about one layer, and it had to do that by hand because there was no way to see both. | **RESOLVED 2026-08-21.** `face=mapserver` on the same two viewers rather than two new pages: the ArcGIS SDK one builds a `MapImageLayer`, the OpenLayers one an `ol.source.ImageArcGISRest`, and on both the picker toggles sublayers instead of replacing a layer — because on a map service the layers are a composition rather than alternatives. Verified in a headless browser against a three-layer service: the toggle sends `layers=show:0,3` and the routes disappear from the drawing. **WMS is the remaining face with no viewer**, and the SDK has a `WMSLayer` for it. |
| D-136 | ~~**The console has no screen for an image service.**~~ **RESOLVED 2026-08-22.** A coverage registered with [ADR-043](adr/ADR-043-imageserver-and-the-raster-face.md) opens in the console and is described there: size, bands, sample kind, reference, pixel size, no-data and the formats it writes, with a link to the ArcGIS SDK viewer. Sharing, status and removal work through the API and the row's own controls. | 2026-08-21, the day image services shipped | **Acceptable for the first cut and named at the moment it became true.** ADR-043 §4 predicted it — *a layer is no longer always a PostGIS table, and this reaches the catalogue, the sharing check, the directory, the console and every capabilities document* — and building the console inside the same increment as the reader, the planner, the warp and the endpoints would have made all five unreviewable together. | Closed. | **What it cost while open, and this is the part worth keeping**: clicking an image service opened the *feature* editor. Feature access, vector tiles, and Query/Create/Update/Delete/Extract — five operations it does not answer, offered as settings. That is correctness gate 2's fifth finding told by a different surface: there the service document claimed `Map,Query,Data` with no query route, here the console claimed it on the service's behalf. **A wrong screen is worse than a missing one**, because a missing screen is visibly missing. | **RESOLVED, and the row was rewritten four times because it was written from guesses and then measured.** *Lists them and cannot open them* — both admin listings were read and `/admin/featureservices` carries them, which is where the console reads from; only `/admin/services` does not, and that endpoint is for system services. *Cannot be shared* — sharing works through the generic service route. **What was actually wrong** was four things, each found by opening the page and looking: the feature editor rendered for a coverage; `IsEmpty` counting layers, so every image service read as empty and was listed under *remove empty services* — a destructive action offered for something the sweep had already been taught to spare; a Limits tab that 404s, because record counts are about rows; and a `/FeatureServer?f=json` probe for a face that does not exist. **And the console's own habit is the reason three of the four existed**: it re-derived from `layers === 0` what the server already decided. The judgement comes from the API now and the count stays local, because a count is a phrase and emptiness is a fact. |
| D-137 | ~~**No ArcGIS Pro user can open imagery from this server, and one untrue word would fix it.**~~ **RESOLVED 2026-08-22, the day it was taken on, by serving the operation rather than claiming it — [ADR-044](adr/ADR-044-tiles-are-served-because-the-claim-had-to-be-true.md).** ArcGIS Pro 3.6's raster reader refuses an image service whose `capabilities` string does not contain `Tilemap`; the grid was measured and it is that word specifically, not merely a second token — see [Q-134](open-questions.md). This face serves `Image` and nothing else, so Pro answers *does not exist or is not supported* and every Pro workflow stops there. **With the word typed in, and nothing else changed, a nine-step probe matches Esri's own Terrain3D service exactly**: opens, describes, reports band count, reference, extent and pixel type, and draws. | 2026-08-22, the day Q-134 was answered | **Acceptable only because the alternative is a lie, and it is not comfortable.** [ADR-043](adr/ADR-043-imageserver-and-the-raster-face.md) condition 5 says a capability is not advertised until it is served. This face has no `tilemap` route, no tiling scheme, and `tileInfo: null` — a client reading the claim would have no scheme in which to name a tile, so the claim is not merely unserved, it is unaddressable. Correctness gate 2's fifth finding refused this exact trade once already, when MapServer claimed `Map,Query,Data` with no query route, and it was repaired by removing the claim. Removing it here costs a whole client. | **Serve the operation rather than claim it.** A tiling scheme in `tileInfo`, `/tilemap/{level}/{row}/{col}/{width}/{height}` answering which tiles of a block the coverage covers, and `/tile/{level}/{row}/{col}` rendering one tile — which is `exportImage` at a fixed size over a computed extent, so the machinery is already here. That makes the word true and gives tiled imagery access, which is a capability rather than a workaround. **It is a scope change to ADR-043 §3.2, which left tiles out of the first cut, so it is the owner's decision and not a thing to slip in.** | **Every ArcGIS Pro user is locked out of imagery entirely, and the failure gives them nothing to go on**: `ERROR 000732: does not exist or is not supported`, which is the same sentence Pro gives for a path that is not there. An administrator seeing it has no reason to suspect a capability string and every reason to suspect the URL, the certificate or their own credentials — three things that are all fine. **The other cost is the temptation itself**: the word is free to type and nothing at runtime would ever catch it, which is why the conformance test was tightened the same day from a substring check to `Assert.Equal("Image", claimed)`. Editing that line by hand is now the moment somebody has to say which route answers the new claim. | Closed. |
| D-138 | ~~**The request log records almost nothing, so the server cannot say what a client asked it for.**~~ **WITHDRAWN 2026-08-22, the day it was written, because it was not true.** The log records everything: 8997 request lines and 4.6 MB of them, written while this row claimed the file was silent. **The row was written from a stale file at the wrong path.** `dev-server.sh` computed its log path relative to the script and then `cd`-ed into the build directory before the redirect, so the server wrote its log there and `tail dev-server.log` beside the script showed a file last touched two days earlier with one request in it. | 2026-08-22, and withdrawn the same day | n/a — there was no debt to accept. | n/a. | **What it actually cost is worth keeping, and it is not zero.** A proxy was built and put in front of the server to capture ArcGIS Pro's requests, which the log already had; the four parser defects [Q-134](open-questions.md) found were real and the trace was a correct instrument, but it was not a necessary one. And the wrong path also pointed `GRATICULA_TEST_LOG` at the stale file, which is why `TokenIsNotLoggedTests.A_token_in_the_query_string_is_redacted_before_it_is_written_down` — [ADR-015](adr/ADR-015-authentication.md) condition 2's only assertion from outside — was failing against an empty file rather than against the server. | **Withdrawn, and it is the same failure this register already warns about two rows from here**: *a row is a lead rather than a finding, read the code the row accuses before starting work aimed at it.* This one accused the server on the strength of one `tail`, and the check that would have caught it in ten seconds — does the file's timestamp match a server that started an hour ago — was not made. |
| D-139 | ~~**`POST` is refused on every ArcGIS rendering and query operation, and the REST specification documents it.**~~ **RESOLVED 2026-08-23, and the row was half wrong in a way worth keeping.** True of `exportImage`, `identify` and the service documents, which answered a bare 405 with an empty body. **Not true of `query`:** that route had always been mapped for both methods and had always read `context.Request.Query` either way — so a posted query answered a different question in silence. Measured: `where=1=1&returnCountOnly=true` in the body returned the full attribute set, because every parameter was absent and every default applied. **That is worse than the 405 it was confused with**, which at least says it did not work. `exportImage`, `identify` and the ImageServer service document all answer a bare HTTP 405 with an empty body to a POST; `MapServer/export` does the same. A client whose request is longer than a URL can carry — a large `bbox`, a rendering rule, a long `where` — has no way to send it. | 2026-08-22, found by an external review of the ImageServer REST surface | **Acceptable only because it is face-wide rather than one face's gap, and fixing it properly is a decision about all of them.** Every operation would need to read its parameters from a form body as well as a query string, the two would need one reader so they cannot disagree, and the empty-bodied 405 would need to become this convention's error envelope.
