Doing that for ImageServer alone would make it the only face that accepts POST, which is a worse inconsistency than the one it fixes. | **One parameter reader that takes both, and every ArcGIS face routed through it.** The shape already exists: `ArcGisResponseFormat` was created on the same day for exactly this reason, because `f` was read carefully by one face and not at all by another. | **A client that cannot fit its request in a URL cannot use this server at all**, and the 405 it gets has no body, so it is told neither that POST is unsupported nor what to do instead. Browsers cap URLs around 2000 characters and some proxies lower; a rendering rule or a long filter reaches that. | Closed. **One seam, not a change per route.** `ArcGisParameters` merges the query string with the form and implements `IQueryCollection`, so the thousand-line query parser was handed a different view rather than taught a second source. It also replaced the case-insensitive lookup that `ImageServerEndpoints` and `MapServerEndpoints` each carried a copy of — and two copies is how the two faces came to agree on the bug: neither read a form, so neither answered a POST. **The cookie is still GET-only**, which is the property this could most easily have broken, and `A_cookie_still_does_not_authenticate_a_post` is what says it did not. Every test asserts the answer rather than the status, because the failure it was written against was a 200. |
| D-140 | ~~**The map viewer has no visible keyboard focus and its error panel is silent to a screen reader.**~~ **RESOLVED 2026-08-22.** `:focus-visible` rules on the map surface and the SDK's widget buttons, declared in each viewer's own stylesheet rather than fought for inside the CDN theme that sets `outline: none` on them. `#note` carries `role="status"` and `aria-live="polite"` — *polite* because it is an explanation rather than an interruption. The map surface and the SDK's zoom buttons carry `outline: none` from the unmodified Calcite theme, so a keyboard user cannot see where they are — while the *Frame layer* button and the attribution link do show focus, which makes the gap look like a rendering fault rather than a missing style. Separately, the `#note` panel that reports a layer failing to load — including the deliberate refusal for a private service — has no `role` or `aria-live`, so it appears silently. | 2026-08-22, found by a Playwright review of the imagery viewer | **Not acceptable, and it is inherited rather than chosen**, which is the only thing in its favour: nothing here was written to suppress a focus ring. WCAG 2.4.7 is a stated obligation for the console and the viewer is the same product. | **Restore the focus ring on the map surface and the zoom widget, and give `#note` `role="status"` with `aria-live="polite"`.** Both are small and neither needs a design decision; they were left because the review that found them also found a view that reset itself on every click, and that was fixed first. | **A keyboard user cannot tell where they are on the page, and a screen-reader user is not told when a layer fails.** The second is worse than it sounds: the private-service refusal is a well-written explanation that a blind operator never hears, so the page reads as simply empty. | Closed. |
| D-141 | ~~**A private image service's own settings page carries no sign that it is private.**~~ **RESOLVED 2026-08-22.** The sharing scope is a pill in the service's head bar, so it is on all four of its pages rather than on one — putting it on the Capabilities page alone would have fixed a quarter of the problem and made the other three look deliberate. **Read from the server's answer rather than from the list the reader may never have seen**, which is the lesson the `kind` lookup on the same screen learned the hard way a few hours earlier. The services list shows sharing as a pill on each row; open one and the Capabilities page it lands on says nothing about it. An operator who arrived from a link or a bookmark — which is exactly the path that was broken for a different reason the same day — has no way to tell a private service from a public one while looking at its settings. **And a coverage's `Pixel size` row was showing bare numbers**: `0.01 × 0.01` for a coverage in degrees beside `100 × 100` for one in metres, four orders of magnitude apart on the ground and rendered as though comparable. | 2026-08-22, found by a Playwright review of the console's image service screen | **The pixel size was not acceptable and is fixed**; the missing sharing indicator is, for now, because sharing is changed from the list and the row there does show it. This row exists so that *you can see it on the other page* does not quietly become the answer. | **Put the sharing state on the service's own header, where every one of its pages can see it**, rather than on the settings page alone. | **An operator can publish imagery believing it is private, or share it believing it already was.** The pixel-size half cost more: `console.js` carries a note two hundred lines above that row recording a real incident in this codebase caused by conflating degrees and metres in an unlabelled value, and the row was written anyway. | Closed. |
| D-142 | ~~**Switching between Server and Studio showed the sign-in screen for about a second.**~~ **RESOLVED 2026-08-22.** Reported by the owner: *"server'dan studio ya geçerken 1 sn liğine password ekranı gelip kapanıyor."* The two surfaces are separate paths by [ADR-034](adr/ADR-034-server-and-studio.md) §5c, so every switch is a cold load; `#signin` had no initial state and the browser painted it as soon as the HTML arrived, while `start()` only corrected it after `console.js` — 454 KB, loaded `defer` — had arrived and compiled. **`/rest/whoami` answers in 6 to 15 ms, so the network was never the cause.** `session.js` now reads `sessionStorage` synchronously from the head and two CSS rules paint the right screen on the first frame. | 2026-08-22, reported and fixed the same day | n/a — this was a defect rather than a compromise. | n/a. | **What it cost is the part worth keeping, and it is two lessons.** **The first fix did not work and looked as though it did.** It put the decision in an inline `<script>`; the console's CSP has no `'unsafe-inline'`, so the browser refused it on every load — and `No_console_page_carries_an_inline_script`, a test this repository has had since [D-44](#), refused it too, with a failure message naming the remedy. **The build was run and that test was not.** On this machine `console.js` loads in about 20 ms, so the flash was invisible whether the script ran or not: the machine was hiding the bug, not the fix. It took delaying `console.js` by 800 ms to see the sign-in screen sit there for the whole delay with a valid token in storage. **The second lesson is that the fix needed a guard and a browser cannot be one.** No assertion in this suite can fail on a machine fast enough to hide the symptom, so the test that exists is structural — it refuses the two ways this regresses, going back inline or gaining `defer` — and it was proved by adding `defer` and watching both pages fail before taking it out again. | Closed. |
| D-143 | ~~**`ListPagingTests.Filtering_returns_to_the_first_page` fails about one run in six, and the cause is not established.**~~ **RESOLVED 2026-08-23. The cause was in the test's own fixture and this row's guess was wrong.** `WithMoreThanOnePageAsync` computed `Math.Max(0, 11 - biggest.Value)` probe services, so **a folder that already held eleven got none** — and then a test whose filter is this suite's own prefix narrowed the list to nothing and reported that filtering had left the table empty *while there were matches*. There were none. The table was right. Measured 2026-08-23: `hosted` held fourteen services, so the floor of zero applied; other suites publish and remove services in the same folder, which is why the count crossed eleven between runs. The floor is one now — eleven is what the pager needs and one is what the filter needs, and only the second was ever in doubt. Five consecutive runs with the folder at fourteen, where it previously failed by construction. | 2026-08-22, seen while closing D-140 and D-141 | n/a — a defect rather than a compromise. | n/a. | **The row named `CatalogFallback` as the thing to measure next and that was wrong.** The listing was accurate throughout; nothing was cached or stale. What the row got right was refusing to write a cause it had not measured — the two hypotheses it did record were checked and eliminated, and eliminating them is what left the fixture as the only place remaining to look. **A wrong lead recorded as a lead costs an hour; a wrong lead recorded as a finding costs a day**, which is [D-138](#). | Closed. |
| D-144 | **Nothing bounds how long one request may hold a database permit.** [ADR-046](adr/ADR-046-admission-control-bounds-the-queue-not-the-wait.md) bounds the queue and says plainly that it does not bound the holder: a single pathological request admitted alone can hold a permit for as long as the statement timeout allows, and every other caller waits behind it. That is the repair [D-128](#) originally named, and ADR-046 chose a different one. | 2026-08-23, created deliberately by ADR-046 | **Acceptable because the alternative cannot be reported.** A request killed after admission has already begun writing, and a truncated GeoJSON document is a worse failure than a refusal — `ErrorResponse` writes a status and a body, and there is no status left once bytes are on the wire. A client sees a parse error and looks at its own parser. | **A way to refuse before the first byte, or a way to end a response that says why.** Either would do. **Neither exists — measured 2026-08-24**: the byte ceiling is enforced by the writer as it writes, which is a mid-stream stop rather than a refusal, and there is no pre-flight cost estimate. The decision is [Q-139](open-questions.md), shared with [D-128](#). The statement timeout already bounds the database's half of a long request, which is where a pathological query spends its time, so what is left is the server's own half — a render, a large encode — and that is bounded by the image ceiling rather than by time. | **One slow request still costs every other caller of that source.** The queue bound makes the cost visible — the waiters count on `/admin/health` will sit high — and does nothing about it. | Open. |
| D-145 | **The load generator cannot prove the queue bound works under sustained load, because it runs on the machine under test.** 480 Python threads doing TLS handshakes beside the server they are testing: sampling the queue counter through a warm flood found it empty in 22 of 24 samples, so the depth is reached in bursts and not held. [ADR-046](adr/ADR-046-admission-control-bounds-the-queue-not-the-wait.md) condition 1 is partly discharged for exactly this reason. | 2026-08-23, while measuring ADR-046 | **Acceptable only as a statement of what is unknown.** What is measured is real: the queue holds at its configured depth and a cold flood sheds 15%. What cannot be measured here is whether a sustained flood sheds, or whether some of the latency growth is queued upstream of admission control where nothing in this server can reach it. **Those two have different repairs and the measurement cannot tell them apart.** | **A load generator that is not this machine.** **Written down as a question 2026-08-24: [Q-140](open-questions.md)**, which names three shapes and notes that [D-30](#)'s concurrency half is blocked on the same input — something neither row said. One host driving another, or a tool that does not pay a GIL and a TLS handshake per request. The probe is nine lines of `urllib` and a thread pool; the thing it lacks is a second machine. | **A bound believed to work is worse than one known not to.** The register now says which half is proven, and the unproven half is the one an operator would rely on during an actual flood. | Open. |
| D-148 | **A request body over the server's limit ends the connection instead of answering 413.** Measured 2026-08-24 while closing [D-31](#): a `GeometryServer/intersect` with two 600,000-vertex operands encodes to about 50 MB, past Kestrel's 30 MB default, and the connection is closed after **23 milliseconds** — the client sees `EOF occurred in violation of protocol`, which is a TLS error rather than a status code. Nothing in the request log records it either, because no request was ever dispatched. | 2026-08-24, measured | **The bound is right and its delivery is not.** 30 MB is a sensible ceiling for a form body and the work behind it is unbounded, so refusing is correct; what is wrong is that a caller cannot tell *too large* from *the server fell over*, and the two want different things done about them. Kestrel answers 413 when the application reads the body and finds it over the limit; a form body is read by the framework before any of our code runs, which is where the answer is lost. | The first client that reports the geometry server dropping connections | **A refusal nobody can read looks like a fault.** The operator's next step after *the connection died* is to look at the server, and there is nothing there to find. | **CLOSED 2026-08-24, the day it was opened, and the repayable form is what was built.** `TryForm` compares `Content-Length` against the ceiling it reads off the request — Kestrel's, so a deployment that changes it changes this — and answers **413** with both bounds named before a byte of the body is read. The service document reports `maximumRequestBytes` beside `maximumVertices`, because a caller sizing against the vertex bound alone builds exactly the request that used to end the connection. Measured: 36.9 MB against a 30 MB ceiling now answers *this request is 36.9 MB and this server reads at most 30 MB in one body*. **413 rather than 400 needed a marker rather than a parameter.** `TryForm` has eight callers and one of its refusals wants a different status; threading it through eight signatures to carry one boolean is how a signature grows a parameter nobody reads, so the refusal marks the context and the one place that writes the answer reads the mark — the same move `ServiceLookup` makes for its blind flag. **And one thing the repair cannot fix, which is HTTP rather than this server.** A client that sends `Expect: 100-continue` — curl does, past a kilobyte — gets the 413 before it sends anything, and reads it. A client that streams the body without asking gets the answer written and the connection reset while it is still writing, so it raises a socket error and never reads the response; measured with both `HttpClient` and Python's `urllib`. The only way to guarantee a streaming client reads the refusal is to accept all thirty-seven megabytes first, which is the cost the ceiling exists to avoid. `OversizedBodyTests` asks the way a large-body client should and asserts the status and both bounds in the message; the comment beside it records why it asks. |
| D-149 | **One register says an assumption is load-bearing and is not, on two lines of the same file.** [architecture-assumptions.md](architecture-assumptions.md) line 35 says A-019 is *`VALIDATED` 2026-08-12 — and demoted the same day: no longer load-bearing. Q-67 removed its premise*. Line 119 lists A-019 in the table headed **Failure impact — the load-bearing five**, with a failure scenario — *Tiles from SQL Server and Oracle are too slow* — that v1 removed the engines for. So the file states and denies the same fact eighty lines apart, and the five are four. | 2026-08-24, found while closing [D-05](#), which is the row both lines were pointing at | Not chosen. Q-67 demoted the assumption and the scope cut removed its scenario; the summary table above it was written before either and neither edit reached it. The same shape as [D-126](#), inside one document rather than across several. | **Now** — a reader choosing what to validate next reads the load-bearing table, which is the whole reason it is at the top | **The list of what this architecture rests on is wrong about its own length**, and it is the list somebody uses to decide where to spend a measurement. Counting a demoted assumption among the load-bearing five makes the pile look larger than it is and hides which four actually carry weight — the same failure [D-46](#) records for fixes and this register records for counts. | **CLOSED 2026-08-24, and it was three instances rather than one — the check found two of them.** **A-019** left the impact table: line 35 of the same file had recorded it *demoted the same day: no longer load-bearing* since Q-67, and the v1 cut then removed the engines its failure scenario was about. **A-003 was the same defect pointed the other way, in a heavier file.** [CLAUDE.md](../CLAUDE.md) §1 called it *the load-bearing assumption under ADR-007* while this register had recorded it **DOWNGRADED to informational 2026-08-15** — [ADR-029](adr/ADR-029-affinity-routing-is-not-the-default.md) took affinity routing out of the default and it holds nothing up. **That sentence outlived the decision by nine days in the document every session reads first**, which is the worst place for it, and it is corrected rather than the table widened. **And then the check named a third nobody had looked at.** `A-014` was still in the table with *blind routing → ADR-007 §4.4 is deleted*, and it was downgraded by the same ADR on the same day as A-003. **So the load-bearing five were three** — and the two that had stopped holding anything up were the two an eye passing over a table would not question, because nothing in the row said so. **The guard is `a_demoted_assumption_still_called_load_bearing`**, and its argument is that the register's own row is the truth, because that is where a status is maintained: no assumption whose row says *demoted*, *downgraded* or *no longer load-bearing* may appear in the impact table, and none may be called load-bearing in CLAUDE.md. Struck text is stripped first — across the whole document, not per line, because this file's history spans lines — and only table rows count inside the impact section, since that section's own prose now names the assumptions it explains the absence of. **Falsified** by putting A-014's row back: it named it. **Two things this round proves about the check rather than about the rows.** It found more than the reading that opened the debt did, on the first run. And the file where a demotion is *written* turned out to be the file where the demotion was not *read*, which is why one fact in one home is not enough on its own — something has to go and look. **Reopened and closed again the same day, because three was not the count either — it was five.** The check was shaped like the instances it was written from rather than like the class: it read the impact table and `CLAUDE.md`, the two places the defect had been found, and two more were standing while it reported clean. [v1-scope.md](v1-scope.md) §6 — the document [CLAUDE.md](../CLAUDE.md) §1 calls **authoritative** for scope — said *A-003 is the load-bearing assumption under ADR-007, and ADR-007 is in v1*. And `architecture-assumptions.md`'s own *Priority* paragraph said it again, **four lines above the table the check did read**. The register that records the demotion was contradicting it in its own prose. **Two defects in the guard, and the second is the one worth keeping.** It read line by line, so the Priority sentence — which wraps between *A-003 is the* and *load-bearing* — was invisible to it; documents are now flowed before they are read. And its *is this a record rather than a claim* test scanned 90 characters either side of the match, which borrows the neighbouring sentence's vocabulary: a planted `A-014 is a load-bearing assumption` at the end of v1-scope was silenced by *no document **said** who would build it* three lines above it. **Measured rather than assumed** — the window was extracted and the suppression reproduced before the comment claiming it was written. The test now reads the sentence the claim is in, which is where the property belongs. **`reviews/` and `research/` are exempt as a class**, along with [architecture-assessment.md](architecture-assessment.md): a review says what was believed on a date and is never rewritten, and `independent-review-3-process` quotes the register's own stale note, which is the thing a review is for. **Falsified after widening** by planting the claim and watching it named, and the first attempt at that falsification reported clean because the grep pattern was wrong, not the check — which is its own small lesson about proving a guard rather than believing it. |
| D-150 | **A lock wait is reported to the caller as a database outage**, and it is the fifth time this file has mistaken a specific fault for a connectivity failure. PostgreSQL raises **55P03** when `lock_timeout` cuts a statement waiting on a lock. `ErrorResponse.Explain` has no arm for it, and `PostgresException` derives from `NpgsqlException`, so it falls through to the general branch — **measured 2026-08-24: `55P03` → 503, *"A database this server depends on is unreachable. Check /healthz/ready and /admin/health"*.** The database is up, connected and answering; a DBA is holding `ACCESS EXCLUSIVE` on one table. An operator reading that goes to check the network. **Reachable in a real deployment**: `LayerConnections.WithStatementTimeout` preserves whatever `Options` an operator set and only appends its own `statement_timeout` when that one is absent, so `-c lock_timeout=2s` in a connection string is supported configuration. | 2026-08-24, found while constructing [D-08](#)'s lock wait | Not chosen, and not visible: nothing sets `lock_timeout`, so the state is unreachable in a default deployment and no test or run has ever produced it. The same shape as the four codes already corrected here — `XX000`, `23505`, `42883`, `42703` — each of which was answered *unreachable* until somebody hit it. | **Before an operator is advised to set `lock_timeout`**, which is the natural response to [D-08](#)'s measurement that a blocked read holds a connection for the full thirty seconds | **The one deployment that takes the advice gets the worst of both**: a bound that works, and a message that sends whoever is on call to the network while a DBA holds a lock they could have released. It also loses the distinction that makes `lock_timeout` worth setting — *this statement is waiting for somebody, not running* is the whole content of 55P03, and it is thrown away at the boundary. | **CLOSED 2026-08-24, the day it was opened, and it is the first of its class caught before anybody met it.** `55P03` has an arm of its own: **503**, and a sentence that sends the reader to whoever is running DDL against that table rather than to the network — *this layer's table is locked by something else … the database is healthy and the query never ran*. **503 rather than 504**, because the statement never ran: *your query was too expensive* is the wrong story when something else holds the table and will let go. **And no `Retry-After`**, deliberately — `RetryAfterFor` answers only where this server knows the recovery time, and nobody here knows how long a DBA holds a lock; a number invented for that header makes every client that believes it retry in lockstep. **The public form was written at the same time**, because [D-03](#)'s rule now has a test that would have caught its absence: an unprivileged caller is told the layer is busy, that the query was not run, and to retry — no table, no database, no lock. `A_lock_wait_is_not_reported_as_an_unreachable_database` asserts the status, that the message says *locked* and not *unreachable*, that it says the query never ran — a caller who thinks a partial answer was possible reads a retry as a duplicate risk — and that no `Retry-After` is offered. The arm is also in `EveryArm`, so the two enumerating checks cover it without anybody remembering to add it. **The before-measurement is the one that opened this row**: the same code path answered 503 *a database this server depends on is unreachable*. |
| D-151 | **[ADR-017](adr/ADR-017-admin-api.md) §3.3 walks a failed registration through the jobs API, and registration is not a job.** The walk's first two steps are `GET /admin/jobs/{id}` — *"Registration is an interactive-class job (ADR-011). Status, phase, and the failing step"* — and `GET /admin/jobs/{id}/log`, *"the provider's actual error, not a wrapped one"*. **Measured 2026-08-24: `JobKind` has two values, `GeodatabaseInspect` and `GeodatabaseImport`**, and `POST /admin/datasources` answers synchronously with the probe's reason. There is no job id to look up. And **`/admin/jobs/{id}/log` is not mapped at all** — the only two job routes are `GET /admin/jobs` and `GET /admin/jobs/{id}`. So an operator following the document's own diagnostic walk asks for an id they were never given, from an endpoint that would not answer it. | 2026-08-24, found while closing [D-09](#), whose stale status cell pointed at this section | Not chosen. Synchronous registration with the reason in the response is **better** than a job to poll, and it is what shipped; the ADR describing the other shape is what did not follow. The same propagation failure [D-126](#) names, in a document whose entire subject is operability. | **Now** — ADR-017 §3 is *the four scenarios, walked*, and a walk nobody can follow is the one kind of operability document that is worse than none | **The document is the instrument**, and this one sends a reader to two endpoints that cannot help: one they have no id for, one that does not exist. Whoever is diagnosing a failed registration at 3 a.m. reads §3.3, finds nothing, and concludes the server lost the error — which it did not, it returned it in the response they had already closed. | **CLOSED 2026-08-24, and the document was the only thing wrong.** §3.3's first two steps are struck and replaced by the walk that exists. **What shipped is better than what the section asked for**: the reason arrives in the response instead of being polled for, and the probe runs *before* anything is written, so a failed registration leaves no row behind — which is the property the section's own *what this forces into existence* paragraph wanted and got. **The three-way distinction it asks for is real**: `ProbeOutcome` is `Usable`, `CannotConnect`, `InsufficientPrivilege`, and the middle one is refused at registration while the third is reported rather than refused, because rights can be granted after a source is registered and refusing would make the ordinary order of work impossible — which the rewritten section now says, because it is a decision a reader would otherwise have to infer from a 201. **The jobs row stays and is honest now**: it points at the two things that *are* jobs, and names `failure` as where the provider's own error is, rather than at a `/log` route that was never mapped. |
| D-152 | **One data source whose credential cannot be opened stops the whole server from starting, and the check that decrypts them exists to decide whether to print a sentence.** `Program.Main` calls `AnythingIsSharedAsync`, whose entire purpose is to decide whether a startup message about sharing is *worth saying*; it calls `PostgresLayerCatalog.ListAsync`, which maps every layer, which calls `SecretProtector.Unprotect` on every registered source. A single `SecretProtectionException` propagates out of `Main`. **Observed 2026-08-24**: a server started against a store whose sources were sealed with a different key never reached the listener — no `/healthz/live`, no refusal document, a stack trace and exit. | 2026-08-24, found while starting a server to measure [D-58](#) | Not chosen, and invisible in every environment that has the right key. The decryption is a side effect of listing layers, and the listing was reached from a cosmetic check — nobody writing either could see the other. | **Before a deployment rotates a key, restores a backup, or registers a source from another install** — all three produce exactly this state, and the first two are ordinary operations | **The failure is total and the cause is partial.** Layers on the other data sources are fine, public layers need no credential at all, and the server would serve every one of them; instead nothing starts. It is also the worst possible shape for [D-14](#)'s neighbour problem: the operator who would fix the credential cannot reach the admin API to do it, because the admin API is inside the server that will not start. The refusal should name the source and start without it, or refuse loudly and still serve what it can. | **CLOSED 2026-08-24, and measured against the store that produced it.** `AnythingSharedAsync` answers the question with **two counts** — how many layers there are, and how many of them have a service shared beyond its owner — and touches `data_source` in neither, so no credential is opened to decide whether one sentence is worth printing. Sharing has been the service's column since migration 11, which is what makes the question answerable without the join that carries the secret. **And the call is no longer able to be fatal at all**: it is wrapped, and a failure that reaches it now means the platform store itself is unreadable, which the health endpoints report and which serving already degrades through. `Log.SharingUnknownAtStartup` says so and the server carries on — *a startup aside is the last thing that should decide whether a server exists*. **Before and after, on the same store with the same wrong key.** Before: no listener, no refusal document, a stack trace out of `Main`. After: the server starts, and `/healthz/live`, `/healthz/ready`, `/rest/info` and `/admin/health` all answer **200**. **What still refuses is one route and it is the honest one**: `/rest/services` answers 503, because building the directory decrypts every credential. **That is [D-154](#)** — the listing should offer what it can read rather than nothing — and it is a much smaller thing than a server that will not start. **One unplanned confirmation.** The 503 body is *"This layer cannot be served at the moment. Retrying will not help; it needs attention from whoever administers this server"* — the anonymous form written for [D-03](#) earlier the same day, reaching a real caller from a real outage, with the operator's version reserved for `admin:manageServer`. D-03 was verified by unit test; this is the first time it has been seen working. |
| D-153 | ~~**`RelationshipEndpoints` reads the catalogue around the fallback in three of its four methods**~~ **WITHDRAWN the day it was opened — all three are admin handlers.**, so a related-records request refuses during a catalogue outage while the feature query beside it serves from memory. Three signatures take `PostgresLayerCatalog` directly; a fourth already reaches through `CatalogFallback.Catalog` and handles it being null, so the file disagrees with itself. **And [D-127](#) says the opposite** — *attachments and relationships inherit it* — which is how this stayed invisible: the register recorded the intention and nothing read the parameter list. | 2026-08-24, found by the check written for [D-127](#) on the day it was written | Not chosen. The fallback was added face by face and relationships are reached *through* a face rather than being one, so they were covered by a sentence rather than by a signature. | **Now, and it is the same trigger [D-127](#) has** — any deployment where the catalogue and the data are separately reachable, which is every deployment with a registered source | **The inconsistency is visible from outside and is the exact shape D-127 records**: same layer, same instant, its features answering 200 with an `X-Catalog-Age` header and its related records refusing 503. **Not a parameter swap**: `CatalogFallback` remembers services and listings, not the per-layer reads a relationship query makes, so what a related-records request should answer from memory is a decision [ADR-026](adr/ADR-026-serving-through-a-platform-store-outage.md) has not taken — and answering *some* related records from a remembered layer set is worse than refusing, because a client cannot tell a short list from a complete one. | **WITHDRAWN 2026-08-24, the day it was opened, and the defect was in the check rather than in the file.** `RelationshipEndpoints` takes the raw catalogue in `DeclareAsync`, `ListAsync` and the `Find` helper they share, and **all three are `/admin/relationships` handlers** — the admin surface, where taking the raw catalogue is not a lapse but the rule: an administrative action during a catalogue outage must fail rather than act on remembered state, which is the reason `AdminEndpoints` is exempt in the first place. The serving handler, `relatedRecords`, already resolves through `CatalogFallback`. **So the file was right and [D-127](#)'s sentence about relationships inheriting the fallback was right, and I was wrong about both** — because the check exempted by **filename**, and `RelationshipEndpoints.cs` is the one file that holds admin routes and a serving route together. A row opened on that reading is a row about my own instrument. **The check is sharper now and the exemption is derived rather than typed**: it reads `app.Map*(…, Handler)` out of each file, examines only methods that are actually registered as routes — a private helper takes whatever its caller has, so `Find` says nothing about a face — and exempts the ones whose route is under `/admin/`. **Falsified by putting a raw catalogue into `WmsEndpoints.GetAsync`**, a genuine serving handler: it named the file and the method. **The lesson is the one this register keeps writing down**, and this time it is about a check written an hour earlier: a guard whose granularity is coarser than the thing it guards will accuse the innocent, and an accusation with a debt number on it is harder to withdraw than one without. |
| D-154 | **The services directory refuses everything when one data source's credential cannot be opened.** `CatalogueAsync` builds `/rest/services` by mapping every layer, and mapping decrypts each layer's connection string, so a single source sealed with a key this process does not hold makes the whole directory answer **503** — measured 2026-08-24 against a store with two sources, one openable. | 2026-08-24, found while closing [D-152](#), by starting a server that could finally start | Not chosen. The decryption is a side effect of building a `PublishedLayer`, and the directory needs the layers rather than their credentials; nobody writing either could see that the listing was paying for something it does not use. | **Whenever a deployment has more than one data source** — which is the ordinary case, since the datastore is registered as one beside every source an operator adds | **A reader cannot tell *nothing is published* from *one credential is stale*.** Every layer on every other source is servable and the directory that would let a client find them is the thing that refuses. It is also the screen an operator opens first when something is wrong, so the one route that should explain the situation is the one the situation removes. The listing needs the layer's identity, not its connection string; a directory that omitted the unreadable ones and said how many it omitted would be strictly better than a 503. | **CLOSED 2026-08-24, measured against the store that produced it.** A layer whose credential cannot be opened is **omitted from the listing rather than taking the listing down**, and the source is named. **Omitted rather than listed, and that is the choice.** Such a layer cannot be served, so advertising it would put something in the directory that answers 503 — a capability report that lies, in miniature. But an omission nobody can see is how a directory comes to disagree with its catalogue, so `UnopenableSources` carries the source names and `/admin/health` reports them. **Named per source, not per layer**, because an operator fixes a credential rather than twenty-nine layers. **Not on the directory document itself, deliberately.** `/rest/services` is Esri's shape and a member of ours on it is exactly the compatibility question [Q-141](open-questions.md) asks about a datum caution — same surface, same answer. Operational detail goes where operational detail goes, and `/admin/health` already distinguishes an administrator from everybody else. **Before and after, same store, same wrong key.** Before: `/rest/services` answered **503** for every caller, with the body *this layer cannot be served at the moment*. After: **200**, listing the services on the source that opens, with folders. The store holds two sources — `datastore` with 22 services and 29 layers, `demo-postgis` with 3 and 3 — and it is the first whose credential this process cannot open, so what was 0 of 25 is now what the second source holds, filtered by sharing as it always was. **Only `SecretProtectionException` is caught**; anything else is a bug and propagates, which is the rule [ADR-026](adr/ADR-026-serving-through-a-platform-store-outage.md) applies to its own blind path. **And the first version of this repair had the defect it was written to prevent — found the same day, by looking at the directory it produced.** Omitting an unreadable layer leaves its service behind with nothing in it, so `/rest/services` answered 200 with two `FeatureServer` documents whose `layers` were `[]` and whose names led nowhere: exactly the *advertise something that cannot be served* the omission existed to avoid, one level up. A service emptied by omission is not listed now. **An empty service that was never filled still is**, because that is a real state and a different one — the distinction is not emptiness but how it arrived. Measured on the same store: `services: []` where it had been two shells, and 356 Postgres integration tests pass. |
| D-155 | **The tile cache key carries no version of the thing that built the tile, so a pipeline change invalidates nothing.** `TileCacheKey` is `(LayerId, Fingerprint, Address)` and `Path()` is `{LayerId}/{Fingerprint}/{z}/{x}/{y}.mvt`. The fingerprint is `FingerprintOf(srid, geometryColumn, attributes, extent, buffer)` — five inputs, all of them properties of the **data**. Nothing in the key describes the **code**: not the simplifier, not the MVT encoder, not the SQL the tile source emits. So an upgrade that changes how a tile is drawn keeps every key it had, and the deployment serves bytes built by the previous release until each entry's TTL expires. There is no version marker in the cache root either — checked. | 2026-08-24, found while answering [Q-45](open-questions.md) | **Because the fingerprint was designed against the opposite fear and got that half right.** [ADR-010](adr/ADR-010-caching.md) §8 worried that keying on plan identity would make every planner change *silently rebuild an entire cache*, and asked for that to be **a deliberate, visible event**. The built key avoids it completely by keying on data shape instead — the layer's *name* is deliberately excluded, because a rename changes no byte and would otherwise throw away a pyramid for a label. That reasoning is right and is not what this debt is about. **What was not noticed is that removing plan identity removed the only thing that would have tracked the code**, and ADR-010 §4's own summary table still says *Key is plan identity + schema fingerprint — **built***, of which one half is built. | **The first release that changes the bytes a tile is made of** and is deployed onto a cache with entries in it. Concretely: a change to `TileSimplify`, to the MVT encoder, to `PostGisTileSource`'s SQL, or to any generalisation the zoom implies. `Extent` and `Buffer` are in the fingerprint, so changing *those* two constants does invalidate — which is what makes the gap easy to miss, since the two most visible knobs are covered. | **A client is served a tile built by code that is no longer running, and nothing anywhere says so.** The failure is quiet by construction: the tile is valid MVT, it draws, and it disagrees with a tile built after its neighbour's TTL expired — so a map can show two generations of the same pipeline side by side, at a seam that moves. This is worse than the event ADR-010 §8 asked to be made visible, because a full rebuild at least announces itself in latency. **Bounded by the TTL**, which is the one thing that stops it being permanent: `CacheLifetime` is per layer, so the exposure is as long as the longest lifetime an administrator has set, and D-25 exists because those are deliberately long for stable data. | **OPEN.** Recorded rather than repaired, because the loop that found it repairs one thing at a time and this was found while reading a question. **The shape of the fix is not obvious and that is the reason to write it down rather than to guess**: a build stamp in the path throws away every tile on every release, including the releases that change nothing about tiling, which is ADR-010 §8's objection arriving from the other side. A stamp that only changes when the pipeline changes has to be maintained by whoever changes the pipeline, and an unmaintained one lies in exactly the situation it exists for — the same argument [Q-101](open-questions.md) makes about a hand-maintained capability vocabulary. **Not measured**: nobody has shipped a pipeline change onto a warm cache, because nobody has shipped anything. |
| D-147 | **A publish naming a data source that does not exist answered 201 Created and wrote nothing.** `PostgresAdminCatalog.PublishLayerAsync` is one statement whose first CTE reads the data source — `select ... from data_source d where d.id = @source` — and every insert after it selects from that read. An id matching no row makes the whole statement a no-op: no folder, no service, no layer. The method then returned the `PublishedLayerAddress` it had computed in memory *before* running it, so the response carried an id, a service name, a layer index and a URL. **Measured: fourteen layers before, fourteen after, and a 201 with `/rest/services/zz_d104_probe3/FeatureServer/0` in the body.** | 2026-08-23, found while repairing [D-104](#) by disabling its check and watching what a publish actually did | **Not acceptable, and it was already known in another form.** The comment on the `slot` CTE three lines above records the same class of defect from earlier: *a service created with no layers and a 201 saying it worked*, which *went unnoticed because every test afterwards published into a service that already existed*. That comment argues for reading the CTE carefully; this one argues for counting the rows it returned. | Closed on discovery | **A silent success is worse than a failure.** A caller that believes a publish worked has a URL that 404s, a name it will not reuse, and no reason to look at the server. | **CLOSED 2026-08-23, the day it was found.** The reader now records whether a row came back at all, and no row raises `UnknownDataSourceException` — an exception rather than a nullable return, because five call sites assume success and a nullable would have been four places to forget a check, which is the shape of the defect being repaired. `POST /admin/layers` turns it into a 404 naming the id and pointing at `GET /admin/datasources`. **Zero rows has exactly one cause**, which is why the message can name it: every other failure in that statement raises — a duplicate name is 23505, a missing service 23503, a bad enum 23514. `SilentPublishTests` asserts the status, the layer count before and after, and that no service was left behind: a 404 that still wrote a row and a 201 that wrote none are the same defect wearing different numbers, and only counting tells them apart. |
| D-146 | **The status page reported the wrong status for four debt rows, and the registers were right the whole time.** `tools/status-page.py` split a table row on every `\|` and took the last cell as the status. A row with one column too many therefore reported its own tail: D-43's published status was the fragment ``Failed)!"`, which discards them...`` and D-75's was a recurrence the row had just closed. Two causes, both mechanical — an unescaped pipe inside prose or inline code, which Markdown treats as content and this tool did not, and a row missing its final pipe. **The same class of defect had put nineteen live assumptions under the heading *Invalidated / superseded* and left ten more out of the register entirely**, because an ADR's assumption table has three columns and the register's has five, so copying one across is an edit rather than a paste. | **Since the first row with a pipe in it**, and the four column-count failures were all present on 2026-08-22; the misfiled assumptions go back to whenever rows were first appended at the end of the file rather than into its table | Not chosen, and not visible either. **A ragged table renders as a table.** The extra column sits off the right edge of a wide row that nobody scrolls, and the generated page looks exactly as generated pages look — which is the whole problem: it is read *instead of* the registers, and it was read for days. | **Now, and repaired in the same hour.** The splitter honours `\|`, three debt rows and six register rows are squared up, nineteen assumptions are back under **Open assumptions**, and ten that existed only inside ADRs are in the register. | **A dashboard that is wrong is worse than no dashboard**, and this one was wrong about whether work was finished — the two directions of that error cost differently and both cost. The assumptions half is worse: [CLAUDE.md §11](../CLAUDE.md) reviews every ADR depending on an assumption when it is invalidated, and it cannot review one the register has never heard of. | **CLOSED 2026-08-23, and closed by two checks rather than by nine repairs.** `registers-check.py` now fails the build when a register row has more or fewer columns than its own table's header — measured from the header, after a first version hard-coded the widths and reported 77 problems that were all its own — and when an ADR declares an assumption the register does not carry. **The second check earned itself immediately:** seven missing assumptions were found by reading the file and three more were still there when the check ran. This is [D-30](#)'s family, and the fourth time an instrument in this repository has been wrong about the thing it measures, which is why the repair is a check and not a correction. |

---

## What belongs here

- A decision taken for schedule reasons that we already believe is wrong.
- A simplification that is correct at the current scale and known to break at a
  larger one.
- A dependency adopted knowing it must be replaced.
- A missing capability that downstream design is quietly assuming exists.
- A criticism from adversarial review that was accepted as valid but deferred
  (§85 requires every material criticism to be resolved *or documented* — this
  register is the "or documented" half).

## What does not belong here

- Deliberate scope decisions. Clustering is deferred by design (§79), not owed.
  It lives in [architecture-completeness.md](architecture-completeness.md).
- Unanswered questions. Those go in [open-questions.md](open-questions.md).
- Unvalidated assumptions. Those go in
  [architecture-assumptions.md](architecture-assumptions.md).

## Review cadence

Reviewed at every phase gate (§65). At each review, for every open entry, ask
whether the trigger has fired — and whether the entry is still honest, or has
quietly become the permanent architecture while nobody was looking.

**And ask a third thing, which was added 2026-08-18 because it kept catching
something: is the entry still true?** A row is written from a symptom, at speed,
and then the code moves and the row does not. Two of the seven open rows checked
against the code that day were describing a state it had left:

- **D-52** named a mechanism — *the startup check never asks whether a build needs a
  newer schema* — that had existed for four days before the row was written, with a
  test. The real cause was a stale constant, which the row could not see because it
  reasoned from the symptom instead of reading the check it accused.
- **D-22** said `where` accepts only `1=1`. The grammar had arrived; `objectid>5`
  filtered correctly and had for some time. One genuine gap was underneath it, and it
  was a different gap from the one the row described.

The other five checked out — **D-24** and **D-33**'s columns really are written and
never read, **D-12** really has no trusted-proxy handling, **D-26**'s composite flag
really is stored and refused, **D-39** really does address two kinds of service at one
path. So the register is not rotten; it is roughly two in seven stale, which is high
enough that **a row is a lead rather than a finding.** Read the code the row accuses
before starting work aimed at it: a day spent on D-52 as written would have added a
second check beside a correct one and left the cause in place.
